# Paper Set Evidence Digest

Number of papers in this paper set: 417

Each item below is a compact paper-level analysis digest. Use only this digest.

- source: Zhang, Z. Y., Elvis, N. T., Wang, J. W., & Hou, G. L. (2024). Can Leading by Example Alone Improve Cooperation?. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that (1) introducing a leader who acts first increases both cooperation and group payoffs compared to a standard PGG; (2) empowering the leader with the ability to punish or reward followers further increases both cooperation and group payoffs, with punishment being more effective than reward in sustaining high cooperation; (3) both leader and follower payoffs are higher in the punishment and reward conditions than in the control or leader-only conditions, though the difference between punishment and reward in final payoffs is not statistically significant; (4) leaders tend to use rewards more frequently than punishments, but punishment is more effective per use. The group average payoff in the punishment and reward conditions is higher than in the control and leader-only conditions, indicating a clear positive effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that, in a 4-player, 32-round linear PGG with a 0.4 MPCR and no communication, enabling leader-administered punishment (with a 1:3 cost/impact ratio) increases group efficiency (average payoff) compared to both a control (no leader, no punishment) and a leader-only (no punishment) condition. The effect is robust and statistically significant. The design matches the prediction task closely, and the reported payoffs allow for direct estimation of efficiency gains from punishment. The evidence is most applicable to settings with similar group size, round count, and punishment technology.

- source: Jiang, X., & Wang, X. H. (2024). Gender difference in leaders framed with responsibility or authority: An experimental study. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a leader with the power to punish increases group contributions and efficiency compared to the no-leader baseline. When the leader's role is framed as responsibility (rather than authority), and especially when the leader is selected by voting, female leaders are more effective at increasing group income and efficiency than male leaders. The responsibility framing increases both leader contributions and the effectiveness of punishment, but the positive effect on efficiency is robustly observed only in the voting stage for female-led groups. In other conditions (authority framing, male leaders, or self-selected leaders), the efficiency gains from punishment are smaller or not significant. The study also shows that leader contributions are a more important driver of efficiency than punishment per se, as punishment can offset its own benefits through costs. Overall, the presence of punishment increases efficiency, but the magnitude depends on leader gender, framing, and selection method.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a public good game increases efficiency, especially when the leader is female, the leadership role is framed as responsibility, and the leader is selected by voting. The game design dimensions most relevant for predicting efficiency gains from punishment are: presence of a leader with punishment power, punishment cost and effectiveness, group size (4), number of rounds (5), and information structure (no chat, summary feedback). The control (no-leader) efficiency is reported, and the treatment (punishment-enabled) efficiency is higher, particularly in the responsibility/voting/female leader condition. For prediction, the paper suggests that punishment is most effective at increasing efficiency when the leader is prosocial (as selected or induced by responsibility framing), and that leader contributions are a key mechanism. The results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in similar lab PGGs with punishment, especially with comparable group size, round number, and punishment parameters.

- source: Chen, J. (2022). Carrots and sticks: new evidence in public goods games with heterogeneous groups. *Journal of Economic Interaction and Coordination*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in public goods games with heterogeneous groups (MPCRs 0.9 and 0.4), allowing punishment does not significantly increase group efficiency or average earnings compared to the control (no incentives) condition. Average per-period earnings in the punishment condition (17.007 tokens) are actually lower than in the control (23.860 tokens), and after adjusting for the resource destruction effect of punishment, final earnings in the punishment condition (23.630 tokens) are still not higher than control. In contrast, reward opportunities lead to much higher efficiency and earnings. The lack of effectiveness of punishment is attributed to antisocial punishment by high-benefit members and lack of strategic punishment by low-benefit members. The results are robust across group and individual analyses, and the paper provides direct evidence that punishment does not improve efficiency in this heterogeneous PGG setting.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in public goods games with heterogeneous MPCRs, enabling punishment does not improve efficiency relative to the control (no-punishment) condition. For prediction tasks, if the game design matches this paper (3 players, MPCRs 0.9/0.4, no chat, continuous contributions, punishment cost 1:3, no rewards in punishment condition, 10 rounds, full feedback), the expected efficiency with punishment enabled should be similar to or lower than the control efficiency. The paper also shows that reward is much more effective than punishment in this context. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in heterogeneous PGGs.

- source: JARUNGRATTANAPONG, R. (2022). PROMOTING COOPERATION IN A PUBLIC GOOD EXPERIMENT THROUGH OSTRACISM AS A PUNISHMENT MECHANISM. *Singapore Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing group members to ostracize (temporarily exclude) others via majority vote in a repeated public goods game increases average contributions by about 45 percentage points and increases average earnings (welfare) by about 23% compared to the control (no punishment) game. The effect is strongest for free-riders but is present for all contributor types. Ostracism is implemented with a small direct cost (1% of endowment per vote), but the main cost is the reduction in group size and thus potential public good returns. Anti-social punishment is observed: non-cooperators who are punished tend to retaliate by voting against others, but this is not seen among high contributors. The positive effect on efficiency is robust, but cooperation and efficiency decline when ostracism is removed (end-game effect).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling ostracism (a form of social punishment) in a repeated public goods game with 4 players, 6 rounds, no communication, and MPCR=0.625 increases both contribution rates and average group earnings (efficiency) compared to a control game without punishment. The effect is large and robust across contributor types. The design details (player count, rounds, punishment cost, etc.) are clearly specified, making the findings directly relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in similar lab PGGs. The main caveat is the presence of anti-social punishment and the collapse of cooperation when punishment is removed, suggesting that the positive effect is contingent on the continued availability of the punishment mechanism.

- source: Ye, H., Zhang, Y. Y., & Lee, C. R. (2023). Dual Effects of Cognitive Ability in Public Goods Dilemmas. *Journal of Neuroscience Psychology and Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This paper experimentally studies the effects of punishment (none, small, large) on efficiency (collective payoff) in both one-shot and repeated public goods games with groups of four. It finds that: (1) In one-shot PGGs, both small and large punishment increase collective payoff compared to no punishment, with large punishment having a stronger effect. Cognitive ability does not significantly affect efficiency except under large punishment, where high cognitive ability groups achieve higher payoffs. (2) In repeated PGGs, the effect of punishment is more complex: small punishment can reduce efficiency for high cognitive ability groups (who contribute less than in the no-punishment baseline), while large punishment increases efficiency for both high and low cognitive ability groups. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is not always positive and depends on both the magnitude of punishment and group cognitive composition.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs depends on the punishment magnitude and the cognitive ability of participants. Small punishment may not always increase efficiency and can even reduce it in repeated games with high cognitive ability groups. Large punishment reliably increases efficiency. The game design dimensions most relevant here are player_count (4), num_rounds (1 for one-shot, 10 for repeated), mpcr (0.5), punishment_cost (institutional, fixed, not paid by peers), and punishment_tech (penalty for under-contribution, with two levels: small and large). There is no chat, no reward, and players receive feedback about others' investments. The findings suggest that, for similar lab PGGs with institutional punishment, enabling large punishment will increase efficiency, but enabling only small punishment may not, especially in repeated settings with high cognitive ability participants.

- source: Peng, H. C., & Fan, Y. H. (2023). Incomplete punishment networks, heterogeneity, and cooperation in public good experiments. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the structure of the punishment network strongly moderates the effect of peer punishment on both cooperation and efficiency in public goods games with heterogeneous MPCRs. Incomplete punishment networks (circle and homo-pairwise) lead to higher group payoffs (efficiency) and higher contributions than the complete punishment network. The complete network, despite more potential punishers, results in lower efficiency, lower contributions, and lower punishment severity, likely due to diffusion of responsibility (bystander effect). The effect of punishment is thus not monotonic in network density: more potential punishers do not always increase efficiency. The type of MPCR (high or low) also affects individual contributions, with high-MPCR individuals contributing more. The punishment mechanism is costly and binary (1 token cost, 3 token impact), and the experiment uses stranger matching over 15 rounds. No communication or chat is allowed. The results suggest that the design of the punishment network is crucial for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public goods games, especially with heterogeneous returns.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that the structure of the punishment network (i.e., who can punish whom) is a critical moderator of the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games with heterogeneous MPCRs. When predicting the efficiency impact of enabling punishment, it is not sufficient to know only the presence of punishment; the network structure (complete vs. incomplete, and the type of incomplete network) must be considered. In this design, incomplete networks (circle and homo-pairwise) outperform the complete network in both contributions and efficiency. The findings also show that punishment severity is lower in the complete network, likely due to a bystander effect. The experiment uses a four-player, 15-round, linear PGG with binary punishment, no chat, and random reassignment of MPCRs and groupings each round. These details should be matched when using this evidence for prediction. The results are most relevant for settings with heterogeneous returns and similar punishment mechanics.

- source: Kamei, K. (2024). Self-regulatory resources and institutional formation: An experiment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a repeated linear public goods game with five players and MPCR 0.4, the introduction of punishment mechanisms (either formal, with a fixed administrative cost and group-voted sanction rate, or informal, with peer-to-peer costly punishment) leads to large and significant increases in group efficiency and payoffs. Without punishment, free riding is prevalent and efficiency is low. With punishment enabled, average group payoffs rise significantly, and a much higher proportion of groups achieve high payoff thresholds. The effect is present regardless of whether participants’ self-regulatory resources are depleted. Formal punishment, when implemented with a deterrent sanction rate, achieves almost full efficiency. Informal punishment also increases efficiency, though not as much as formal deterrent punishment. The act of voting on institutions or sanction rates does not itself account for the efficiency gains; the presence of the punishment mechanism is the key driver.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment in a standard repeated public goods game. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, no chat, continuous contributions, no reward, etc.) are all clearly specified and match the canonical PGG setup. The control (no-punishment) condition yields low efficiency, while enabling punishment (either formal or informal) produces large, robust increases in efficiency and group payoff. The results are robust to manipulations of self-regulatory resources and to whether the punishment institution is chosen endogenously or imposed exogenously. For prediction tasks, the findings support a strong positive effect of punishment on efficiency in similar game designs, with formal deterrent punishment achieving near-full efficiency and informal punishment producing substantial, though somewhat lower, efficiency gains.

- source: Joseph, G., Miquel-Florensa, J., Pahuja, S., Hoo, Y. R., & Tebekew, T. (2025). Motivating Public Sector Employees: Public Good Contributions in Addis Ababa Water and Sewerage Authority. *Economic Development and Cultural Change*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compared three public good game variants: (1) standard, (2) threshold (group must reach a minimum contribution or all is lost), and (3) monitor (one randomly chosen, anonymous group member can punish others at a cost). Both the threshold and monitor games increased individual contributions compared to the standard game, with the threshold game increasing contributions the most. However, because about one-quarter of groups failed to meet the threshold and lost all contributions, the monitor game produced the highest average group payoff (efficiency). The punishment mechanism was effective: monitors punished in about 73% of groups, and punishment was targeted at low contributors. The effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to order effects and individual characteristics. The study provides direct evidence that introducing costly punishment in a one-shot, anonymous, five-player public good game increases group efficiency relative to the control.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in a five-player, one-shot public good game with continuous contributions, no communication, and a known MPCR of 0.4. The monitor (punishment) treatment increased group efficiency/payoff compared to the control. The punishment mechanism involved a cost of 1 token to the punisher and a loss of 3 tokens to the punished. The design closely matches standard PGGs, and the efficiency outcome is directly reported. The findings support the prediction that, in similar settings, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control. The paper also provides comparative evidence on a threshold (common goal) mechanism, which increased contributions but did not increase group efficiency due to the risk of losing all contributions if the threshold was not met.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., Gross, J., de Kwaadsteniet, E. W., van Dijk, E., & de Dreu, C. K. W. (2023). Discriminatory punishment undermines the enforcement of group cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In repeated public goods games with and without punishment, punishment opportunities increased group contributions and group wealth in uniform groups (where all members shared the same academic background). In pluriform groups (mixed academic backgrounds), punishment was applied more to dissimilar others regardless of their contribution, leading to discriminatory punishment. As a result, punishment failed to increase cooperation or group wealth in pluriform groups. The paper provides direct evidence that the effectiveness of punishment in improving efficiency depends on group structure: it is effective in uniform groups but not in pluriform groups, where it can even undermine group outcomes due to discrimination.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is moderated by group structure. When groups are uniform, punishment increases efficiency (group wealth, public good provision). When groups are pluriform (members differ in salient characteristics), punishment is less effective or even counterproductive for efficiency, due to discriminatory punishment. For prediction tasks, the presence of group heterogeneity (pluriformity) should be considered a key moderator: control efficiency is not a sufficient predictor of treatment efficiency unless group structure is accounted for. The game design dimensions most relevant here are group composition (uniform vs pluriform), punishment cost/tech, and information about group member identity.

- source: Sparks, A., Burleigh, T., & Barclay, P. (2024). Expressed disapproval does not sustain long-term cooperation as effectively as costly punishment. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a 40-round, 4-player public goods game, the presence of costly punishment (where players could pay to reduce others' earnings) led to a sustained increase in contributions and, over time, group earnings. Costless public disapproval (where players could assign disapproval points with no material consequence) produced only a temporary increase in contributions, which then declined to control levels. Group earnings in the costly punishment condition started lower (due to punishment costs) but increased over time, eventually matching or slightly exceeding the control and disapproval conditions by the end of the game. The study provides strong evidence that only punishment with tangible consequences can maintain long-term cooperation and group efficiency; costless disapproval is not sufficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence that enabling costly punishment in a standard repeated public goods game increases long-run group efficiency (as measured by group earnings) compared to a control game without punishment. The effect is robust over 40 rounds, with punishment leading to higher contributions and, eventually, higher or equal group earnings despite the cost of punishment. Costless disapproval (without material consequences) does not sustain efficiency. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the expectation that, holding other design dimensions constant, enabling costly punishment will increase efficiency relative to control, especially in longer games. The specific game parameters (4 players, 40 rounds, MPCR 0.4, no chat, continuous contributions, punishment cost and tech specified) make this study highly informative for similar designs.

- source: Krügel, J. P., & Maaser, N. (2025). How do higher-order punishment institutions shape cooperation and norm-enforcement?. *Review of International Organizations*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares three conditions in a repeated linear public goods game with third-party punishment: Baseline (third party can punish), Fourth Party (third party can be sanctioned by an external observer), and Competition (two third-party candidates compete for selection by proposing punishment plans). Both Fourth Party and Competition increase average contributions and group earnings relative to Baseline. Fourth Party leads to the highest contributions and most severe/widespread punishment, but Competition achieves the highest average earnings for public good players, as punishment is more targeted and less frequent. Regression analyses confirm that both higher-order institutions significantly increase earnings and contributions, with Competition outperforming Fourth Party in terms of player payoffs. The results are robust across all periods and when focusing on later periods only.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of enabling punishment (and higher-order punishment institutions) on efficiency in public goods games. The experimental design is a standard repeated linear PGG with three players, 20 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and full feedback. The Baseline (punishment-only) condition provides a control for efficiency, and the two treatments show how additional institutional features (Fourth Party monitoring, Competition) further increase efficiency. The findings support the prediction that enabling punishment increases efficiency, and that the design of the punishment institution (especially targeted, competitive punishment) can further enhance efficiency by reducing unnecessary punishment. The paper's detailed reporting of payoffs, contributions, and punishment allows for quantitative calibration of efficiency effects under different institutional designs.

- source: Botelho, A., Harrison, G. W., Pinto, L. M. C., Ross, D., & Rutström, E. E. (2022). Endogenous choice of institutional punishment mechanisms to promote social cooperation. *Public Choice*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study replicates and extends classic public goods experiments by allowing subjects to experience both a punishment-enabled and a no-punishment institution, then vote on which to use for a high-stakes final round. Across 13 sessions, punishment increased contributions but almost always reduced average profits due to the direct costs of punishing. Only one session (out of 13) saw a majority vote for the punishment institution, and this was in a small cohort with high returns and high initial cooperation. Statistical analysis shows that subjects' votes are overwhelmingly determined by which institution gave them higher profits, not by which supported higher cooperation. The negative effect of punishment on efficiency is robust to variations in matching protocol, order of experience, and marginal per-capita return. The paper also reviews prior studies and finds similar patterns: punishment often fails to improve, and sometimes reduces, group efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in standard public goods games with costly voluntary punishment, enabling punishment typically reduces efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline, even though it increases contributions. The effect is robust across a range of game parameters (player count, MPCR, matching protocol, order, and returns). Exceptions are rare and occur mainly when punishment is used very sparingly and cooperation is already high. For prediction tasks, the presence of costly punishment should generally be expected to lower efficiency relative to control, unless the design or context strongly favors efficient, low-use punishment. The paper also highlights that the marginal per-capita return (MPCR) and matching protocol (reputation effects) can moderate the effect, but the main driver is the costliness and frequency of punishment.

- source: Kim, H., Iris, D., Lee, J., & Tavoni, A. (2025). Representation, Peer Pressure and Punishment in a Public Goods Game. *Environmental & Resource Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment opportunities increase contributions in all treatments, but the efficiency gains depend on the decision structure. In the control (NoR, self-representation), punishment increases contributions to 75% of the endowment and does not reduce group payoffs, so efficiency is maintained or improved. In representation treatments (RnoM and RM), punishment increases contributions but also increases antisocial punishment, leading to lower group payoffs and reduced efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline. Communication (RM) between team members and representatives slightly improves outcomes compared to RnoM but does not fully restore efficiency. Thus, the effect of punishment on efficiency is positive in self-representation but negative or neutral under representation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is moderated by the decision structure: punishment is most effective (and efficient) when individuals decide for themselves, but can reduce efficiency when representatives decide for groups, especially if communication is limited. For prediction, if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling punishment will likely increase efficiency in self-representation settings, but may decrease or only modestly increase efficiency in representation settings, particularly when antisocial punishment is prevalent. The marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are all specified, allowing for direct mapping to prediction models. Communication channels (chat/messages) can moderate but not fully reverse negative efficiency effects under representation.

- source: Cobo-Reyes, R., Katz, G., Markussen, T., & Meraglia, S. (2022). Voting on sanctioning institutions in open and closed communities: experimental evidence. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares public goods games with endogenous institutional choice (formal vs. informal sanctions) in closed (fixed group) and open (migration allowed) societies. Formal sanctions always yield higher average payoffs and contributions than informal sanctions, but the gap is much larger in open societies. In closed societies, informal sanctions can sustain high cooperation and efficiency if initial group cooperation is high, but this is undermined when migration is allowed. The popularity and effectiveness of informal sanctions are fragile to group openness. The main efficiency outcome is average individual payoff per period, which is significantly higher under formal sanctions than informal sanctions in open societies (MT: FS=65.59, IS=60.63), but not significantly different in closed societies (NMT: FS=68.29, IS=67.59).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency gain from enabling punishment (specifically, formal/centralized punishment) is larger in open societies (where group membership is endogenous) than in closed societies. When predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, the presence of migration or endogenous group formation is a key moderator: punishment is more effective at raising efficiency when groups are open. The marginal per-capita return (MPCR), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are all specified, allowing for precise mapping to prediction dimensions. Control (no-punishment) efficiency is not directly reported, but the comparison between informal and formal sanctions provides a strong basis for predicting efficiency changes when punishment is enabled. The results are most informative for settings with 5 players, 30 rounds, no chat, continuous contributions, and the specific punishment/reward technologies described.

- source: Sun, X. P., Liu, X. Z., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of reputation-driven locally variable synergy factors on public goods games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing manipulators—who both punish defectors and reward cooperators—into a spatial public goods game can sustain cooperation and increase average group payoff, even when the synergy factor (r) is low and cooperation would otherwise fail. The model shows that for a range of parameter values (punishment/reward efficiency, manipulation cost), manipulators and cooperators can coexist, and the group achieves higher payoffs than in the absence of punishment/reward. When punishment is sufficiently efficient (beta/gamma high), the system can reach the all-cooperator state, maximizing group payoff. If the cost of manipulation is too high or punishment/reward is inefficient, cooperation collapses and payoffs drop. Reward withdrawal (manipulators reducing rewards when surrounded by cooperators) reduces the average payoff of cooperators and narrows the payoff gap between manipulators and cooperators. The model demonstrates that the combined effect of punishment and reward is robust but sensitive to parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-signal evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a spatial public goods game increases group efficiency and average payoff, especially when the control (no punishment/reward) condition would result in low cooperation and low payoff. The effect is moderated by the efficiency of punishment (punishment impact per cost), the cost to punishers/rewarders, and the synergy factor (mpcr equivalent). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, enabling punishment (especially when combined with reward) will generally increase efficiency, provided the punishment is not too costly and is sufficiently effective. The model also highlights that the presence of defectors and the structure of the population (spatial lattice) are important, but the main prediction is robust across a range of parameter values. This paper should be weighted heavily when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in similar spatial PGG designs.

- source: Bühren, C., Haarde, J., Hirschmann, C., & Kesten-Kühne, J. (2023). Social preferences in the public goods game-An Agent-Based simulation with EconSim. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The agent-based model, using reinforcement learning and a social preference framework, closely replicates the experimental findings of [20] in a public goods game with endogenous punishment. For low cooperators, severe punishment increases contributions and can raise payoffs toward the social optimum, but mild punishment is ineffective and reduces payoffs due to its cost. For high cooperators, punishment (both mild and severe) increases contributions only slightly but reduces payoffs because the cost of punishment outweighs the marginal gain in cooperation. Middle cooperators benefit from mild punishment, which increases both contributions and payoffs. The model demonstrates that the effect of punishment on efficiency is highly dependent on the underlying social preference structure of the group. Endogenous institution choice in the simulation leads agents to select the institution that maximizes their own payoffs, mirroring the learning observed in the experimental Sorted-Info treatment but not in the Sorted treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games with known design parameters. The simulation results, validated against experimental data, show that the impact of punishment on efficiency depends on the group’s baseline cooperativeness: severe punishment can increase efficiency for low cooperators but is costly and can reduce efficiency for high cooperators. The model’s detailed reporting of payoffs under different punishment regimes, player types, and institution selection mechanisms makes it highly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. The findings highlight the importance of considering both the cost and effectiveness of punishment, as well as the social preference composition of the group.

- source: Huang, S. T., Dong, Y. L., & Zhang, B. Y. (2024). THE OPTIMAL INCENTIVE IN PROMOTING COOPERATION: PUNISH THE WORST AND DO NOT ONLY REWARD THE BEST. *Singapore Economic Review*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper provides a theoretical analysis of public goods games with institutional incentives, showing that: (1) Punishing only the lowest contributor (relative punishment with high selectivity) is the most effective way to sustain full cooperation and maximize group welfare for a given punishment budget. (2) Rewarding only the highest contributor is not effective; to promote cooperation, the reward must be distributed such that even lower contributors have a chance to receive it. (3) When both reward and punishment are available, group welfare is maximized by using as much reward as possible and only the minimal punishment needed to sustain full cooperation. (4) The paper gives explicit formulas for the minimum incentive size (punishment and/or reward) required to achieve full cooperation, and shows that, above this threshold, the cooperative Nash equilibrium is globally stable and group efficiency is maximized.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It provides explicit theoretical conditions for when punishment (and/or reward) will lead to full cooperation and maximal group welfare, as a function of the number of players, the marginal per-capita return, and the size and targeting of incentives. The results suggest that, for a given game design, introducing punishment targeted at the lowest contributor will sharply increase efficiency if the punishment is just above the critical threshold. The paper also clarifies that the efficiency gain from punishment is maximized when the punishment is minimal but sufficient, and that reward should be used as much as possible if available. These findings can be directly mapped to the prediction task, especially for settings where the control game (no punishment) has low efficiency and the treatment introduces targeted punishment.

- source: Yang, Z. H., & Yang, Y. L. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics of tax-based strong altruistic reward and punishment in a public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, both strong altruistic punishment and reward are unable to sustain cooperation when implemented individually and funded only by voluntary contributions, due to their high cost. However, when a tax is imposed on all participants and the proceeds are used to fund either punishment or reward institutions, cooperation and group efficiency are greatly enhanced. The model shows that with sufficient tax compensation, strong altruistic punishers or rewarders can coexist with cooperators, eliminating defectors and increasing group welfare. Furthermore, tax-based strong altruistic reward leads to higher levels of cooperation and efficiency than tax-based strong altruistic punishment under otherwise identical conditions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that institutional (tax-based) punishment and reward mechanisms can substantially increase group efficiency in public goods games, compared to traditional voluntary punishment/reward or no-sanction conditions. The results suggest that, for a given set of game design parameters (e.g., player count, MPCR, cost/effectiveness of punishment/reward), introducing a tax-funded punishment or reward institution will increase the average efficiency of the group, with reward institutions being even more effective than punishment. The findings are most directly applicable to settings where punishment/reward is implemented institutionally and funded by mandatory contributions (taxes), and less so to peer-to-peer or voluntary sanctioning. The results are based on evolutionary game theory and numerical simulations, not empirical experiments.

- source: Duell, D., Mengel, F., Mohlin, E., & Weidenholzer, S. (2024). Cooperation through collective punishment and participation. *Political Science Research and Methods*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a repeated public goods game with imperfect monitoring (noisy signals about individual contributions, but perfect information about total group contribution), the authors experimentally compare five regimes: no punishment, peer-to-peer punishment, collective punishment, peer-to-peer punishment with commitment, and collective punishment with commitment. The key finding is that only collective punishment with commitment (where group members can opt in to a regime that automatically punishes the whole group if total contributions fall short) leads to significantly higher group payoffs and cooperation rates compared to both no punishment and peer-to-peer punishment. Peer-to-peer punishment does not improve payoffs or cooperation relative to no punishment in this noisy environment. The positive effect of collective punishment with commitment is especially pronounced in the later rounds, suggesting a learning or coordination effect. The study also shows that the ability to participate in the decision to implement collective punishment (i.e., voting/commitment) is crucial for its effectiveness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong, directly relevant evidence for predicting the effect of enabling collective punishment with commitment in a repeated public goods game with imperfect monitoring. If the control (no-punishment) efficiency is known, enabling collective punishment with commitment is likely to increase efficiency (group payoff) substantially, especially over time, provided the punishment is collective, pre-committed, and participation in the institution is possible. The effect is not observed for peer-to-peer punishment or for collective punishment without commitment. The experimental design and reporting allow for direct mapping to prediction tasks involving the effect of punishment on efficiency, given the game design dimensions.

- source: Ohdaira, T. (2025). The second-order probabilistic pool punishment proportional to the payoff difference can solve the punishment problem of previous studies. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that the second-order probabilistic pool punishment proportional to payoff difference leads to higher average payoff than both standard pool punishment (which suffers from fixed costs) and peer punishment (which suffers from high per-punishment costs) in spatial public goods games. This mechanism is less effective at fully eliminating second-order free riders, but allows for coexistence of punishers and cooperators with high group efficiency. The results are robust across different network structures (regular and randomized ring lattices).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that a probabilistic, payoff-difference-based pool punishment mechanism can increase group efficiency relative to both standard pool and peer punishment, under the specified game parameters (n=5, r=3, c=1, punishment cost G=0.3, punishment impact B=1, peer punishment cost gamma=0.3, peer punishment impact beta=1). The findings suggest that, for spatial PGGs with these parameters, enabling this form of punishment will increase efficiency compared to no punishment or to standard punishment mechanisms. The results are most applicable to settings with continuous contribution, no communication, and fixed group size and structure. The study is simulation-based, so real-world generalizability may be limited, but the mechanism-level insight is strong for theoretical prediction.

- source: Cui, P. B., Wu, Z. X., & Zhou, T. (2022). Small world can alleviate the social dilemmas originating from self-regulation or community policing issues. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the introduction of prosocial punishment in public goods games, especially when implemented on small-world networks, can substantially increase group efficiency (average payoff) by suppressing defectors and supporting the dominance of cooperative strategies. The effect is strongest when the network structure balances local clustering (which supports network reciprocity) and shortcuts (which allow punishers to reach defectors). Both the strength of punishment (fine) and the sensitivity of punishment implementation are important moderators. Regular lattices support cooperation but mainly favor cooperator-driven punishers, while random networks suppress punishment and cooperation. Small-world networks enable both high cooperation and high efficiency, with an optimal range of network randomness maximizing the effect. The model provides direct predictions for how efficiency changes as a function of punishment parameters and network structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that enabling punishment in public goods games increases efficiency, especially when the game is played on small-world networks with appropriate parameter settings (moderate randomness, sufficient punishment fine, and intermediate feedback sensitivity). The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency (without punishment), introducing punishment will lead to a substantial increase in efficiency if the network structure allows for both local clustering and shortcuts. The results also indicate that the effect of punishment is moderated by the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and the network topology. The paper's detailed mapping of efficiency and strategy frequencies across parameter spaces can be used to inform quantitative predictions of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions.

- source: Zefferman, M. R. (2023). Constraints on cooperation shape hierarchical versus distributed structure in human groups. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the efficiency of punishment and monitoring technologies fundamentally shapes the optimal structure of cooperation in public goods games. When punishment is efficient (i.e., the cost to punishers is low relative to the impact on free-riders), hierarchical (tree-like) organizations are optimal and can support universal cooperation (full efficiency) even in large groups. When punishment is inefficient (costly for punishers), distributed (collective) monitoring and punishment are optimal, but these structures are limited in scale by monitoring costs. The model provides explicit analytic conditions for when universal contribution (full cooperation) is a Nash equilibrium and socially efficient, and when universal free-riding is the only equilibrium. The model also shows that the structure of monitoring and punishment networks (hierarchical vs. distributed) is an endogenous outcome of the costs and effectiveness of these institutions. Thus, the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency depends critically on the cost-effectiveness of punishment and monitoring, as well as group size.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games as a function of game design dimensions. It shows that enabling punishment can increase group efficiency (by supporting universal cooperation) if punishment is sufficiently efficient (low cost, high impact), and if monitoring is not prohibitively costly. The model gives explicit formulas for the thresholds at which universal cooperation is efficient and stable, depending on group size, public goods multiplier, monitoring efficiency, and punishment efficiency. It also shows that the structure of monitoring and punishment (hierarchical vs. distributed) is determined by these parameters, which in turn affects the scalability of cooperation. For prediction, the model implies that the effect of punishment on efficiency will be more positive when punishment is cheap and effective, monitoring is efficient, and group size is not too large for the given monitoring technology. If punishment is costly or monitoring is inefficient, enabling punishment may not improve efficiency, especially in larger groups.

- source: Gao, S. P., & Li, N. (2023). Preference reversal and the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that consensual peer punishment in public goods games can increase cooperation and group efficiency, but only if the level of cooperators' preference for punishment is above a threshold and the consensus required to implement punishment is not too high. When preference reversal is introduced (i.e., cooperators may change their mind about punishing), the effect on efficiency and cooperation depends on the structure of reversal probabilities and thresholds. If those who initially do not want to punish are likely to reverse and join in punishment, cooperation and efficiency are promoted. If those who initially want to punish are likely to reverse and abstain, the effect is negative. When reversal probabilities are uniform, increasing reversal undermines the positive effect of punishment, and high reversal can eliminate cooperation and reduce efficiency. Thus, the efficiency effect of punishment is highly sensitive to the details of collective decision-making and preference instability.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence that the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games depends on the interplay between group size, consensus thresholds, punishment cost and effectiveness, and especially the stability of individuals' preferences for punishment. For prediction tasks, it suggests that enabling punishment will not always increase efficiency: the effect is positive only when the group is likely to reach consensus to punish and when cooperators' willingness to punish is stable or when preference reversal tends to increase the number of punishers. If preference reversal is common and leads to fewer punishers, punishment may not improve and can even reduce efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions (in terms of group size, punishment parameters, and preference dynamics) for when punishment will or will not increase efficiency, which can be mapped to the prediction dimensions.

- source: Ishikawa, B. Y. S., & Fontanari, J. F. (2025). Revisiting institutional punishment in the N-person prisoner's dilemma. *Theory in Biosciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that institutional punishment in public goods games can stabilize high-efficiency (all-punishers) equilibria, but only if the cost to punishers is sufficiently low relative to the fines imposed on non-punishers and defectors. Cost sharing among punishers (i.e., dividing the maintenance cost of the institution) expands the parameter region where high efficiency is possible and increases the basin of attraction for the all-punishers equilibrium. However, the all-defectors equilibrium (zero efficiency) is always stable, so initial conditions and parameter settings are crucial. The group size N and the minimum number of punishers required to establish the institution (K_m) both affect the threshold frequency of punishers needed for high efficiency. In finite populations, demographic noise can sometimes allow cooperators to fixate, but this does not generally lead to robust high-efficiency outcomes. The model does not include optional participation, peer punishment, or reward mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct theoretical evidence for how institutional punishment, cost sharing, group size, and punishment cost parameters affect the efficiency of public goods games. For the prediction task, it shows that enabling institutional punishment can increase efficiency dramatically, but only if the punishment cost is not too high relative to the fines and the group can reach a critical mass of punishers. Cost sharing (lower per-punisher cost as more punishers join) makes high efficiency more likely. Larger groups require a higher absolute number of punishers, but the threshold frequency can vary non-monotonically with group size. If the control game (no punishment) is at low efficiency (all-defectors), enabling punishment can, under the right conditions, shift the system to high efficiency, but only if the cost/benefit ratios and initial conditions are favorable. The paper does not address peer punishment, rewards, or communication, so its findings are most applicable to institutional punishment in large, well-mixed groups.

- source: Botta, R., Blanco, G., & Schaerer, C. E. (2024). Discipline and punishment in panoptical public goods games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in a compulsory, fully monitored PGG, it is possible to achieve full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) by punishing only a fraction of defectors, provided the punishment is sufficiently severe. The model formalizes the trade-off between the fraction of defectors punished (scope) and the severity of punishment (magnitude), showing that these can be tuned to reach stable full cooperation. The results indicate that the threat of punishment, even if not universally applied, can discipline the population and sustain cooperation, thus maximizing group payoff. The findings are entirely theoretical, based on replicator dynamics and equilibrium analysis.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the idea that, in a compulsory and fully observable PGG, enabling punishment (even if not universal) can substantially increase efficiency compared to the no-punishment baseline. The model gives explicit conditions (in terms of player count, MPCR, punishment severity, and punishment scope) under which full cooperation is stable. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that introducing a sufficiently strong and well-targeted punishment mechanism will likely move efficiency close to 1, even if the control (no-punishment) efficiency is low. However, the model assumes perfect monitoring, compulsory participation, and centralized punishment, so predictions should be adjusted if these assumptions do not hold in the target environment.

- source: Prétôt, L., Taylor, Q., & Mcauliffe, K. (2024). Children cooperate more with in-group members than with out-group members in an iterated face-to-face Prisoner's Dilemma Game. *Journal of Experimental Child Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in linear public goods games (CPR settings), cooperation and high efficiency can be sustained if and only if the group collectively invests a sufficient fraction of its resources into monitoring and sanctioning rule violations. When monitoring is funded unilaterally (peer punishment), self-interested agents will not invest enough, leading to a second-order free-rider problem and low efficiency. When monitoring is funded collectively from the common-pool resource (institutional punishment), there exists a threshold investment (beta) above which full cooperation and high efficiency are stable equilibria. The paper provides explicit formulas for the minimum investment required and shows that the effectiveness of monitoring (punishment technology), the cost of monitoring, and the severity of sanctions all moderate the efficiency gains from punishment. The model also predicts that initial 'free' monitoring (by some agents ignoring costs) may be needed to escape the no-cooperation equilibrium, but once cooperation is established, monitoring can be fully incentivized and efficient.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, quantitative guidance for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment in public-goods-like games, conditional on game design dimensions. It shows that the presence and structure of punishment (especially whether it is institutionally organized and collectively funded) are critical moderators of efficiency. The explicit inequalities derived in the paper allow one to predict whether enabling punishment will increase efficiency, given the marginal per-capita return, punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, group size, and the fraction of resources invested in monitoring. The findings suggest that, for a given control efficiency, enabling institutionally organized punishment with sufficient investment will reliably increase efficiency, while peer punishment may not. The results are most directly applicable to settings where monitoring and sanctioning are collective, and less so to purely peer-based punishment. The paper is highly relevant for mapping from design dimensions to expected efficiency outcomes.

- source: Powers, S. T., Perret, C., & Currie, T. E. (2023). Playing the political game: the coevolution of institutions with group size and political inequality. *Philosophical Transactions of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutionally arranged punishment in public goods games allows groups to sustain high levels of cooperation and achieve much larger group sizes (higher efficiency/welfare) than groups without such institutions. As groups grow, the cost of forming consensus over rules increases, but the evolution of hierarchy (a skewed distribution of influence) allows large groups to maintain efficient cooperation-promoting institutions by reducing the cost of consensus formation. Groups that invest just enough in punishment to deter defection, while allocating the rest to technology (increasing carrying capacity), achieve the highest efficiency. If hierarchy cannot evolve, large groups cannot sustain cooperation and remain small and inefficient. The model also shows that if the political game (consensus formation) is too difficult, groups may fail to maintain cooperation-promoting institutions, leading to lower efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment in public goods games increases group efficiency, especially as group size increases, provided that institutional rules are set optimally (not over- or under-investing in punishment). The model also highlights that the cost of consensus formation (political game) can limit the efficiency gains from punishment in large groups unless hierarchy evolves to reduce these costs. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is strongly moderated by group size, the cost of institutional rule formation, and the ability to evolve or implement hierarchy. The findings are most directly relevant to settings where punishment is institutionally arranged and the costs of monitoring/sanctioning are covered by the group, rather than being individually altruistic.

- source: Chen, J. C., D'Attoma, J., Fonseca, M. A., & Malezieux, A. (2025). Promoting Cooperation in an Unequal World: Experimental Evidence on the Role of Transparency and Punishment. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two lab experiments using a four-player linear public goods game with unequal endowments (determined by merit and luck), the paper finds that: (1) Peer punishment increases contributions and group payoffs, but only when endowments are transparent. (2) When endowments are secret, punishment has a limited effect on cooperation and does not reduce inequality; advantaged participants contribute a lower fraction of their endowment and earn much higher payoffs. (3) When endowments are made public, punishment becomes much more effective at increasing contributions and reducing inequality, especially among the advantaged. (4) Willingness to pay for secrecy is higher among advantaged participants, especially when their advantage is due to luck. (5) Overall, transparency is necessary for punishment to improve efficiency and mitigate the negative effects of inequality.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games is highly dependent on whether endowments (wealth) are transparent. In games with secret endowments, punishment has little effect on group payoff or efficiency; in games with public endowments, punishment significantly increases contributions and group payoffs, especially among the advantaged. For prediction, the presence of transparency (showing endowments or relevant summaries) is a key moderator: punishment is more likely to improve efficiency when transparency is high. The game uses continuous contributions, no chat, and a fixed MPCR of 0.4. Punishment is costly (cost 1, impact 3), and there is no reward option. The findings are most directly applicable to games with similar design features.

- source: Bühren, C., Dannenberg, A., & Händel, P. (2025). The demand for complete and incomplete punishment institutions to promote cooperation. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a public goods game with endogenous, costless, formal punishment institutions, complete punishment institutions (where all group members are covered) reliably lead to the implementation of deterrent punishment when cooperation is profitable, resulting in high contributions and, by implication, high efficiency. In contrast, incomplete punishment institutions (where only a subgroup is covered, or where subgroups can independently implement punishment) lead to less consistent implementation of deterrent punishment, lower average punishment rates, and lower contributions, even when cooperation is profitable. The marginal group return (MGR) is a strong predictor of punishment demand and contributions in complete institutions, but much less so in incomplete institutions, where individual cooperativeness and subgroup coordination become more important. Coordination failures between subgroups often prevent the realization of efficient outcomes in incomplete institutions.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the efficiency effect of punishment in public goods games, this paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment institution (complete vs. incomplete) is a critical moderator. When punishment is costless and covers all players, enabling punishment leads to near-maximal efficiency if cooperation is profitable. When punishment is incomplete (applies only to a subgroup or is implemented separately by subgroups), enabling punishment leads to less reliable efficiency gains, with outcomes depending more on group composition and coordination. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a strong predictor of efficiency gains from punishment in complete institutions, but much weaker in incomplete ones. Control efficiency (without punishment) is not directly reported, but the design implies low efficiency in the absence of punishment when cooperation is not profitable. The findings suggest that, for prediction, the presence of a complete, costless, formal punishment institution is a strong positive moderator of efficiency, while incomplete institutions introduce substantial uncertainty and lower average efficiency.

- source: Han, D., & He, Y. X. (2023). The impact of labor subsidy, taxation and corruption on individual behavior. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Theoretical analysis and numerical simulations show that: (1) Taxation, when used to fund both labor subsidies for executors and the costs of reward/punishment, can promote and stabilize cooperation in public goods games. (2) Moderate labor subsidies for executors (who implement reward/punishment) are optimal for forming stable alliances of cooperators and executors, excluding defectors. (3) Excessive reward (relative to punishment) or excessive subsidies can destabilize cooperation and allow defectors to invade. (4) The presence of a corruption (bribery) mechanism has little effect on cooperation levels when subsidies are high, but can slow the increase of cooperation as taxation increases. (5) The main outcomes are the steady-state fractions of each strategy; average payoffs are derived but not the focus. (6) The model is entirely theoretical and does not provide empirical or experimental data.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical, mechanistic evidence about how the structure of punishment, reward, taxation, and executor subsidies affect the evolutionary stability of cooperation in public goods games. It is directly relevant for understanding how punishment and reward mechanisms (and their funding) interact to affect cooperation, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff as a fraction of the social optimum. The results suggest that moderate, well-funded punishment (and reward) mechanisms, especially when executors are compensated, can stabilize cooperation, but excessive reward or subsidy can backfire. The presence of corruption can reduce the effectiveness of punishment, but this effect is mitigated by higher subsidies. For prediction tasks, this paper informs the expected direction and possible moderators of punishment's effect on cooperation, but not directly on efficiency.

- source: Sun, X. P., Bi, Y. Z., Kang, H. W., Shen, Y., & Chen, Q. Y. (2024). Effect of distribution of fines on evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Chinese Physics B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Redistributing fines from defectors to cooperators and punishers in a spatial public goods game generally increases the persistence of cooperation and raises the total system payoff, especially when punishment costs are high. When punishment costs are low, the standard pool punishment model (without redistribution) can achieve higher cooperation and higher total payoff, as punishers dominate and defectors are eliminated. The effect of fine redistribution is thus mixed: it is beneficial for efficiency and cooperation under high punishment cost, but can reduce efficiency and cooperation when punishment cost is low. The distribution parameter (alpha) also matters: allocating more fines to punishers (lower alpha) increases cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the cost of punishment and the mechanism for handling fines. If fines are redistributed to cooperators and punishers, efficiency losses from punishment can be mitigated, especially when punishment is costly. However, in settings where punishment is cheap, standard punishment (without redistribution) may yield higher efficiency by allowing punishers to eliminate defectors. For prediction, the presence or absence of fine redistribution, the punishment cost, and the fine distribution parameter (alpha) are important moderators. The results suggest that enabling fine redistribution is likely to increase efficiency when punishment is expensive, but may not always do so when punishment is cheap.

- source: Gao, S. P., Suo, J. H., & Li, N. (2025). Cooperation under endogenous punishment in the spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous punishment can promote cooperation in spatial public goods games when the network reciprocity alone is insufficient (i.e., when the enforcement factor is below the threshold for cooperation in the absence of punishment). The effectiveness of punishment depends on the willingness of cooperators to punish (probability p), the cost and fine of punishment, and the network degree. There is a critical value of p above which cooperation can be sustained. However, when network reciprocity is already strong enough to support cooperation, adding endogenous punishment can actually hinder cooperation or have no additional effect. The evolutionary dynamics can be bi-stable, with both all-cooperator and all-defector states possible depending on initial conditions. The model provides explicit threshold formulas for when cooperation is favored.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical guidance on how endogenous punishment, punishment cost and effectiveness, and network structure interact to affect the likelihood of achieving full cooperation (and thus maximal efficiency) in spatial public goods games. While it does not report empirical efficiency values, it gives explicit formulas for the thresholds at which cooperation becomes stable or dominant, as a function of the marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network degree. For prediction tasks, the results suggest that enabling punishment can increase efficiency (by supporting all-cooperation) when the control game is below the cooperation threshold, but may have little or even negative effect when the control game already achieves high cooperation due to network reciprocity. The model is most informative for structured populations with endogenous, probabilistic punishment.

- source: Nakagawa, M., Lefebvre, M., & Stenger, A. (2022). Long-lasting effects of incentives and social preference: A public goods experiment. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that all three incentive mechanisms (monetary punishment, non-monetary reward, recommendation) significantly increase contributions to the public good during the policy phase. Monetary punishment is most effective in raising contributions, but this effect is not persistent: contributions drop sharply after the punishment phase ends. Non-monetary reward and recommendation also increase contributions, with some evidence of longer-lasting effects, especially for recommendation among certain types (e.g., free-riders). However, the main outcome is contribution rate, not efficiency or group payoff. The study also finds that the effect of incentives varies by social preference type: monetary punishment increases contributions across all types, while non-monetary reward and recommendation mainly affect strong conditional cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling monetary punishment in a repeated linear PGG increases contribution rates during the intervention, but the effect is not persistent after removal. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency outcomes is not possible. The design dimensions (player count, rounds, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are well specified and match standard PGG lab experiments. The findings support the expectation that punishment increases cooperation (and likely efficiency) during the intervention, but the lack of payoff/efficiency data means the magnitude of efficiency gains cannot be directly inferred. The results are most useful for predicting contribution rate changes, not efficiency per se.

- source: Coutts, A. (2024). The age of consequences: Unraveling conflict's impact on social preferences, norm enforcement, and risk-taking. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that exposure to conflict (via radio reception of hate messages during the Rwandan genocide) is associated with higher contributions in public goods games, but this effect is entirely driven by individuals who were children during the genocide. In games with punishment and reward options, conflict exposure increases the likelihood of punishing and rewarding others, but this effect is driven by those who were older during the genocide. Risk-taking, as measured by contributions in a risky public goods game and by survey, is higher for all age groups exposed to conflict. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. However, it does show that the presence of punishment increases contributions relative to baseline, and that conflict exposure increases the propensity to punish.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment and reward options affect contribution behavior in public goods games, and how these effects are moderated by past conflict exposure and age at exposure. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment increases contributions (relative to baseline), and that the propensity to punish is higher among those exposed to conflict as adults. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides indirect evidence that punishment can increase cooperation, but does not quantify the effect on group payoff or efficiency. The detailed game design information and behavioral results are useful for understanding the context and potential moderators.

- source: Zhao, Y. R., Li, Z. R., Jin, S., & Zhang, X. M. (2024). The Impact of Cognitive Load on Cooperation and Antisocial Punishment: Insights from a Public Goods Game Experiment. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that (1) the presence of a punishment option increases average contributions to the public goods pool, (2) high cognitive load reduces contributions and accelerates the collapse of cooperation, and (3) high cognitive load increases the frequency and amount of antisocial punishment, but does not significantly affect punishment of free riders. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how punishment and cognitive load affect contribution behavior in a two-player linear public goods game. It confirms that punishment increases contributions, and that high cognitive load undermines cooperation and increases antisocial punishment.
