However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The behavioral results (contribution rates) may be informative for predicting efficiency in similar designs, but the lack of payoff data is a key limitation for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Funk, F., & Mischkowski, D. (2022). Examining Consequentialist Punishment Motives in One-Shot Social Dilemmas. *Zeitschrift Fur Psychologie-journal of Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study found that providing participants with the prospect of receiving information about the effect of their punishment on transgressors did not increase punishment investments in a one-shot public goods game. There was no significant main effect of the information manipulation, nor any interaction with dispositional prosociality (SVO or Honesty-Humility). The only consistent predictor of punishment was the participant's relative contribution compared to group members. The study does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, and the main focus is on punishment motives and behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a one-shot public goods game with four players, punishment is infrequent and not significantly affected by the availability of consequentialist information. The main outcome is punishment behavior, not efficiency or group payoff, so the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, the low punishment rates and high baseline cooperation suggest that, in similar one-shot, small-group, no-communication settings, introducing punishment may have limited behavioral or efficiency impact, especially when baseline cooperation is high. The paper does not provide direct evidence for the efficiency prediction task, but suggests that punishment may not always be effective in increasing cooperation or efficiency in one-shot games.

- source: Eisenkopf, G., & Walter, C. (2022). Leadership with Imperfect Monitoring. *Leadership Quarterly*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that in a three-player, 20-round public goods game, punishment by a leader only increases contributions when the leader can observe individual follower contributions (individual monitoring). Under group monitoring (only aggregate info), punishment does not increase contributions compared to a simple recommendation. Leading-by-example increases coordination (alignment of contributions) but not aggregate contributions relative to recommendations. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined. However, since contributions are higher with punishment under individual monitoring, it is likely that efficiency would also be higher in that condition, assuming standard PGG payoff structure. The effect of punishment is thus strongly moderated by the monitoring technology.
  prediction_guidance: For prediction tasks, this paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends critically on whether the punisher can observe individual contributions (i.e., the 'punishment_tech' and monitoring dimension). If only aggregate (group-level) monitoring is available, punishment does not increase cooperation or likely efficiency. If individual-level monitoring is available, punishment increases cooperation and likely efficiency. The paper does not provide direct efficiency or payoff data, so predictions must be inferred from contribution rates. Other design dimensions (player count, number of rounds, MPCR, etc.) are clearly specified and match standard PGG setups. The absence of communication and rewards, and the use of continuous contribution choices, are also informative for prediction.

- source: Alt, M., Gallier, C., Kesternich, M., & Sturm, B. (2023). Collective minimum contributions to counteract the ratchet effect in the voluntary provision of public goods. *Journal of Environmental Economics and Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation in public goods games depends critically on the perceived probability of enforcement. When punishment is enforced with high probability (p=1 or p=0.8), contributions are higher and sustained over time. When enforcement is weak (p=0.2), contributions decline and punishment does not deter free-riding. The study also finds that punishment behavior is backward-looking: individuals who escaped punishment in the past are more likely to be punished later. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the credibility of punishment enforcement is a key moderator of punishment's effect on cooperation in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the contribution results imply that efficiency gains from punishment are only realized when enforcement is highly credible. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the effectiveness of punishment in increasing efficiency is contingent on the probability of enforcement. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the precision of quantitative predictions.

- source: Hoeft, L., & Mill, W. (2024). Abuse of power An experimental investigation of the effects of power and transparency on centralized punishment. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated public goods game with a single designated punisher, punishers contribute significantly less than non-punishers, and the gap widens over time. Punishers enforce contribution norms on others that they do not themselves follow. These results are robust to information conditions (private vs. public knowledge of contributions). The paper does not report on group efficiency, total group payoff, or welfare, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the behavioral effects of punishment in a public goods game, specifically that designated punishers may enforce cooperation while themselves contributing less. However, because the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes when punishment is enabled. The findings suggest that the presence of a designated punisher can stabilize higher contributions among non-punishers, but the punisher's own low contributions may offset some of the efficiency gains. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper signals that the structure of punishment (e.g., designated vs. peer) and the behavior of punishers are important, but quantitative efficiency effects cannot be extracted from this study.

- source: Ozono, H., & Nakama, D. (2022). Effects of experimental situation on group cooperation and individual performance: Comparing laboratory and online experiments. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds no significant difference in PGG contribution or punishment behavior between laboratory and online settings among Japanese university students. The introduction of punishment does not significantly increase average contributions or maintain higher cooperation levels, though it may slow the decay of cooperation over rounds. In the online-worker sample, cooperation is lower and the effect of punishment is even weaker. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution, punishment), not efficiency or group payoff. The paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is likely weak or negligible in these settings, especially with a punishment efficiency of 2 (punishment impact per unit cost).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a standard repeated PGG with a punishment efficiency of 2, enabling punishment does not meaningfully increase average group cooperation or, by implication, efficiency, in either laboratory or online settings among Japanese university students. In a Japanese online-worker sample, punishment may even be associated with lower cooperation. The absence of direct efficiency or group payoff data limits quantitative prediction, but the behavioral results suggest that, under these design parameters, the efficiency gain from enabling punishment is likely minimal or absent. The study also highlights the importance of sample heterogeneity and the specific punishment parameters (cost and effectiveness) in moderating punishment effects.

- source: Hopp, D., & Süss, K. (2024). How altruistic is indirect reciprocity? - Evidence from gift-exchange games in the lab. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment shows that when a single group member is given punishment power in a repeated public goods game, most punishers abuse their power by punishing others for contributing more than themselves. Abuse is highest when punishment power is high and transparency is low. High transparency reduces abuse only in the high-power condition. Contribution rates of non-punishers increase over time, especially when transparency is high, but punishers consistently contribute less than non-punishers except in the high-power, high-transparency condition. The study does not report group efficiency or total payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how centralized punishment power and transparency affect behavioral outcomes like abuse of power and contribution norms in public goods games. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that centralized punishment can lead to norm enforcement but also to widespread abuse, especially when power is high and transparency is low. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper is only indirectly informative: it shows that punishment can increase contributions (especially among non-punishers), but also that punishers may undercontribute, potentially reducing group efficiency. The interaction between power and transparency is important for behavioral outcomes, but the net effect on efficiency remains unreported.

- source: Jin, S. X., Columbus, S., van Lange, P. A. M., & Balliet, D. (2024). Conflict, cooperation, and institutional choice. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, higher conflict of interests (lower MPCR) leads to lower cooperation, lower beliefs about others' cooperation, and lower normative expectations and personal norms of cooperation. However, the degree of conflict does not increase the likelihood that individuals or groups vote to establish a sanctioning institution (peer punishment, centralized punishment, or gossip/ostracism). Centralized punishment is more likely to be chosen than peer punishment or gossip/ostracism, but this is not moderated by conflict level. The main outcomes are behavioral (contributions, beliefs, norms, votes), not efficiency or group payoff. There is no direct evidence on how enabling punishment affects efficiency or group payoff relative to a no-punishment baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence on how conflict of interests and institutional choice affect cooperation, beliefs, and norms in public goods games, but does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment. Thus, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. However, it does show that the mere availability of punishment institutions (when endogenously chosen) is not more likely under high conflict, and that cooperation declines more rapidly under high conflict. The lack of efficiency or payoff analysis means this paper should be used for context and mechanism, not for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency effects.

- source: Xu, Z. C. (2023). Obedience to the symbol of authority: Experimental evidence on the symbolic source of legitimate authority*. *European Journal of Political Economy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that introducing a symbolic scepter for the authority significantly increases citizens' support for the authority's punishment decisions, regardless of the payment scheme (appropriation vs. fixed salary) or information transparency. However, the symbol does not affect the frequency or style of punishment by authorities, nor does it affect citizens' contribution rates or group/individual payoffs. The mechanism is psychological: the symbol increases intrinsic obedience and perceived legitimacy, not actual cooperation or efficiency. There is no evidence that the symbol improves efficiency, welfare, or total group earnings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that symbolic manipulations of authority can increase perceived legitimacy (support for punishment) in public goods games with punishment, but do not affect efficiency, group payoffs, or contribution rates. For the downstream prediction task, the presence or absence of a symbolic authority is unlikely to affect the efficiency outcome when punishment is enabled, holding other game design dimensions and control efficiency constant. The main moderators (payment scheme, transparency) do not interact with the symbol to affect efficiency. Thus, symbolic authority should not be expected to improve efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: Chen, F. D., Nave, G., & Wang, L. (2025). Calculated Punishment. *Journal of Business Ethics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both prosocial and antisocial punishment rates are strongly affected by the cost and impact of punishment. When punishment is free, antisocial punishment increases substantially. The study does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, but the findings imply that the design of punishment (especially cost) can influence the prevalence of antisocial punishment, which is typically detrimental to group welfare. The main contribution is a process-level account of punishment decisions, showing that people are not intuitively predisposed to punish, and that punishment is a calculated response sensitive to incentives.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the cost and impact (effectiveness) of punishment are critical moderators of punishment behavior in public goods games. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, the finding that antisocial punishment increases when punishment is free suggests that low-cost punishment can undermine group welfare. For prediction tasks, the results support the importance of including punishment cost and effectiveness as key dimensions, and caution that enabling low-cost punishment may not always improve efficiency due to increased antisocial punishment. The paper does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency, so its value is mainly in highlighting moderators and mechanisms.

- source: Wu, Z. H., & Sun, Y. (2022). How to Treat Gossip in Internet Public Carbon Emission Reduction Projects?. *Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that gossip can promote cooperation in public goods game environments, even under heterogeneous interpersonal relationships. Among the three mechanisms for dealing with gossip (punishment only, punishment with reputation compensation, and punishment with monetary compensation), punishment with monetary compensation is most effective at increasing cooperation rates and total contributions. The model also identifies optimal parameter ranges for tolerance, penalty time, and rebirth coefficient to maximize cooperation. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of behavioral measures (cooperation speed, total contributions) rather than efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that monetary compensation for those wrongly affected by gossip, combined with punishment for false gossipers, can best promote cooperative behavior.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment (especially with monetary compensation for those wrongly affected) can increase cooperation rates and total contributions in public-goods-game-like environments. However, because the outcomes are not reported as efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be interpreted as supporting a more positive effect of punishment on cooperation and contribution behavior, rather than on efficiency per se. The model's design and parameter exploration may inform predictions about the likely direction of efficiency changes when punishment is enabled, but quantitative predictions about efficiency require caution. The paper is most useful for understanding the behavioral mechanisms and the relative effectiveness of different punishment/compensation schemes.

- source: Shen, Y., Guo, J., & Kang, H. W. (2024). The Influence of Fine Distribution and Compensation on Cooperation in Public Goods Game. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study shows that introducing probabilistic punishment into a spatial public goods game increases the prevalence of cooperation compared to the baseline. Adding fine distribution (punishers receive a share of fines from defectors) and compensation (punishers compensate cooperators when punishment fails) further increases the prevalence and stability of cooperation, especially across a broader range of punishment costs and intensities. The compensation mechanism is most effective at low punishment costs, while fine distribution is most effective at high punishment costs. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that probabilistic punishment, fine distribution, and compensation mechanisms can increase the prevalence of cooperation in spatial public goods games. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that, under similar game design dimensions (e.g., spatial structure, probabilistic punishment, fine distribution, compensation), enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates, which may be associated with higher efficiency in many PGG contexts, but this is not directly demonstrated. The paper is most informative for predicting the direction and robustness of cooperation rate changes, not for quantitative efficiency predictions.

- source: Posten, A. C., Ugurlar, P., Kube, S., & Lammers, J. (2025). Maintaining cooperation through vertical communication of trust when removing sanctions. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that when punishment institutions are removed from a public goods game, contributions (i.e., cooperation) typically drop sharply. However, if the authority communicates trust in participants' willingness to cooperate, this drop is substantially reduced, and contribution levels remain similar to those observed when punishment is still in place. This effect is observed for centralized, third-party, and peer punishment mechanisms. The effect of vertical trust communication persists beyond a single round and is mediated by increased trust in coplayers. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency cannot be determined, but the findings strongly suggest that vertical trust communication can substitute for punishment in maintaining cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the removal of punishment institutions in public goods games leads to a sharp decline in cooperation (contributions), but that this decline can be prevented if the authority communicates trust in participants' willingness to cooperate. While the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, the close link between contribution rates and efficiency in standard PGGs suggests that vertical trust communication could maintain efficiency at levels similar to those with punishment, at least in the short term. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence or absence of vertical trust communication is a critical moderator when punishment is removed, but does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes. The game design dimensions (player count, rounds, MPCR, etc.) are well specified, but the main outcome is contribution, not efficiency.

- source: Dewey, G., Ando, H., Ikesu, R., Brewer, T. F., Goto, R., & Nishi, A. (2024). Punishment is slower than cooperation or defection in online network games. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment decisions are consistently slower than cooperation or defection in repeated network public goods games. The frequency of punishment is low (~6-7%) and is not significantly affected by the imposition of time pressure. Time pressure reduces decision times but does not shift the frequency of punishment, cooperation, or defection. The study classifies punishment decisions by hypothesized mechanism (retaliation, negative reinforcement, inequality aversion, unclassified) and finds no effect of time pressure on the frequency of any mechanism. The paper does not analyze the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, and payoff outcomes are only reported as descriptive means.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the behavioral frequency and timing of punishment in repeated network PGGs, but does not report or analyze efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment. Therefore, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency. It does, however, confirm that punishment is rare and not easily suppressed by time pressure in this design, which may be indirectly relevant for predicting the magnitude of any efficiency effect (i.e., if punishment is rare, its effect on efficiency may be limited).

- source: Rodrigues, J., Leipold, N., Hewig, J., & Hein, G. (2024). The relationship of the source of punishment and personality traits with investment and punishment in a public goods game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that allowing punishment from the common pool (i.e., at no personal cost) or giving participants a choice between punishment sources increases investment in the public good compared to a no-punishment baseline. When punishment must be paid from one's own money, investment does not increase relative to the no-punishment condition. Punishment is more frequent and higher in magnitude when it can be taken from the pool. Personality traits (anger, altruism, empathy) modulate investment and punishment behaviors, but these effects are secondary to the main manipulation. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the design of the punishment mechanism (who pays the cost) affects contribution rates in public goods games. Specifically, punishment that is free to the punisher (taken from the pool) increases contributions, while punishment that is costly to the punisher does not. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on whether punishment is personally costly or not, and that punishment from the common pool may increase contributions but could also reduce total group resources due to the cost being taken from the pool. For prediction tasks, this paper is most useful for understanding how punishment design affects contribution behavior, but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Wang, Y., & Lu, S. N. (2025). Environmental-feedback-driven time-varying reputation update solve social dilemmas. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The study demonstrates that in a continuous SPGG with punishment, the use of exploration-based particle swarm optimization (EPSO) and its parameter optimization via Q-learning (QPSO) can rapidly drive the system to a state of full cooperation (ACI=1) under certain parameter regimes. Both low and high values of the individual learning coefficient (c1), higher inertia weight (omega), lower punishment intensity (eta), and higher gain intensity (r) promote higher cooperation intensity. The main reported outcome is ACI, not direct group payoff or efficiency. The findings are based on simulation experiments with large populations (L=200, i.e., 40,000 agents) on a square lattice, and the results are robust across a range of parameter values.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task: while it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that punishment (with certain parameterizations) can lead to maximal cooperation intensity in a spatial PGG with continuous contributions. The results suggest that, in similar simulation/theoretical models, enabling punishment with low punishment intensity and high gain intensity can drive the system to near-full cooperation, which would likely correspond to high efficiency if mapped to payoff outcomes. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff reporting limits the direct applicability to the prediction task. The findings are most relevant for understanding how parameter settings (punishment intensity, gain intensity, learning coefficients) modulate the effect of punishment on cooperation, which may inform priors for efficiency outcomes in similar game designs.

- source: Huang, Y. F., Zhang, Y. L., Hu, X. M., Yang, W. Q., & Mai, X. Q. (2024). The development of cooperation and cooperative norm enforcement in children and adolescents: The role of in-group favoritism. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that children and adolescents show in-group favoritism in both cooperation (higher contributions to in-group members) and norm enforcement (more lenient punishment of in-group free-riders), but these biases decline with age. Eighth graders show less bias in both cooperation and punishment. The effect of group membership on cooperation is mediated by cooperative expectations in fourth and sixth graders, but not in eighth graders. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined. The punishment mechanism is present as a third-party punishment opportunity, but only one-shot decisions are made, and the main outcome is tokens spent to punish, not group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral effects of punishment opportunities in a one-shot 4-player PGG with children and adolescents, specifically regarding in-group/out-group bias in cooperation and punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design dimensions (player count, MPCR, punishment cost/tech, etc.) are described and can be extracted, but the main outcomes are contribution and punishment behavior, not efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how group membership and age moderate cooperation and punishment behavior, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. If using this paper for prediction, one should note that the presence of punishment allows for third-party costly punishment of free-riders, but the effect on group efficiency is not measured.

- source: Zhang, Y. G., Zhu, Q., & Li, Z. Q. (2024). The Nature and Motivation of Human Cooperation from Variant Public Goods Games. *Human Nature-an Interdisciplinary Biosocial Perspective*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that in a repeated PGG with essential costs and a punishment mechanism for the lowest contributor, nearly all participants make non-zero contributions, and contributions are higher in intergroup competition than intragroup competition. The punishment mechanism ensures that absolute free-riding (zero contribution) is extremely rare, as punished individuals cannot benefit from the public good. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoffs, or welfare, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly assessed. The main behavioral outcome is contribution level, not payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that a punishment mechanism targeting the lowest contributor in a repeated PGG with essential costs leads to near-universal non-zero contributions and higher contributions in intergroup competition. However, because the study does not report group efficiency or total payoffs, it does not directly inform the prediction of treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment can strongly suppress absolute free-riding, but the efficiency implications (e.g., whether punishment increases or decreases group payoff relative to the no-punishment baseline) cannot be determined from this paper.

- source: Kingsley, D. C., & Smith-Walter, A. (2024). Grid-group cultural theory and behaviour in a public good experiment. *Applied Economics Letters*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that high-group individuals contribute more to the public good, both with and without punishment. High-grid individuals are more likely to monitor and punish others when the punishment mechanism is available. The presence of punishment increases average contributions for all groups, but the paper does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare. The main focus is on how cultural orientation moderates contribution and punishment behaviors, not on the efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the opportunity for punishment increases contributions in a standard public goods game, and that individual differences in cultural orientation moderate this effect. However, it does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design details (group size, rounds, MPCR, punishment cost/tech) are clearly specified, which may help contextualize the results for other studies, but the lack of efficiency data limits its direct predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Guilfoos, T., & Suter, J. F. (2025). Time pressure and motivated delay effects on sanctioning in a social dilemma: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that cognitive manipulations (time pressure and motivated delay) do not affect the probability that groups vote to adopt sanctions in a public goods game. However, motivated delay reduces average contributions to the public good, and when sanctions are adopted, they are more effective at increasing contributions in the motivated delay condition. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly determined from the reported results. The findings suggest that while cognitive mode does not affect rule adoption, it can affect the behavioral response to sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a linear PGG with endogenous voting on a fixed-cost, non-deterrent sanction, the adoption of sanctions is not affected by cognitive manipulations (time pressure or motivated delay). While the effect of punishment on efficiency is not directly reported, the results suggest that sanctions can increase contributions, especially under motivated delay, but the cost of sanctions may offset these gains. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that the presence of a costly, group-level sanction can increase contributions, but efficiency effects depend on whether increased contributions are sufficient to cover sanction costs. The paper does not provide direct quantitative evidence on efficiency, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited.

- source: Makovi, K., Bonnefon, J. F., Oudah, M., Sargsyan, A., & Rahwan, T. (2025). Rewards and punishments help humans overcome biases against cooperation partners assumed to be machines. *Iscience*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that both peer rewards and punishments independently increase contributions in a repeated public goods game, regardless of whether partners are assumed to be humans or machines. However, the gap in contributions ('machine penalty') between human- and machine-assumed partners persists unless both rewards and punishments are available together, in which case the gap is eliminated. The combination of rewards and punishments is uniquely effective for machine-assumed partners but inefficient for human-assumed partners. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public goods game with four players and 20 rounds, enabling peer punishment (and/or reward) increases contribution rates, but only the combination of both eliminates the machine penalty in contribution. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, it does not directly inform the prediction of efficiency outcomes. The findings are most useful for predicting changes in contribution rates under different sanctioning regimes, especially in human-machine interaction contexts, but less so for direct efficiency prediction unless contribution is used as a proxy.

- source: Feng, S. A., & Liu, X. S. (2023). Effects of the limited incentive pool on cooperation evolution in public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of a limited punishment pool in a spatial public goods game strongly suppresses defectors and promotes the dominance of cooperators, more so than a limited reward pool. Increasing the capacity or cost of the punishment pool further reduces defection and can lead to full cooperation under certain parameter ranges. In contrast, the reward pool is less effective, and its impact on defectors depends on the cost and capacity settings. The model does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the results imply that punishment is a more robust mechanism for sustaining cooperation in resource-limited institutional settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games with limited institutional resources, punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward mechanisms at promoting cooperation and suppressing defection. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, enabling punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates and reduce defection, which may translate to higher efficiency, but this is not directly quantified. The model details (e.g., spatial structure, limited pool, agent types) should be considered when transferring these insights to other settings.

- source: Pancotto, F., Righi, S., & Takács, K. (2023). Voluntary play increases cooperation in the presence of punishment: a lab in the field experiment. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiments show that punishment increases contributions in public goods games, both when participation is compulsory and when it is optional. The effect is strongest when participation is compulsory and punishment is available. Optional participation (opt-out) alone does not significantly increase contributions compared to compulsory participation. Loners (those who opt out) contribute less when forced to participate, but are not responsible for anti-social punishment. Instead, low contributors (free-riders) are the main source of anti-social punishment. The study does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, but the contribution data suggest that punishment increases group welfare, especially in compulsory settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in discrete public goods games with 4 players, 1:3 punishment technology, and no communication, enabling punishment increases contributions, especially when participation is compulsory. While efficiency is not directly reported, the increase in contributions strongly suggests that efficiency would also increase with punishment, particularly in compulsory games. The design dimensions are well specified, allowing for mapping to prediction tasks, but the lack of direct efficiency or group payoff data means that predictions must be inferred from contribution outcomes. The results are most informative for settings with similar group size, discrete contributions, and punishment technology.

- source: Wang, T. M., Heine, F., & van Witteloostuijn, A. (2023). Pro-social risk-taking and intergroup conflict: A volunteer's dilemma experiment. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that in a standard public goods game, pure punishment or pure reward alone (without tax) cannot sustain cooperation in a well-mixed population. Introducing a tax-based mechanism (where all players pay a tax to fund punishment or reward) enables the stable coexistence of cooperators and tax-based pure punishers or rewarders, thus sustaining cooperation. Tax-based pure reward leads to a higher frequency of cooperators than tax-based pure punishment. The analysis is based on replicator dynamics and focuses on the evolutionary stability of strategy frequencies, not on direct efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical evidence that institutional (tax-based) punishment and reward mechanisms can stabilize cooperation in public goods games, whereas individual-level (pure) punishment or reward cannot. However, the outcomes are reported in terms of the frequency of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that enabling institutional punishment (via tax) is likely to increase cooperation rates, and that institutional reward may be even more effective. However, it does not provide direct quantitative predictions about efficiency or group payoff, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency is indirect and limited to the expectation that efficiency should increase when tax-based punishment or reward is enabled.

- source: Kang, H. W., Liu, S. X., Chen, Q. Y., Shen, Y., & Sun, X. P. (2024). Bonus-based mercenary punishment promotes cooperation in public goods games. *Heliyon*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that a bonus-based mercenary punishment mechanism can substantially increase the proportion of cooperators in a public goods game, compared to the standard game without punishment. The effect is sensitive to the size of the bonus, the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and the allocation of fines collected from defectors. There is an optimal range for the bonus: too little fails to support punishers, too much burdens cooperators. Allocating more fines to punishers (rather than cooperators) is more effective in sustaining cooperation. The model is simulated on a network, and the main reported outcomes are the proportions of each strategy over time and under different parameter settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (with bonus support for punishers) increases cooperation rates in public goods games, especially when the parameters are tuned appropriately. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The findings suggest that, in similar game designs, enabling punishment (with proper support) is likely to increase cooperation, which is often associated with higher efficiency, but this link is not directly quantified here. The paper is most useful for understanding the qualitative and parametric effects of punishment and bonus mechanisms on cooperation prevalence.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Wang, Z. Y., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Negative Feedback Punishment Approach Helps Sanctioning Institutions Achieve Stable, Time-Saving and Low-Cost Performances. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The negative feedback punishment approach, in which punishment intensity decreases as group cooperation increases, leads to more stable and higher cooperation rates, requires less time to reach high cooperation, and results in lower cumulative punishment intensity (interpreted as lower cost) compared to constant or positive feedback punishment methods. Theoretical analysis shows that this approach allows for stable intermediate levels of cooperation, while constant or positive feedback methods tend to produce unstable or all-or-nothing outcomes. The findings are based on evolutionary simulations of a public goods game with pool punishment, but do not report efficiency or group payoff directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the structure of the punishment rule (specifically, negative feedback between cooperation and punishment intensity) can substantially affect the stability and cost of achieving high cooperation in PGG-like environments. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, its direct value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited. The results suggest that negative feedback punishment rules may reduce the cost (in terms of punishment applied) and time to reach high cooperation, but do not quantify the effect on group efficiency. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment rule design (punishment_tech) and dynamic adjustment can moderate the behavioral and cost outcomes of punishment institutions.

- source: Shin, H. C., Vallury, S., Janssen, M. A., & Yu, D. J. (2022). Joint effects of voluntary participation and group selection on the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the effect of voluntary participation on the evolution of cooperation and altruistic punishment is asymmetric: in large groups and for a moderate range of nonparticipant payoffs, voluntary participation can increase the long-run frequency of cooperation compared to compulsory participation. If the nonparticipant payoff is too low, voluntary participation has little effect; if too high, it sharply reduces cooperation. In small groups, voluntary participation does not increase cooperation for any nonparticipant payoff. The model tracks frequencies of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff, so direct implications for efficiency are not reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a public goods game with punishment and voluntary participation, showing that the effect of enabling voluntary participation (an 'exit' option) depends on group size and the outside option's payoff. While the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, higher frequencies of cooperation (especially in large groups with moderate outside options) suggest that efficiency could be higher in those cases. However, because efficiency is not directly measured, the results should be used as informative indirect evidence: enabling voluntary participation may increase efficiency only in large groups and only if the outside option is not too attractive or too poor. The model's structure and parameterization are relevant for mapping to game design dimensions, but the lack of direct efficiency outcomes limits its predictive value for the downstream task.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of cooperation in optional public goods games with loners' punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The introduction of loners who punish cooperators (LP) into the optional public goods game leads to new evolutionary phases, including three-strategy cyclic states (C+D+LP), pure cooperator phases, and coexistence of cooperators with both types of loners. The presence and intensity of punishment (punishment cost and fine) modulate the transitions between these phases. Under certain conditions (e.g., high punishment fine and/or cost, high loner income), the system can escape the typical cyclic dominance and reach a pure cooperation phase or a coexistence phase where cooperators are protected by traditional loners from punishing loners. However, the paper does not quantify the effect of punishment on group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence and stability of cooperation as a strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed theoretical and simulation-based evidence on how punishment by loners affects the prevalence of cooperation in optional public goods games, under varying game parameters (synergy factor, loner income, punishment cost, punishment fine). However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results are most useful for understanding how game design dimensions (e.g., punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, loner income) affect the likelihood of cooperation dominating or persisting, which may be indirectly informative for efficiency predictions if one assumes that higher cooperation rates translate to higher efficiency. Caution is warranted, as the mapping from strategy frequencies to efficiency is not explicit in this work.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Quan, J., Ma, S. T., & Zhao, J. H. (2025). The evolution of cooperation and punishment in spatial public goods games with bribery. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in spatial public goods games with peer punishment, the introduction of bribery (defectors bribing punishers to avoid sanctions, and punishers accepting bribes) leads to complex phase transitions among strategies. When punishment intensity is low, bribery can increase the fraction of contributors (interpreted as higher cooperation), as bribe-recipients and bribers form alliances that resist defectors. As the bribe amount increases, the system can enter cyclic coexistence states involving bribers, bribe-recipients, and defectors. When punishment intensity is high, bribery undermines the maintenance of punishment and can reduce the contribution level. The effect of bribery is highly sensitive to the synergy coefficient (analogous to MPCR), punishment fine, and bribe amount. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the prevalence of strategies and contribution rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and bribery mechanisms interact to affect cooperation in spatial PGGs, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that the effect of punishment on cooperation (and by proxy, efficiency) is moderated by the possibility of bribery and the parameters of the game (synergy coefficient, punishment fine, bribe amount). In environments where punishment is weak, bribery can increase cooperation; where punishment is strong, bribery can undermine it. However, since efficiency is not directly measured, these results should be used cautiously for predicting efficiency outcomes. The model is also spatial and evolutionary, which may differ from typical lab PGGs.

- source: Zhang, Y. Q., Zheng, Z. H., Zhang, X. M., & Ma, J. L. (2025). Dynamic punishment-reputation synergy drives cooperation in spatial public goods game. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a reputation-based cost reduction for punishers in a spatial public goods game with dynamic probabilistic punishment greatly enhances the evolution and stability of cooperation. Higher increments to punisher reputation (T) further promote cooperation and accelerate convergence to full cooperation. The model demonstrates that cooperation can be sustained even when the cost of punishment exceeds the fine imposed on defectors, and that higher initial punishment probabilities speed up the emergence of cooperation. The effect of the maximum reputation threshold is minimal. However, all results are reported in terms of cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial public goods games with dynamic punishment and reputation-based cost reduction, punishment mechanisms can robustly increase cooperation rates, even under adverse cost conditions. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The payoff structure is specified, so in principle, efficiency could be inferred, but the paper does not report these results. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper provides only indirect support, suggesting that conditions which increase cooperation rate (such as reputation-based cost reduction for punishers) are likely to also increase efficiency, but this is not demonstrated directly.

- source: Qian, J., Zhang, T. D., Zhang, Y. F., Chai, Y. T., Sun, X., & Wang, Z. (2023). The construction of peer punishment preference: how central power shapes prosocial and antisocial punishment behaviors. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The model demonstrates that the effectiveness and efficiency of central power (e.g., the Rule of Law) determine the prevalence of antisocial punishment in a population, while prosocial punishment remains stable. Antisocial punishment is highest at intermediate levels of central power and suppressed when central power is either very strong or very weak. The population can be decomposed into three basic types (Collaboration Enthusiast, Fence Sitter, Rational Bystander), and the central power shifts the proportions of these types, which in turn determines the observed punishment and contribution behaviors. The model is validated at the 'phenomenal level' by comparison to cross-regional experimental data, but does not directly report efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for understanding how institutional context (central power) shapes the behavioral landscape of punishment and cooperation in public goods games. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions and control efficiency. It is most useful for predicting how changes in central authority or social norms might shift the prevalence of antisocial punishment and, indirectly, contribution rates. For direct efficiency prediction, empirical or simulation studies that report group payoff or efficiency are needed.

- source: Odouard, V. V., Smirnova, D., & Edelman, S. (2023). Polarize, catalyze, and stabilize: how a minority of norm internalizers amplify group selection and punishment. *Behavioral Ecology and Sociobiology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The presence of norm internalizers in the strategy space (even as a small minority, around 10% of the population) leads to higher mean cooperation rates, longer and more stable periods of high cooperation, and fewer long periods of low cooperation, compared to conditions with only group selection and punishment. This effect is most pronounced at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios, where cooperation is possible but not guaranteed. The mechanism is indirect: norm internalizers polarize groups (increasing inter-group variance), catalyze spikes in cooperation when global cooperation is low, and stabilize high-cooperation states. The effect is not due to norm internalizers themselves cooperating more, but to their influence on the behavior of other agents. The paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, so the direct effect on efficiency is not established.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in agent-based models of public goods games with group selection and imperfect punishment, adding a minority of norm internalizers can substantially increase mean cooperation rates, especially at intermediate benefit-to-cost ratios. However, because the reported outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used as indirect evidence for predicting efficiency: higher cooperation rates likely imply higher efficiency, but the mapping is not quantified here. The model assumes punishment is imperfect (not all defections are observed), and punishment is implemented as withholding benefits rather than costly punishment. The results are robust across two different model structures (abstract and spatial/naturalistic). For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the presence of norm internalization mechanisms can amplify the effect of punishment and group selection on cooperation, but does not provide direct quantitative estimates of efficiency.

- source: Guo, P., Wang, X. N., & Zhang, D. (2023). Optimization of supervision mechanism for public cooperation considering limited supervision ability and heterogeneous preferences. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that targeted punishment via the HPS mechanism increases the fraction of cooperators compared to random supervision, especially at higher synergy factors and with well-chosen exemption list parameters. Increasing supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list capacity generally increases cooperation rates. Moderately long observation periods and short removal times also promote cooperation. The results are robust across different network structures. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that targeted punishment mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in public goods games, and that design parameters such as supervision ability, penalty severity, and exemption list management are important moderators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The results may be useful for predicting the direction of change in cooperation rates under different punishment regimes, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Zhang, G., Yao, Y. C., Zeng, Z. Y., Feng, M. Y., & Chica, M. (2025). The evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game with tolerant punishment based on reputation threshold. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing punishment targeted at defectors in low-reputation groups, especially with a high reputation threshold and strong punishment, substantially increases the cooperation rate in spatial public goods games. The synergy of reputation and punishment mechanisms is key: when both are present and the environment is strict (high threshold, high punishment), cooperation dominates. When punishment is absent or the reputation threshold is low (lenient environment), defection dominates. The model demonstrates that the structure and targeting of punishment (via group reputation) can be critical for sustaining cooperation. However, the findings are reported in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial PGGs, the presence and targeting of punishment (especially when combined with reputation mechanisms) can dramatically increase cooperation rates. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results suggest that punishment targeted at low-reputation groups is likely to increase efficiency if cooperation and efficiency are closely linked. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, the direct quantitative effect on efficiency cannot be inferred. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment design and reputation interact to affect cooperation, which may be a proxy for efficiency in similar models.

- source: Du, K., Fan, R. G., Xue, H., Wang, Y. T., & Bao, X. G. (2023). Evolution of cooperation in spatial public goods game based on heterogenous social preferences and dynamic punishment. *Journal of Intelligent & Fuzzy Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) heterogeneous social preferences (especially altruism) promote higher cooperation rates in spatial PGGs; (2) dynamic punishment mechanisms are more effective than static punishment in increasing cooperation and addressing second-order free-riding; (3) the enhancement factor (r_e, analogous to MPCR) is a key moderator—when it is low, cooperation is hard to sustain even with punishment, but when it is high, cooperation is high even without punishment; (4) inequality aversion slightly inhibits cooperation, and reciprocity has a mixed effect depending on r_e. All findings are based on simulation of agent-based models, with the main outcome being the proportion of cooperators.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task: it suggests that enabling punishment (especially dynamic punishment) increases cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, particularly when the enhancement factor is moderate. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes. The findings are most useful for understanding how punishment and social preference heterogeneity affect cooperation rates, which may correlate with efficiency in some settings. The model's focus on spatial structure and dynamic punishment is relevant for games with similar features.

- source: Billinger, S., & Rosenbaum, S. M. (2023). On the limits of hierarchy in public goods games: A survey and meta-analysis on the effects of design variables on cooperation. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The meta-analysis finds that, among various design variables in hierarchical PGGs, only punishment reliably sustains higher contribution rates over time compared to baseline. The effect size for punishment is notably higher (d=0.75) than for other mechanisms. Rewards, monitoring, and other variables have positive but short-lived effects. The effect of punishment is stronger when it is free for the punisher than when it is costly. Group stability (partner matching), monitoring, and asymmetric information also increase cooperation, but not as durably as punishment. The analysis is based on contribution rates, not direct measures of efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in hierarchical PGGs increases and sustains contribution rates, which, in standard PGGs, is closely linked to higher efficiency. However, the meta-analysis does not directly report efficiency or group payoff, so predictions about efficiency must be inferred from the contribution results. The effect of punishment is robust across studies and is stronger when punishment is free. Other design variables (rewards, monitoring, group stability) have positive but less durable effects. For prediction tasks, the presence of punishment should be expected to increase efficiency, especially when punishment is not costly to the punisher, but the exact magnitude for efficiency must be inferred indirectly.

- source: Nielsen, Y. A., & Pfattheicher, S. (2024). Separating individual and group-level cooperation in the Public Goods Game. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that group-level convergence in cooperation (as measured by ICC) increases over the first five rounds of repeated PGGs and plateaus between 20% and 50% under standard conditions. The presence of punishment opportunities and detailed feedback (showing all group members' decisions) substantially increases group convergence, sometimes up to 70%. Small groups (≤4 players) and groups with homogenous social preferences also show higher convergence. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment opportunities and detailed feedback increase group-level convergence in cooperation in repeated PGGs, especially in small and homogenous groups. However, because the primary outcome is ICC (convergence in contributions) rather than efficiency or payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency. The results suggest that punishment is likely to increase behavioral alignment within groups, which may be associated with higher or lower efficiency depending on the equilibrium reached, but the paper does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Jin, S. X., Spadaro, G., & Balliet, D. (2025). Institutions and Cooperation: A Meta-Analysis of Structural Features in Social Dilemmas. *Journal of Personality and Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that enabling punishment (and/or reward) in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games robustly increases cooperation rates. The meta-analytic effect size for punishment is d = 0.60 (Study 2), indicating a substantial increase in cooperation when punishment is available. The effect is stronger in repeated interactions (partner matching) than in one-shot (stranger matching) games. Communication is also highly effective. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or net welfare, and notes that prior research (e.g., Bochet et al., 2006) found that punishment may increase contributions but not efficiency due to its cost. Thus, while the evidence for increased cooperation is strong, the effect on efficiency is not directly addressed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in public goods and prisoner's dilemma games increases cooperation rates, especially in repeated interactions. For the downstream prediction task, this suggests that, all else equal, enabling punishment is likely to increase average contributions. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform whether punishment increases, decreases, or leaves unchanged the efficiency of the group (i.e., total payoff relative to full cooperation). The costliness of punishment is not analyzed in relation to efficiency. Therefore, the paper is highly informative for predicting behavioral cooperation, but only indirectly informative for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Zachník, V. (2023). Social Norms and Agent Types: Bridging the Gap Between the Theoretical Models and Their Applications. *Philosophy of the Social Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper proposes a refined theoretical model of social norms that distinguishes between norm subjects, enforcers, and audience, and analyzes their mutual influences and behavioral possibilities. It argues that the presence of enforcers (who punish norm violations) is crucial for sustaining cooperation, referencing public goods game experiments and the literature on punishment. The paper also discusses how group identity, audience effects, and the structure of enforcement (centralized vs. distributed) can affect cooperation and norm stability. It introduces the concepts of 'misplacement' (enforcers avoiding punishment duties) and 'identification avoidance' (subjects avoiding norm-triggering situations) as subtle forms of norm violation. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes; its contribution is conceptual and integrative.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insights into how punishment, enforcement roles, and group structure may affect cooperation and norm stability in public goods game-like environments. It affirms, based on cited literature, that punishment can increase cooperation and that the structure of enforcement (centralized vs. distributed) can moderate these effects. However, it does not provide direct evidence or quantitative estimates for predicting efficiency outcomes as a function of game design dimensions. Its value for prediction is in highlighting the importance of agent roles, group identity, and enforcement structure as potential moderators, but it does not supply empirical data for model training or calibration.

- source: Levy, D. A. (2022). Optimizing the social utility of judicial punishment: An evolutionary biology and neuroscience perspective. *Frontiers in Human Neuroscience*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper argues that the human tendency to punish non-cooperators, even at a personal cost, is rooted in evolutionary and neural mechanisms. It reviews evidence from public goods games and other economic experiments showing that punishment can increase cooperation and, in some cases, average group profit. The author discusses the adaptive value of punishment, the neural reward mechanisms involved, and the importance of institutionalizing punishment to maintain social order and cooperation. The paper also highlights complexities and limitations, such as the costliness of punishment, the potential for retaliation, and the need for centralized enforcement in large societies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and review-based support for the idea that punishment can increase cooperation in public goods games and, under some conditions, improve group outcomes. It references empirical studies (e.g., Fehr & Gächter, Gächter et al., 2008) that find punishment can increase average profit, but also notes limitations and boundary conditions (e.g., costliness, retaliation, institutional context). For the prediction task, this paper supports the general mechanism by which punishment can improve efficiency, but does not provide direct quantitative evidence or dimension-specific effect sizes. It is most useful for contextualizing why and when punishment might be effective, rather than for precise prediction.

- source: Duong, M. H., Durbac, C. M., & Han, T. A. (2024). Cost Optimisation of Individual-Based Institutional Reward Incentives for Promoting Cooperation in Finite Populations. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the expected total cost to the institution for achieving a target cooperation level in finite-population PGGs depends strongly on the threshold for incentive application (t), the strength of selection (β), mutation rate (μ), and the type of incentive (reward, punishment, hybrid). For small thresholds (t=1), the cost function is always non-decreasing in the per-capita incentive cost, and explicit formulas are derived. For larger thresholds, the cost function can exhibit non-monotonic behavior and phase transitions, especially at high selection strength. Numerical results show that both reward and punishment incentives can be optimized for cost, but the optimal cost and its behavior depend on the game parameters and incentive scheme. The paper provides a framework for predicting the institutional cost required to achieve a desired cooperation level, given the game design and population parameters.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical and numerical evidence on how the cost of institutional incentives (reward, punishment, hybrid) required to achieve a target cooperation level in PGG-like environments depends on game design dimensions such as player count, group size, marginal per-capita return, and the structure of the incentive scheme (threshold t, cost per unit, effectiveness). While the main outcome is the institutional cost rather than group efficiency, the results are directly relevant for predicting how changes in punishment or reward design (e.g., cost, threshold, effectiveness) will affect the efficiency of institutional intervention. The paper is most informative for settings where the goal is to optimize the cost of achieving cooperation, rather than to maximize group payoff per se. It provides explicit formulas and numerical results that can be used to inform predictions about the efficiency of punishment or reward interventions, conditional on game design and baseline cooperation levels.

- source: Nhim, T., Schuch, E., & Richter, A. (2023). Water scarcity and support for costly institutions in public goods: Experimental evidence from Cambodia. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
