There is modest evidence that contributions are slightly higher at prime-numbered rounds, but this effect is driven by early rounds (2 and 3) and disappears when they are excluded. Placebo tests suggest the observed effect is unlikely to be spurious, but exploratory analysis casts doubt on the substantive importance of the prime-number effect. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and the experimental condition does not include punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as the analyzed condition lacks any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide a clear description of a standard PGG baseline (strangers, 4 players, 27 rounds, no punishment, no chat, continuous contributions, MPCR=0.1), which can be used as a control condition in comparative studies. The findings on contribution decline and the absence of efficiency or payoff analysis mean this paper is only useful for establishing baseline behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Griessmair, M., & Hippmann, P. (2022). Anger, Guilt, and Repeated Cooperation in Social Dilemmas. *Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in repeated public goods games, experienced anger (arising when others contribute less than expected) leads to a greater decline in contributions, while experienced guilt (arising when one contributes less than others or less than expected) cushions this decline. These effects are robust to controlling for cognitive-economic variables (beliefs about others' contributions). The effects of anger and guilt differ by cooperative type: free riders act on anger but not guilt, while cooperators and reciprocators act on both. The emotional effects are present in both partner and stranger matching, with some evidence for emotional carryover. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on the baseline dynamics of cooperation in repeated linear PGGs without punishment or reward. It shows that emotional responses (anger, guilt) are important drivers of contribution decline, but does not address the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding control (no-punishment) efficiency and the psychological mechanisms underlying contribution decline. It does not provide evidence on how punishment would affect efficiency, nor does it manipulate any of the key game design dimensions related to punishment or reward.

- source: Dorner, Z., Tucker, S., & Hassan, G. M. (2024). Heterogeneous productivity stabilizes public good contributions under certainty, uncertainty and ambiguity. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing heterogeneous productivity (certain, uncertain, or ambiguous) into a repeated public goods game leads to more stable contributions over time compared to the standard homogeneous productivity baseline, where contributions typically decline. Under certain heterogeneity, contributions remain stable and end higher than in the homogeneous case. Under uncertainty or ambiguity, contributions start lower but are also stable. The effect is driven by high-productivity types contributing more, which supports higher group contributions. However, the paper does not include any punishment or sanctioning treatments, and all outcome measures are behavioral (contribution levels), not payoff-based (efficiency, group earnings, or welfare).
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in productivity affects contribution dynamics, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects. The design dimensions (e.g., player count, rounds, MPCR) are well specified, but the absence of punishment means this paper cannot inform predictions about efficiency changes due to punishment.

- source: Bazart, C., Lefebvre, M., & Rosaz, J. (2022). Promoting socially desirable behaviors through persuasion and commitment: Experimental evidence. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing anonymous, open-ended communication (exhortations) in a public goods game leads to substantially higher contributions compared to a baseline with no communication. The effect is attributed to cultural norms and prior experience with exhortations, not to reputation-building, as anonymity precludes reputational effects. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not directly analyze efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on contribution rates and the content/effect of messages.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling anonymous communication (chat/exhortation) increases cooperation (contributions) in public goods games, even in the absence of reputation effects. However, it does not address punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is informative about the effect of the 'chat' dimension (present/absent) on contribution rates, but does not inform about the effect of punishment or about efficiency per se. It is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency, but supports the importance of communication as a moderator of cooperation.

- source: Yeo, J., & Zhuo, S. (2024). The usage of apologies and group cooperation. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Allowing participants to send apologies (either public or private) after observing contributions in a repeated public goods game leads to significantly higher individual contributions compared to a control with no communication. The effect is stronger for public apologies. The increase in cooperation is mediated by more optimistic beliefs about others and group-level adherence to sincere apology norms. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings. All outcome measures are behavioral (contributions, beliefs, apology usage).
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that simple, norm-laden communication (apologies) can increase cooperation in repeated public goods games, especially when made public. However, it does not study punishment, sanctions, or efficiency/payoff outcomes, so it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. Its findings may be useful for understanding the role of communication and social norms as alternative or complementary mechanisms to punishment, but it cannot be used to predict efficiency changes from enabling punishment.

- source: Liu, J., Sonntag, A., & Zizzo, D. J. (2022). Information defaults in repeated public good provision. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in a repeated linear public goods game (n=4, 10 rounds), the default for seeing group contribution information (opt-in vs. opt-out) does not affect the proportion of subjects who actually obtain the information. However, when the default is no information, the decline in contributions over time is slower, suggesting that cooperation unravels more slowly. The price of information (cost to see group contribution info) has a strong effect: when information is free or incentivized, almost all subjects get it; when costly, about a third still pay for it. Subjects adjust their contributions toward the group average, using beliefs when information is not available. There is no direct analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism. It does, however, provide informative evidence about the effects of information architecture (defaults and cost of information) on contribution dynamics. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly relevant. For tasks involving the effect of information availability or defaults on contribution (not efficiency), the finding that a default of no information slows the decline of cooperation may be useful. However, since efficiency is not measured or analyzed, and punishment is not present, this paper should not be weighted heavily for the core prediction task.

- source: Chang, D., Chen, R., Krupka, E. L., & Song, Z. W. (2024). Do policy instruments that restrict social identity expression increase economic cooperation?. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Restricting identity expression (banning pin display) increases contributions to the mixed-group public good when Leavers are the majority, with no effect on in-group contributions. The increase in mixed-group contributions is explained by more optimistic beliefs about others' contributions, not by changes in social norms or pinning behavior. The effect is context-specific and may not generalize to other identities, repeated games, or settings with different group compositions. No punishment or sanction mechanism is present, and efficiency or group payoff is not directly measured or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions in public goods games, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its findings are not directly relevant for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide detailed evidence on how identity expression restrictions can affect contribution behavior via beliefs, in a one-shot, simultaneous, multi-group PGG without punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment or efficiency, this paper is not informative.

- source: Bose, N., & Sgroi, D. (2022). The role of personality beliefs and small talk in strategic behaviour. *Plos One*.

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: Allowing 4 minutes of pre-game chat (small talk) between paired subjects leads to higher contributions in a two-person public goods game compared to a control group with no chat. This effect is mediated by beliefs about the partner's extraversion, which are formed during the chat. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. The findings are relevant for understanding the effect of pre-game communication and personality beliefs on cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public goods games. It does provide strong evidence that pre-game chat (communication) increases cooperation in a two-person public goods game, mediated by personality beliefs, but does not report group payoff or efficiency. For the downstream prediction task (punishment effect on efficiency), this paper is not directly informative, but it does support the importance of communication as a moderator of cooperation. It cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency.

  findings: The PPO-ACT algorithm, which combines proximal policy optimization with adversarial curriculum transfer, enables higher and more robust cooperation rates in spatial public goods games compared to standard PPO, Q-learning, and Fermi update rules. PPO-ACT achieves stable all-cooperator states at lower enhancement factors (r) than other algorithms, and is robust to challenging initial conditions (e.g., all-defector starts). The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators; no explicit analysis of group efficiency or total payoff is provided. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how advanced learning algorithms affect cooperation rates in spatial PGGs, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The design dimensions and results may be useful for modeling baseline cooperation dynamics in the absence of punishment, but not for the downstream task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Shi, Z. Y., Wei, W., Zheng, H. W., & Zheng, Z. M. (2023). Bidirectional supervision: An effective method to suppress corruption and defection under the third party punishment mechanism of donation games. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when the weight of reputation in the strategy update rule is moderate, expanding the influence range of high-reputation individuals promotes cooperation (higher fraction of cooperators). When the reputation weight is low, expanding the influence range actually reduces cooperation. The effect of expanding influence range has diminishing returns (marginal effect). The model is a spatial PGG on a square lattice, with no punishment or reward mechanisms. Payoff is only discussed for subgroups and not as a main outcome. The findings are about cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are about the effect of reputation-based influence range on cooperation rates in a spatial PGG. While it provides insight into how information and influence structures can affect cooperation, it is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Wang, S., Li, Z., Li, W. D., & Jiang, H. (2022). Continuous Spatial Public Goods Game Based on Particle Swarm Optimization with Memory Stability. *Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that in a continuous spatial PGG with PSO-based learning, the memory stability coefficient (α) has a complex effect on the average cooperation level. At low enhancement factors (r), high memory stability (large α) helps maintain a moderate level of cooperation, while reducing memory stability (lower α) leads to a collapse of cooperation. At high r, reducing memory stability (lower α) allows the system to forget low-investment strategies and promotes the formation of high-cooperation clusters, increasing the average cooperation level. The effect of memory stability is more pronounced when players rely more on their own history (high ω) than on neighbor imitation. No punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms are included in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanisms are present. The findings are relevant for understanding how learning rules and memory affect cooperation rates, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The results may be indirectly useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in spatial, continuous PGGs with PSO-type learning, but cannot inform the downstream prediction task regarding punishment effects.

- source: Kang, H. W., Jiang, C., Shen, Y., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). Neighbor-aware reinforcement learning fosters cooperation in spatial public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that integrating neighbor-aware reinforcement learning (NI) and reputation mechanisms in a spatial public goods game model can substantially increase the fraction of cooperators and promote the formation of stable cooperator clusters. The effect is especially strong when agents can observe a wider neighborhood (larger perceptual range). Reputation alone can promote cooperation if it is sufficiently weighted in the reward function, but the combination of NI and reputation produces a synergistic effect. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms in the sense of costly peer or institutional sanctions/rewards. The main dependent variable is the cooperation rate, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how social learning, reputation, and local information can promote cooperation, but not for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment. The model and results may be useful for understanding moderators of cooperation in spatial and learning-based PGGs, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Stock, E. V., Valverde, P., González-Avella, J. C., Iglesias, J. R., Gonçalves, S., & da Silva, R. (2025). Cyclical oscillations and absorbing-state probabilities in optional public goods games: Interplay of reward and group size. *Physical Review E*.

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that the OPGG exhibits rich dynamical behavior, including cyclic dominance among strategies and multistability in absorbing states, depending on the multiplication factor (r) and group size (S). For r < 2, loners dominate; for r > S, cooperators dominate; and for 2 < r < S, the system can end in any of the three absorbing states, with the likelihood and duration of cycles depending on S and r. Larger group sizes and higher r values tend to favor cooperation, but the model does not include punishment or analyze efficiency or total payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed evidence on how group size and the multiplication factor affect the stability and dynamics of cooperation, defection, and abstention in optional public goods games. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline dynamics in the absence of punishment.

  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

The model replicates standard PGG dynamics, showing that cooperation rate increases with the synergy factor r, and that their synchronous update method can foster cooperation at lower r values compared to a baseline. However, the paper does not include any punishment or reward mechanisms, nor does it report on efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes.

- source: Lv, S. J., Li, J. Y., & Zhao, C. H. (2025). Reinforcement learning in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The study finds that both higher synergy coefficient (r) and stronger environmental feedback (alpha) promote higher levels of cooperation in the SPGG. The effect of the Q-learning discount factor (gamma) on cooperation is non-monotonic and depends on the values of r and alpha. When r or alpha is low, higher gamma (more future-oriented agents) promotes cooperation; when r and alpha are high, lower gamma (more myopic agents) is more favorable for cooperation. The results are based on agent-based simulations and focus on the fraction of cooperators and spatial clustering, not on payoff or efficiency outcomes. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the model.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings are relevant for understanding how environmental feedback and reinforcement learning affect cooperation rates, but not for payoff-based outcomes or for the effect of punishment. The game design dimensions covered (e.g., player count, mpcr, rounds, absence of punishment/reward) may be useful for contextualizing baseline cooperation in spatial PGGs with environmental feedback, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Xu, Z. H., Liu, X., Wang, H. N., Cui, L. Q., Han, X. P., & Meng, F. Y. (2024). Free-rider or contributor: A dilemma in spatial threshold public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

  findings: The study investigates how the presence and behavior of late movers (who can adapt their strategy after observing others) affect cooperation in a spatial threshold public goods game. Key findings are: (1) Higher synergy factors (multipliers) increase cooperation; (2) Moderate fractions of late movers can enhance cooperation, but too many may reduce it; (3) Higher thresholds for game activation promote cooperation; (4) Clustering of late movers spatially can further increase cooperation. The main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not efficiency or group payoff. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.

However, it provides informative context on how game design parameters such as group size, threshold, and synergy factor affect cooperation rates in threshold public goods games. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation dynamics in threshold PGGs without punishment.

- source: Wang, J. W., Dai, W. H., Zheng, Y. F., Yu, F. Y., Chen, W., & Xu, W. S. (2024). Partial intervention promotes cooperation and social welfare in regional public goods game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.

The effect is robust across synergy factors (r). However, the paper does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes. There is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism in the model.

- source: Wang, J. W., Chen, W., Yu, F. Y., Zhou, S. Y., He, J. L., Xu, W. S., & Dai, W. H. (2024). The emergence of cooperation in the context of prior agreement with threshold and posterior compensation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.

  findings: The study finds that in a spatial public goods game where only the top V individuals by cumulative wealth can participate and must pay a participation cost, the effect of participation cost on cooperation depends on V. For small V, increasing participation cost can promote cooperation up to a point but increases wealth polarization. For large V, increasing participation cost slightly inhibits cooperation but reduces polarization. The paper does not analyze efficiency or group payoff, and there is no punishment or sanctioning mechanism studied.

It is not directly informative for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Cheo, R., Huang, K. A., & Li, J. P. (2023). Group cooperation of village officials in Chinese rural resettlement: A lab in the field. *Contemporary Economic Policy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that higher average contributions by village officials in a public goods game are associated with higher self-reported support for a resettlement project among villagers. This relationship is robust to controls for demographic and village characteristics. Mechanism analysis suggests that more cooperative leadership teams inform villagers earlier about the resettlement, and that higher PGG contributions are correlated with more altruistic behavior in a dictator game. However, the study does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes from the PGG. Actual sign-up rates for resettlement contracts are reported as supplementary evidence, showing a correlation with officials' PGG contributions, but these are not the main focus.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. It uses a standard PGG without punishment or reward, and the main outcomes are non-payoff behavioral measures (contribution, support, information flow). There is no manipulation or analysis of punishment, nor any efficiency or group payoff outcome reported. Therefore, this paper should not be used to inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes for the public good game. Exploratory analyses suggest some effects of redistribution on productivity in a real-effort task, but these are not the main focus and do not pertain to public good game efficiency.

- source: Luo, J., & Wang, X. X. (2023). Leave behind or migrate? Evidence from a field experiment in China concerning the impact of being left behind/migrating status on children's behaviour and preferences. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=exact | punishment=none | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study provides evidence that being left behind or migrating is associated with lower prosocial behavior (including cooperation in a public goods game) among children, with migrant children generally more negatively affected than left-behind children. The negative effects of migration dissipate over time and are less severe with shorter migration distances. Living with at least one parent mitigates some negative effects for left-behind children. However, the study does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism in the public goods game or other tasks, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

While it uses a public goods game to measure cooperation, it does not include any punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it does not inform the downstream prediction task regarding the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Del Ponte, A., Masiliunas, A., & Lim, N. (2025). Decentralized voluntary agreements do not reduce emissions in a climate change experiment. *Ecological Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, mpcr
  findings: In a lab experiment with a public bads game (3 players, 18 rounds), the introduction of voluntary pledge-and-review agreements, automatic financial penalties for exceeding pledges, and/or peer evaluation (reputation contest) did not reduce average production or disaster risk compared to baseline. Most participants joined agreements and complied with their pledges, but pledges were not ambitious enough to reduce emissions. Financial penalties increased compliance but did not improve group efficiency, as participants could avoid penalties by not joining or by making unambitious pledges. Peer evaluation did not reduce production and even reduced the ambition of pledges. The results suggest that, in decentralized and voluntary settings, punishment mechanisms do not improve efficiency unless participation and ambition can be enforced.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a voluntary, decentralized public bads game with 3 players and 18 rounds, adding automatic financial penalties for exceeding pledges does not improve group efficiency or reduce emissions compared to baseline, because participants can avoid penalties by not joining or by making unambitious pledges. Peer evaluation (reputation-based non-monetary punishment) also does not improve efficiency and may reduce ambition. For prediction, this suggests that in similar voluntary, opt-in, pledge-based games, the presence of punishment mechanisms (even if automatic and with high penalty rates) will not increase efficiency unless there are mechanisms to ensure ambitious pledges and broad participation. Control efficiency is a good predictor of treatment efficiency when punishment is voluntary and avoidable.

- source: Casari, M., & Tavoni, A. (2024). Climate clubs in the laboratory. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment compares several treatments, including a baseline public goods game (PGG), a version with punishment (PGGwP), and a 'climate club' treatment that combines conditional cooperation and mandatory punishment. The key finding is that adding punishment opportunities (and counter-punishment) does not significantly increase cooperation rates and leads to a statistically significant decrease in net earnings (efficiency) compared to the baseline. The climate club treatment increases cooperation rates but still results in lower net earnings than the baseline, due to the costs associated with punishment. The results are robust across different group compositions and treatments. The paper provides direct evidence that, in this design, punishment reduces efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a three-player, 30-round, binary-contribution public goods game with costly punishment and counter-punishment, enabling punishment reduces group efficiency (net earnings) compared to the control without punishment. The effect is robust and statistically significant. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, under similar design parameters (binary choice, no chat, known rounds, punishment cost per unit = 1, punishment effectiveness varies by target, no rewards), enabling punishment is likely to decrease efficiency relative to the control. The paper also shows that the presence of counter-punishment does not mitigate this effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the impact of punishment on efficiency in similar lab PGG environments.

- source: DeCaro, D. A., DeCaro, M. S., Janssen, M. A., Lee, A., Graci, A., & Flener, D. (2024). Learning from regulatory failure: How Ostrom's restorative justice design principle helps naïve groups create wiser enforcement systems to overcome the tragedy of the commons. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Facilitated groups (those guided to discuss enforcement using restorative justice principles) achieved higher group efficiency (net tokens harvested) than unfacilitated groups. The highest efficiency was observed in groups that first experienced regulatory abuse (punishment without communication) and then received facilitation (PFN treatment). Groups that experienced regulatory failure but did not receive facilitation often failed to develop effective enforcement systems and had lower efficiency. The presence of coordinated punishment systems (as opposed to independent or no punishment) was associated with the highest group efficiency. The study demonstrates that both the structure of punishment opportunities and the process of group learning and facilitation are critical moderators of punishment's effect on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of enabling punishment on group efficiency in public-goods-like games depends not only on the presence of punishment but also on the context in which it is introduced (e.g., prior experience with regulatory failure/abuse) and whether groups receive facilitation to discuss enforcement principles. In prediction tasks, the presence of facilitated discussion (restorative justice), the opportunity for communication, and the structure of the punishment system (cost, effectiveness, coordination) should be considered as moderators. The control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than the treatment efficiency (with punishment and facilitation), especially when groups experience regulatory abuse and are then guided to reflect and coordinate. The findings suggest that punishment can increase efficiency, but only under certain institutional and learning conditions.

- source: van Klingeren, F., & Buskens, V. (2024). Graduated sanctioning, endogenous institutions and sustainable cooperation in common-pool resources: An experimental test. *Rationality and Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: In a dynamic common-pool resource (CPR) game with four players and repeated rounds, the introduction of sanctioning (punishment) mechanisms—either strict or graduated—leads to higher cooperation and resource sustainability compared to no sanctioning. Strict sanctioning (fixed, high-cost punishment) initially results in higher resource size and profit, but graduated sanctioning (punishment severity increases with repeated defection) leads to greater cooperation and lower appropriation over time, eventually closing the gap in resource size and profit. When groups can endogenously choose their sanctioning mechanism (via majority vote every five rounds), cooperation and resource outcomes improve further, especially in the long run. However, the differences in group and individual profit between sanctioning types are small and diminish over time. The study provides partial support for the effectiveness of graduated and endogenously chosen sanctioning, especially for long-term cooperation, but strict sanctioning may be more effective in short games or early rounds.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, high-quality evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in repeated, dynamic CPR games with endogenous resource dynamics and mutual monitoring. The results suggest that both strict and graduated punishment mechanisms increase efficiency (resource sustainability and group profit) relative to no punishment. Strict punishment is more effective in the short term, but graduated punishment becomes equally or more effective over longer time horizons. Endogenously chosen punishment mechanisms (via group voting) further enhance cooperation and resource outcomes, though the effect on profit is small. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the expectation that enabling punishment will increase efficiency, with the magnitude and time course depending on the punishment regime and whether the institution is endogenously chosen. The findings are most applicable to settings with 4 players, 40 rounds, continuous contribution/appropriation, no chat, and mutual monitoring.

- source: Milinski, M., & Marotzke, J. (2022). Economic experiments support Ostrom's polycentric approach to mitigating climate change. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a lab experiment with groups of 9 players, subdivided into 3 subgroups of 3, the opportunity for subgroups to impose costly sanctions on other subgroups (punishment treatment) did not increase the probability of reaching the collective target or improve group payoffs compared to a control without sanctions. Sanctioning was used frequently, but its costs reduced overall payoffs, and it failed to discipline free riders effectively because fair contributors who were also sanctioned reduced their own contributions. Thus, punishment in this polycentric, subgroup-based setting did not improve efficiency or welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a public-goods-like collective-risk game with subgroup-level costly punishment, enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency or the probability of reaching the collective target, and may even reduce efficiency due to the costs of punishment and negative effects on fair contributors. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, in similar designs (polycentric, subgroup-based, punishment between subgroups, costly punishment), enabling punishment is unlikely to improve efficiency over the control condition without punishment, and may even be detrimental.

- source: Lu, Z. Y., Hua, S. J., Wang, L. C., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Hybrid reward-punishment in feedback-evolving game for common resource governance. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a feedback-evolving common resource game (a close variant of the public goods game), the introduction of punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) by a third-party institution significantly increases the likelihood of achieving both full cooperation and sustainable resource levels, which are direct analogs to high efficiency. Pure reward is less effective than punishment or hybrid schemes, especially when the resource growth rate is moderate. Increasing the per capita incentive (tax) and allocating more of the incentive budget to punishment (lower w) both make the ideal outcome (full cooperation and resource sustainability) more likely. When the resource growth rate is too low, no incentive scheme is effective. The results are robust across a range of parameter values and are supported by both analytical and numerical phase diagrams.
  prediction_guidance: For predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in environments where resource renewal is not too slow, adding punishment (or a hybrid of reward and punishment) increases the likelihood of achieving high efficiency (full cooperation and resource sustainability) compared to reward alone or no incentives. The effect is stronger when the per capita incentive is higher and when more of the incentive budget is allocated to punishment. The model is deterministic and infinite-population, but the qualitative insights about the superiority of punishment (or hybrid) over pure reward for efficiency are likely to generalize to similar institutional public goods settings. The paper does not address stochasticity, finite groups, or experimental data, but its results are directly relevant for theoretical prediction.

- source: Garrido, C. M., Santos, F. C., Domingos, E. F., Nunes, A. M., & Pacheco, J. M. (2025). A new electoral bottom-up model of institutional governance. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a collective risk dilemma (CRD) modeled as a threshold public goods game, the introduction of local, bottom-up institutions—created when a minimum number of group members contribute to their formation, and whose nature (reward or punishment) is decided by majority vote—substantially increases group efficiency (η_G) compared to no institution, reward-only, or punishment-only institutions. Reward-only institutions are generally more efficient than punishment-only, but the electoral (mixed) institution is superior to both, especially at lower risk levels. The model shows that the critical risk threshold for achieving a given level of group efficiency is lower under the electoral institution, and the transition from defection to cooperation is steeper. The model is theoretical and does not use empirical or experimental data, but provides direct predictions for how institutional design affects efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in threshold public goods games with risk of group failure, the presence and design of institutional punishment and/or reward mechanisms have large effects on group efficiency. Specifically, enabling local, flexible institutions that can implement either punishment or reward (as decided by group vote) leads to higher efficiency than fixed punishment-only or reward-only institutions. The model quantifies these effects using the η_G metric, which is directly analogous to efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that the presence of a flexible, locally decided institution (with both punishment and reward possible) should be expected to yield higher efficiency than either punishment-only or reward-only, and much higher than no institution. The model also provides parameterizations for group size, number of rounds, risk, and institutional costs, which can inform predictions when these dimensions are known.

- source: Kamei, K., Putterman, L., & Tyran, J. R. (2023). Civic engagement, the leverage effect and the accountable state. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: In a large-group lab experiment, participants first face a public goods allocation problem (main stage) and can only enable a central punishment institution (which enforces optimal contributions) if enough group members voluntarily complete costly 'civic tasks' in a pre-stage (second-order dilemma). When the punishment institution is absent, group efficiency is low (public sector allocations rapidly decline to ~13% of the optimum). When the institution is present, allocations and group earnings approach the social optimum. The probability of achieving the institution is much higher when the cost of civic engagement is low and when social interaction is allowed. The study demonstrates that the presence of a punishment institution, conditional on sufficient civic engagement, robustly increases efficiency in public goods provision. The effect is strongest when the cost of civic engagement is low and when social feedback is available.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling a central punishment institution in a public-goods-like environment dramatically increases efficiency, moving group payoffs close to the social optimum. The effect is conditional on the institution being present, which depends on a prior (second-order) cooperation problem. The efficiency gain from punishment is robust across treatments, but the likelihood of achieving the institution (and thus the efficiency gain) is higher when the cost of enabling punishment is low and when social interaction is present. For prediction tasks, this suggests that, given a design where punishment is enabled (i.e., the institution is present), efficiency will be much higher than in the control (no-punishment) condition. The effect is moderated by the cost of enabling punishment and by the presence of social interaction, but the direction is consistently positive for efficiency.

- source: Vinayak, R. K. (2025). FARMER'S GAME: A GAME THEORETIC APPROACH TOWARDS BURNING OF CROP RESIDUE. *Journal of Dynamics and Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, without intervention, individual incentives lead to inefficient outcomes (universal burning of crop residue), analogous to the tragedy of the commons. Social pressure can sometimes induce cooperation, but is often not strong enough. The introduction of government punishment (penalty per burned plot) and/or reward (per non-burned plot) can shift the Nash equilibrium to full cooperation (no burning), maximizing group payoff and efficiency. The model provides explicit conditions for when this shift occurs: when the sum of penalty and reward per plot exceeds the net private benefit of burning minus the greener alternative and pollution cost. Simulations confirm these theoretical results, showing that moderate levels of punishment and reward are sufficient to achieve efficient, cooperative outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical and simulation-based evidence that introducing punishment (and/or reward) in a public-goods-like environment with negative externalities can shift the equilibrium from inefficient (all defect) to efficient (all cooperate), provided the policy parameters are set so that the penalty plus reward per unit exceeds the net private benefit of defection. The model is directly relevant for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency: if the punishment is sufficiently strong (relative to the private incentive to defect), efficiency will increase dramatically, potentially reaching full efficiency. The findings are most applicable to settings where punishment is implemented as a per-action cost, is known to all, and is not so severe as to cause practical or political infeasibility. The model does not address empirical implementation issues, but provides clear guidance on the direction and mechanism of efficiency change.

- source: Wang, L. C., Hua, S. J., Liu, Y. Y., Zhang, L., & Liu, L. J. (2024). Threshold incentive mechanisms for the sustainable management of public resources. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that threshold-based punishment and reward mechanisms can effectively promote cooperation and sustainable management of public resources, leading to higher efficiency (resource sustainability and group payoff) at lower cost than continuous incentives. Punishment is only triggered when the number of cooperators exceeds a threshold, targeting defectors, while reward is triggered when cooperation is low or resources are depleted. These mechanisms can stabilize the system at higher resource levels and higher cooperation rates, but their effectiveness is limited if the resource's natural growth rate is too low or the initial resource stock is too small. The models also show that increasing population size can reduce equilibrium resource levels, and that the interplay of reward/punishment thresholds and strengths is important for optimal outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support that threshold-triggered punishment (and reward) mechanisms in public-goods-like resource games can increase efficiency relative to no incentives, especially when the resource can regenerate and initial conditions are not too dire. The effect of punishment is more positive for efficiency when it is targeted (threshold-based) rather than universal, and when combined with reward mechanisms. The model's explicit tracking of average payoffs and resource levels makes its results directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in games with similar design dimensions. However, the results are theoretical and may not capture all real-world behavioral nuances.

- source: Wang, J. F., & Shen, A. Z. (2024). The synergy of elimination and zero-determinant strategy on dynamic games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that implementing endogenous reward and punishment policies in a regional public goods game increases both cooperation and total social welfare (W_t), especially when the intervention is targeted (partial intervention) rather than universal. The efficiency gains from punishment are largest when the cost of policy implementation is low or the punishment is strong. There is often an optimal fraction of groups to intervene in, beyond which additional intervention yields diminishing or negative returns due to policy costs. Size-based selection of groups for intervention is generally superior to random or degree-based selection. Allowing non-intervened groups to spontaneously adopt the policy can further boost cooperation and welfare, but only if the additional costs are offset by increased benefits. Terminating intervention leads to rapid collapse of cooperation and welfare, indicating the need for sustained policy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical/simulation evidence that enabling punishment (and reward) in a PGG-like environment increases efficiency (total social welfare), but the magnitude and even the sign of the effect depend on the cost of policy implementation, the strength of punishment, and the fraction of groups covered. For prediction, the model suggests that punishment will generally increase efficiency relative to control, especially when the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is not too low and policy costs are not too high. However, if policy costs are high or the environment is resource-poor (low mpcr), the efficiency gain may be small or even negative. The method of targeting intervention (e.g., size-based) can further moderate the effect. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG variants with spatial or regional structure and endogenous institutional punishment.

- source: Gao, L. Y., Pan, Q. H., & He, M. F. (2024). Impact of dynamic compensation with resource feedback on the common pool resource game. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that (1) static punishment (compensation) above a critical threshold leads to full cooperation, resource sustainability, and maximum group payoff; (2) dynamic punishment with resource feedback can promote cooperation and efficiency even when static punishment is insufficient, especially at low initial compensation intensity; (3) the relationship between punishment parameters and efficiency is non-monotonic and context-dependent, with oscillatory or convergent dynamics depending on parameter regions; (4) the proportion of fines used for ecological compensation (vs. other uses) should be tuned to maximize efficiency, with all fines used for compensation when feedback is high or initial intensity is high, and none when both are low.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that increasing punishment intensity (compensation) above a critical threshold sharply increases efficiency (group payoff) in common pool resource games. Dynamic, feedback-based punishment can further improve efficiency, especially when static punishment is weak. The results suggest that, for prediction, the presence and strength of punishment, as well as whether it is dynamically adjusted based on resource feedback, are key moderators of efficiency gains. The findings are directly relevant for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, especially for settings with resource feedback, variable punishment intensity, and allocation of fines to resource restoration.

- source: Cooney, D. B. (2025). Exploring the Evolution of Altruistic Punishment with a PDE Model of Cultural Multilevel Selection. *Bulletin of Mathematical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that altruistic punishment, when combined with group-level selection, can promote higher levels of cooperation and increase group efficiency compared to scenarios without punishment. However, the effect of punishment on efficiency is sensitive to the cost structure (per-interaction vs. fixed), the strength of punishment, and the form of group-level competition. In some models, increasing punishment strength or cost leads to higher cooperation but lower efficiency, due to the collective cost of punishment outweighing its benefits. The paper provides analytical expressions for the threshold group selection strength needed to sustain cooperation and for the steady-state average payoff, showing that these can depend non-monotonically on punishment parameters. The results highlight that the design of punishment mechanisms and the structure of group competition are critical moderators of the efficiency impact of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct, mechanistic, and quantitative guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments with group structure. It shows that the efficiency impact of punishment depends on the cost and strength of punishment, the type of cost (per-interaction or fixed), and the form of group-level competition. For prediction, the results suggest that: (1) punishment is more likely to increase efficiency when costs are per-interaction rather than fixed; (2) increasing punishment strength generally increases cooperation, but may decrease efficiency if costs are high; (3) the effect of punishment on efficiency can be non-monotonic, especially under group-level competition rules that normalize by maximum/minimum group payoffs; (4) the threshold group selection strength required for cooperation is lower with stronger or cheaper punishment. The paper's analytical formulas and numerical results can be used to estimate treatment efficiency from control efficiency and game design parameters, especially in settings with group-structured populations and multilevel selection.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., Cherki, B., & Israel, S. (2024). Cooperation, punishment, and group change in multilevel public goods experiments. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that peer punishment increases cooperation in standard (single-group) public goods games, sustaining high contribution levels and preventing decay. In multilevel public goods games (where participants can contribute to private, local, or global accounts), punishment only increases total and global contributions if participants previously played a single-group PGG. If the multilevel PGG is played first, punishment does not significantly increase cooperation or efficiency. The effect of punishment is thus moderated by the order in which group structures are experienced. Normative expectations and punishment patterns suggest that prior experience with single-group PGGs helps groups coordinate on efficient (global) cooperation norms in multilevel settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games depends on the structure of the game (single-group vs. multilevel) and the order in which participants experience these structures. For prediction, the presence of a multilevel structure and whether participants have prior experience with single-group PGGs are key moderators. In standard PGGs, punishment reliably increases efficiency. In multilevel PGGs, punishment only increases efficiency if participants have previously played a single-group PGG. The marginal per-capita return (mpcr), punishment cost, and punishment effectiveness are clearly specified, and the absence of communication and anonymity of punishment are also relevant. The findings suggest that, all else equal, enabling punishment will increase efficiency in single-group PGGs and in multilevel PGGs only if there is prior single-group experience.

- source: Amirova, I., Petrick, M., & Djanibekov, N. (2022). Investment traps and resilience to shocks: An experimental study of Central Asian collective water governance. *Irrigation and Drainage*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat
  findings: The experiment found multiple stable equilibria (lock-ins) in collective investment in irrigation infrastructure. Communication (chat) treatments increased collective investment and made high-investment equilibria more likely, but did not guarantee them. Penalty (punishment) treatments, both low and high, reduced collective investment compared to baseline, contrary to theoretical expectations. The penalty treatments crowded out intrinsic motivation to cooperate, resulting in lower investment levels and less resilience to shocks. The main outcome is collective investment, which is a behavioral proxy for group efficiency, but the paper does not report explicit group payoffs or efficiency ratios.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in a repeated public-good-like field experiment with real farmers, introducing punishment (penalties) can reduce collective investment and thus lower efficiency-related outcomes, compared to a baseline with no punishment. Communication (chat) increases investment, but does not guarantee high efficiency. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar field settings may decrease efficiency, especially when intrinsic motivation is important. The main outcome is collective investment, not explicit group payoff, but the relationship is close. The findings are most relevant for field settings with real-world participants and repeated games with investment as the public good.

- source: Molenmaker, W. E., Lelieveld, G. J., De Kwaadsteniet, E. W., & Van Dijk, E. (2022). Applying a logic of appropriateness to understand behavioral differences between common resource dilemmas and public good dilemmas. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The paper finds that, holding the underlying payoff structure constant, participants are more cooperative in CRDs than in PGDs. Punishment is used more frequently in CRDs, while reward is used more frequently in PGDs. These effects are robust to changes in the framing of the action (taking/leaving/giving/keeping). However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so it does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how the willingness to punish and cooperate differs between CRDs and PGDs, but it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. For the downstream prediction task (predicting efficiency with and without punishment), this paper does not provide direct evidence, but it does suggest that punishment is more likely to be used in CRDs and that cooperation is higher in CRDs. The game design dimensions are well described, so the paper is useful for understanding behavioral responses to punishment opportunities, but not for predicting efficiency effects.

- source: Maeda, K., Kumai, Y., & Hashimoto, H. (2022). Potential influence of decision time on punishment behavior and its evaluation. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The study finds that participants assign more punishment under time pressure (intuition) than after deliberation, especially in one-shot PGG and second-party punishment games. However, punishers are evaluated more positively when their punishment is deliberative rather than intuitive, and when they are third-party (not direct victims). The study does not report on group efficiency, total earnings, or welfare, so it does not directly inform how punishment affects efficiency in PGG-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in PGG or related games. It does, however, provide informative context about how punishment behavior and its social evaluation depend on decision time and the role of the punisher. For the prediction task, this paper suggests that the psychological and reputational consequences of punishment may depend on whether punishment is seen as deliberative or intuitive, and whether it is administered by a direct victim or a third party. However, since efficiency or payoff outcomes are not measured, the paper cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency given game design dimensions.

- source: Angelsen, A., & Naime, J. (2024). The mixed impacts of peer punishments on common-pool resources: Multi-country experimental evidence. *World Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Peer punishment in a CPR extraction game increases cooperation (reduces deforestation) when it is prosocial (targeting above-average extractors), but reduces cooperation when it is antisocial (targeting cooperators). The frequency and impact of punishment vary by country and by group endowment inequality, with Indonesia showing much higher punishment rates and stronger norm enforcement. The experiment does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but shows that punishment changes the marginal incentives for extraction and that prosocial punishment can make deviation from the group norm unprofitable. Antisocial punishment, however, undermines cooperation. The findings highlight the importance of cultural and institutional context for the effectiveness of peer punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the effect of peer punishment on cooperation in CPR-like games depends on the type of punishment (prosocial vs. antisocial), the cultural context, and group heterogeneity. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, it shows that prosocial punishment increases cooperation and reduces extraction, which would be expected to increase efficiency in a standard PGG/CPR context. However, antisocial punishment can reduce cooperation and thus efficiency. The results suggest that the effectiveness of punishment for increasing efficiency is context-dependent and can be undermined by antisocial punishment. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment can improve efficiency when prosocial, but that the presence of antisocial punishment and cultural context are important moderators. The lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits quantitative prediction.

- source: Chavez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2025). Social context, framing, and compliance with the law: experimental evidence. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that higher monitoring probabilities increase compliance in all treatments. Law framing increases compliance only in the strategic (public good) environment, and actually decreases compliance in the non-strategic environment where the law does not benefit others. Compliance is not higher when noncompliance imposes an externality on third parties. Compliance is higher in public good (strategic) environments than in non-strategic ones, especially when monitoring is weak. The main outcome is compliance, not efficiency or group payoff, so the results are most directly informative about behavioral responses to punishment rather than efficiency effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about how punishment (monitoring and sanctions) affects compliance rates in public goods game-like environments, especially under different social framings. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design and results are highly relevant for predicting compliance behavior, and the strategic (public good) treatments are close analogs to standard PGGs with punishment. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective at increasing compliance (and thus likely efficiency) when the public good benefits group members, especially under weak enforcement. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Shuvo, M. F. R., & Kabir, K. M. A. (2024). Exploring cyclic dominance in rock-paper-scissor strategies within the Optional Prisoner's Dilemma: A quantitative analysis with environmental feedback. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Punishment increases cooperation in one-shot, three-person PD games, both when imposed exogenously (costless) and endogenously (costly to the punisher). The effect of punishment is decomposed into a direct effect (altering incentives for the punished individual) and an indirect effect (raising expectations that others will cooperate, thus encouraging conditional cooperators). Both effects are of similar magnitude. In the endogenous punishment condition, the cost of punishment is small relative to the benefit from increased cooperation, resulting in a net positive benefit. However, the main outcome is contribution, not group efficiency or total payoff, though some calculations of net benefit are provided.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment (even when costly to the punisher) increases cooperation in public-goods-like environments, and that the effect is due to both direct and indirect mechanisms. While efficiency (as defined by group payoff relative to maximum possible) is not directly reported, the paper's calculations of net benefit and cost-effectiveness suggest that punishment increases group welfare. The design is a one-shot, three-player, continuous-contribution PD with and without punishment, and with both exogenous and endogenous punishment. For prediction tasks, the findings support a more positive effect of punishment on efficiency, especially when both direct and indirect effects are present, and when punishment is not prohibitively costly. However, the lack of direct efficiency reporting means that predictions must be inferred from contribution and net benefit data.

- source: Yan, R. Y., Xu, M. Q., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Evolution of cooperation under pure institutional reward and punishment in collective-risk social dilemma games. *Physics Letters A*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that introducing institutional pure punishment or pure reward into a collective-risk social dilemma game can promote cooperation, but with important differences. Pure reward leads to stable coexistence of cooperators and defectors, with the domain of coexistence expanding as reward intensity increases. Pure punishment produces more complex dynamics, including monostable, bistable, tristable, and quadristable outcomes, and can more effectively suppress free-riding when punishment intensity is high or the implementation threshold is low. In finite populations, lower thresholds for implementing incentives (reward or punishment) promote cooperation, while higher thresholds favor defection. The cost and intensity of incentives modulate these effects. However, the paper does not directly report group efficiency or total payoff; instead, it focuses on the evolutionary stability and prevalence of cooperative strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for predicting the effect of institutional punishment on cooperation in collective-risk public goods games, especially regarding how punishment intensity, cost, and implementation threshold affect the prevalence of cooperation. However, it does not provide direct evidence on group efficiency or total payoff, so its value for predicting efficiency outcomes is limited to inferring that higher cooperation rates (under certain punishment regimes) are likely to be associated with higher efficiency, assuming the mapping from cooperation to efficiency is known. The results are most relevant for settings with institutional (not peer) punishment, nonlinear threshold public goods, and no direct cost of cooperation for punishers/rewarders. The findings suggest that lower punishment thresholds and higher punishment intensity (relative to cost) are more likely to promote cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency if cooperation is strongly linked to group payoff.

- source: Zhang, W., Zhao, D. K., Jin, X., Zhang, H. Z., An, T. B., Cui, G. H., & Wang, Z. (2025). Q-learning facilitates norm emergence in metanorm game model with topological structures. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a metanorm game with punishment and metapunishment, agents using self-regarding Q-learning can achieve high and robust cooperation rates (over 90% in many cases), even when agents lack information about others' payoffs. Punishment and metapunishment are essential for maintaining cooperation and suppressing defection and non-punishment. The model is robust to changes in network topology (scale-free, random, small-world), temptation payoff, population size, and observability. Highly connected agents are more likely to adopt punitive strategies. However, the paper does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this work.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that punishment and metapunishment mechanisms can sustain high cooperation rates in social dilemma environments with local interactions and limited information, especially when agents use reinforcement learning. However, because the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for understanding the behavioral and dynamic effects of punishment and metapunishment, and for anticipating that high cooperation is possible under a wide range of design parameters, but not for quantitative efficiency prediction.

- source: Kido, Y., & Takezawa, M. (2024). Coevolution of norm psychology and cooperation through exapted conformity. *Evolutionary Human Sciences*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can evolve in collective action dilemmas when injunctive norms strongly encourage punishment, but not when they only encourage cooperation. Conformity (descriptive norm psychology) alone can support intermediate cooperation, but is unstable and easily undermined. When conformity is exapted (pre-existing), it can expand the conditions under which punishment-based cooperation evolves, acting as a scaffold for the emergence of stable, high-cooperation societies. The model identifies two stable types of cooperative societies: those based on conformity (unstable, lower cooperation) and those based on punishment (stable, higher cooperation). However, all outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation and punishment rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical support for the importance of punishment norms (and the cost/benefit structure of punishment) in enabling high cooperation in public-goods-like environments, especially when conformity is present. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment is more effective than conformity at sustaining high cooperation, and that the presence of conformity can expand the parameter space where punishment-based cooperation emerges. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment mechanisms (especially when normatively encouraged) are likely to increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative efficiency outcomes.

- source: Yang, H. B., Zhang, Y. K., Lyu, Y. R., & Tang, C. M. (2023). Group bias under uncertain environment: A perspective of third-party punishment. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment is harsher under environmental uncertainty than under certainty. Out-group members are punished more than in-group members (out-group derogation), and in-group favoritism is observed when violations are clear. The amount of punishment increases with the severity of the harvest (i.e., the more a player takes, the more they are punished). Gender of the third-party also moderates punishment patterns.
