However, the study does not report on group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how punishment behavior is affected by environmental uncertainty, group affiliation, and gender in a CRD setting similar to a public goods game. However, because it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is useful for understanding behavioral mechanisms and moderators of punishment, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Qian, J., Sun, X., Zhang, T. D., & Chai, Y. T. (2022). Authority or Autonomy? Exploring Interactions between Central and Peer Punishments in Risk-Resistant Scenarios. *Entropy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that both central and peer punishment can promote group collaboration in risk-resistant (threshold) scenarios, with peer punishment generally having a stronger effect, especially as risk increases. Central punishment can suppress the effect of peer punishment at all risk levels, while peer punishment only constrains central punishment at high risk. Resource wastage from punishment is generally small compared to the loss from group failure, but increases with peer punishment intensity and risk. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff in the standard sense, focusing instead on collaboration rates and resource wastage.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It supports the idea that punishment (both central and peer) increases group collaboration in threshold public goods-like environments, and that the effectiveness and interaction of punishment types depend on risk level. However, because the main outcome is collaboration rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the model is theoretical/simulation-based rather than empirical, its direct predictive value for efficiency is limited. The resource wastage analysis suggests that high peer punishment can lead to diminishing returns or inefficiency at high risk. The model's all-or-nothing threshold structure and explicit manipulation of punishment cost and effectiveness are informative for mapping to game design dimensions.

- source: Chica, M., & Santos, F. C. (2023). Seeding leading cooperators and institutions in networked climate dilemmas. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in networked collective-risk dilemmas with local punishment institutions, seeding a small fraction of punishers (institution supporters) at random or in network hubs can shift the system from low to high group achievement and institution prevalence, even when initial conditions are otherwise unchanged. Seeding cooperators is much less effective. The presence of punishment institutions is necessary for high cooperation; network structure alone does not suffice. Seeding network hubs is more expensive but not more effective than random seeding. The main outcomes are group achievement and institution prevalence, not direct efficiency or payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that introducing punishment institutions (via seeding punishers) in a networked public-goods-like environment can strongly increase group-level cooperation outcomes (group achievement and institution prevalence), even when the control (no punishment) condition yields low cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, its findings should be used as informative but indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment. The results suggest that, in similar networked games, enabling punishment (especially via institutional mechanisms) is likely to increase efficiency if group achievement is a good proxy for efficiency. The paper also highlights that the effectiveness of punishment depends on both network structure and the presence of institutional mechanisms, and that seeding punishers is more effective than seeding cooperators.

- source: García, J., & Traulsen, A. (2025). Picking strategies in games of cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that the structure of the strategy set and the microeconomic assumptions in evolutionary models of cooperation (including public goods games with punishment) can have a large impact on the predicted prevalence of cooperation. For example, allowing for antisocial punishment or for loners to be punished can lead to the collapse of cooperation, while more restrictive strategy sets can support high levels of cooperation. The paper emphasizes that these modeling choices can bias results and that more systematic, unbiased inclusion of strategies is needed for robust predictions. However, the paper does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on the abundance of cooperation and the evolutionary stability of different strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides important theoretical caution for interpreting the effects of punishment in public goods games: the predicted effect of punishment on cooperation (and by extension, efficiency) can depend strongly on the details of the strategy set and microeconomic assumptions. If a model or experiment restricts who can punish or be punished, or omits antisocial punishment, it may overestimate the positive effect of punishment on cooperation. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it does not provide direct quantitative support for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of modeling assumptions and the potential for qualitative shifts in cooperation prevalence, which may translate to efficiency in some settings.

- source: Itao, K., & Kaneko, K. (2025). Self-organized institutions in evolutionary dynamical-systems games. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a minimal two-player harvesting game with environmental feedback, the evolution of decision-making functions leads to the self-organization of social institutions (shared norms and punishment rules). Players evolve to monitor both their own and their opponent's states, and punish selfish overharvesting by retaliatory overharvesting, which degrades the environment and reduces both players' payoffs. This punitive mechanism stabilizes cooperation and leads to the emergence of efficient, periodic harvesting patterns (modes) that maximize group fitness. The model shows that the most efficient (highest fitness) cooperative modes are only evolutionarily stable if players' strategies are sufficiently plastic to punish defectors. The results are robust across a range of parameter values. The paper provides a formal framework for understanding how punishment and norm evolution can sustain high efficiency in common-pool resource settings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling punishment (here, context-dependent punitive overharvesting) in a repeated common-pool resource game will increase group efficiency, provided that players can monitor each other's states or actions. The model shows that the emergence of punishment mechanisms is necessary for the stability of efficient cooperative outcomes, and that the efficiency gains are robust to parameter changes. The findings are most directly applicable to two-player, repeated, binary-action resource games with environmental feedback, but the underlying principles are likely to generalize to other public goods or common-pool resource games with similar structure. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the presence of punishment, monitoring, and plasticity in strategies are key moderators of efficiency gains from punishment.

- source: Wang, L. C., Liu, Y. Y., Guo, R. Q., Zhang, L., Liu, L. J., & Hua, S. J. (2024). Cooperation and resource sustainability in coupling social-ecological systems with dynamic growth rates. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_other_summaries, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study finds that a peer rating-based heterogeneous distribution system (PRHDS) can promote cooperation in a structured population, but is fragile to random exploration (strategy noise). The main threat is the invasion of egoistic cooperators, which undermines prosocial clusters. Introducing monetary punishments for minority raters (either as a fixed fine or as a reduction in distribution share) can restore and stabilize cooperation, making the system robust to low levels of noise. Non-monetary punishment (reducing the voice of minority raters) is ineffective. The effect of the system depends on the matching of the public goods multiplier and the weight of ratings in the distribution formula. The model is all-or-nothing in both contribution and rating, and the main reported outcomes are behavioral (cooperation rates, strategy fractions), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence for the prediction task. It models a close variant of the public goods game with a peer rating-based distribution system, which acts as a combined reward/punishment mechanism. The main outcomes are behavioral, not efficiency or group payoff, so direct prediction of efficiency is not possible. However, the results suggest that monetary punishment mechanisms (with explicit costs or share reductions) can stabilize cooperation in noisy environments, while non-monetary punishments are ineffective. The model is all-or-nothing, with no chat, and the marginal per-capita return (mpcr) is a key parameter. The findings suggest that, in similar designs, enabling monetary punishment is likely to increase cooperation rates (and thus, by implication, efficiency), especially when the system is subject to noise. The absence of direct efficiency outcomes limits the strength of inference for the prediction task.

- source: Pedrazzini, S., Gurtner, L. M., Aggrey, V., & Moser, S. (2025). Exploring individual and organizational factors influencing cooperation in commons: a scoping review. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: chat, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The review finds that both individual (e.g., social identification, trust, values) and organizational (e.g., incentives, communication, social norms, anonymity) factors significantly influence cooperation in commons and social dilemma settings. Regarding punishment, meta-analyses and experimental studies included in the review show that punishment systems reliably increase cooperation rates, but may reduce group earnings or efficiency due to the cost of punishment, especially in early rounds. Reward systems also increase cooperation and tend to result in higher group payoffs than punishment. The review notes that the effectiveness of punishment depends on factors such as cost, group homogeneity, information accuracy, and cultural context. However, the review does not provide quantitative estimates or systematic synthesis of efficiency or group payoff outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation as the main dependent variable.
  prediction_guidance: This review is useful for identifying which game design dimensions are likely to moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation, such as the presence and cost of punishment, group size, communication, and information structure. It provides strong evidence that punishment increases cooperation, but only indirect and mixed evidence about its effect on efficiency or group payoff, with some included studies noting that punishment can reduce group earnings due to its cost. For the downstream prediction task, this review suggests that enabling punishment will likely increase cooperation, but the effect on efficiency will depend on the costliness of punishment, the possibility of antisocial punishment, and whether rewards are also available. The review does not provide direct quantitative evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2024). Social norm dynamics and cooperation in changing groups. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=close | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper finds that group changes, such as the entry of newcomers, tend to reduce cooperation in the short term because newcomers contribute less than incumbents. Over time, newcomers adapt to the incumbents' norms and increase their cooperation. Punishment mechanisms can induce newcomers to conform to group norms, but the review does not report on efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The evidence is based on both observational data from an online game (Ikariam) and lab experiments with public goods games and peer punishment. The review emphasizes the need for more causal and longitudinal research, especially on the role of normative differences and the potential for two-way adaptation between newcomers and incumbents.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by summarizing how group composition changes and punishment mechanisms affect cooperation and norm conformity in public goods game-like environments. However, it does not provide direct evidence on efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so its value for predicting treatment efficiency is limited. The review suggests that punishment can help align newcomer behavior with group norms, which may support higher cooperation, but the lack of payoff data means that efficiency effects must be inferred indirectly from behavioral outcomes. The paper is most useful for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment and group dynamics interact, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Cox, C. A., & Stoddard, B. (2024). Inequality and the allocation of collective goods. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The experiment compares standard linear public goods games (equal-share) to treatments where a third-party allocator divides the collective good among contributors. The allocator mechanism increases efficiency in all cases, but the effect is strongest in homogeneous groups. When group members differ in endowments or productivity, the allocator's ability to boost efficiency is significantly diminished, though still positive relative to the equal-share baseline. Allocators generally reward contributors in proportion to their relative effort (percentage of endowment contributed), but in heterogeneous groups, conflicting fairness ideals make it harder to sustain high efficiency. The study provides direct evidence that flexible allocation mechanisms can substitute for punishment/reward institutions in promoting efficiency, but their effectiveness is moderated by group heterogeneity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that mechanisms allowing for flexible allocation of the collective good (by a third-party allocator) can substantially increase efficiency in public-goods-like environments, especially when groups are homogeneous. However, the efficiency boost is reduced in the presence of heterogeneity in endowments or productivity. For prediction tasks, the presence of an allocator (as a form of centralized sanctioning/reward) should be expected to increase efficiency relative to equal-share baselines, but the magnitude of the effect will be smaller in heterogeneous groups. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with allocation mechanisms, and indirectly relevant for understanding the limits of punishment/reward institutions in heterogeneous environments.

- source: Chiba-Okabe, H., & Plotkin, J. B. (2024). Can institutions foster cooperation by wealth redistribution?. *Journal of the Royal Society Interface*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper finds that: (1) A prosocial institution (maximizing average population payoff) can increase cooperation and efficiency, but only if it is sufficiently forward-looking (low discount rate); if it is myopic, it fails to intervene and the outcome is the same as anarchy. (2) A pro-cooperator institution (maximizing cooperator payoff) always increases cooperation and efficiency above anarchy, even if it is myopic. (3) A selfish institution (maximizing its own revenue) can sometimes increase efficiency above anarchy if it is forward-looking, but if it is myopic, it exploits the population and reduces efficiency below anarchy. The model provides explicit analytical conditions for when each regime leads to higher or lower efficiency than the no-institution baseline.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of institutional interventions (taxation and redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like environments. It shows that the effect of 'punishment' or institutional intervention is not universally positive or negative for efficiency; rather, it depends on the institution's objective and its ability to value future outcomes (discount rate). For prediction tasks, the model suggests that institutional efficiency (analogous to punishment/reward effectiveness), the institution's foresight, and the alignment of institutional objectives with group welfare are critical moderators. The results are most directly applicable to settings where the 'punishment' is implemented as a tax-and-redistribute mechanism, rather than direct costly punishment between individuals.

- source: Herne, K., Kuyper, J., & Lappalainen, O. (2023). The Mode of Communication as a Driver of Sustainable and Equitable Asymmetric Common Pool Resource Use. *Environmental Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that both unstructured and structured (deliberative) communication robustly increase group contributions and the efficiency of resource generation in an asymmetric common pool resource game, compared to a no-communication baseline. Deliberation (structured communication) reduces the impact of player position on contributions, leading to more equitable participation, but does not significantly increase total efficiency or equality of extraction compared to unstructured communication. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not provide direct evidence on the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that communication—both unstructured and structured—substantially increases efficiency in asymmetric common pool resource games relative to a no-communication baseline. However, it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, so it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment. The design and results are highly relevant for understanding the effects of communication and deliberation, but not for punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, though it does establish a high-efficiency baseline for games with communication.

- source: Kurokawa, S. (2023). Evolution of spite in an n-player game with an opting-out option. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that in repeated n-player games with an opting-out option, spiteful (costly harming) strategies can sometimes persist, but only under restrictive conditions: low cost-to-harm ratio, small group size, and specific opting-out rules. As group size increases, it becomes harder for spiteful strategies to achieve higher payoffs than nonspiteful strategies, and the basin of attraction for all-nonspiteful populations grows. The opting-out rule (how easily groups disband) is a key moderator: when homogeneous groups (all spiteful or all nonspiteful) are forced to disband, spite can persist more easily, but this is a somewhat artificial rule. The model is mathematically explicit about when spiteful strategies can dominate, and these conditions almost always correspond to lower group efficiency compared to all-nonspiteful populations. The analysis is directly about payoff and efficiency, not just behavior.
  prediction_guidance: For the downstream prediction task, this paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like n-player games with an opting-out mechanism, the introduction of costly punishment (here, spiteful harming) generally reduces group efficiency unless the cost-to-harm ratio is extremely low and the group size is small. As group size increases, the conditions for punishment/spite to persist become more restrictive, and group efficiency is more likely to be maximized when punishment/spite is absent. The details of the opting-out rule (e.g., whether homogeneous groups are forced to disband) can moderate this effect, but in most realistic settings, punishment/spite is not efficiency-enhancing. The model is not an exact PGG with standard punishment, but the payoff structure and evolutionary logic are closely related, and the results are directly about payoff/efficiency. Thus, the paper supports the prediction that enabling punishment/spite in large groups with standard cost structures will not increase efficiency, and may reduce it.

- source: Fontanari, J. F., & Santos, M. (2024). Solving the prisoner's dilemma trap in Hamilton's model of temporarily formed random groups. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper finds that in the 'others-only' n-person prisoner's dilemma (a strong altruism scenario), cooperation cannot evolve under asexual reproduction or sexual reproduction with selfing, but can evolve and become stable under biparental sexual reproduction if the group benefit multiplier (r) is sufficiently large relative to group size (n) and the cost of cooperation (c) is not too high. The model does not include punishment or reward, and the main mechanism enabling cooperation is the genetic structure imposed by biparental reproduction. The main outcome is the probability that the population reaches the all-cooperator state (fixation probability), which is a direct measure of group efficiency or welfare. The results are summarized in phase diagrams showing regions where cooperation is stable, bistable, or unstable, as a function of c, r, and n. The findings are robust to demographic noise and extend to dioecious populations. The paper does not provide evidence about the effects of punishment, communication, or other institutional design features.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is highly informative for predicting efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments that lack punishment or reward mechanisms, especially when the game structure is the 'others-only' variant of the n-person prisoner's dilemma. It shows that, in the absence of punishment, efficiency (measured as fixation of cooperation and group payoff) is only achievable under specific genetic and demographic conditions—namely, biparental sexual reproduction, sufficiently high group benefit multiplier (r), and not-too-large group size (n) or cost (c). The results suggest that, without punishment, efficiency is generally low unless these conditions are met. However, the paper does not provide any evidence about the effect of enabling punishment or sanctions, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the efficiency difference between control and punishment-enabled conditions. Its main value is in establishing a baseline: in the absence of punishment, efficiency is generally low except under the specified genetic/demographic conditions. For prediction tasks involving punishment, this paper is only indirectly relevant, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment.

- source: Sugaya, T., & Wolitzky, A. (2023). Bad apples in symmetric repeated games. *Theoretical Economics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in large repeated public goods or similar games, the possibility of sustaining cooperation and high efficiency depends critically on the ability to punish defectors specifically. If the only available incentives are rewards or withdrawal of provision (i.e., voluntary mechanisms), then cooperation collapses and efficiency is low when there are even a few commitment types (bad apples) who always defect. Involuntary punishment (such as fines) that can be targeted at defectors restores the possibility of cooperation and high efficiency. The main technical result is that if a 'population dominant' action exists (i.e., defecting yields a higher payoff than the population average, regardless of others' actions), then equilibrium efficiency converges to the inefficient outcome as the population grows. If no such action exists, and punishment can be targeted, then high efficiency is possible. The results are general and apply to a wide class of symmetric repeated games, including public goods games with or without transfers.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical guidance for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods and related games. It implies that enabling targeted, involuntary punishment (e.g., fines for non-contributors) is necessary for sustaining high efficiency in large groups with possible commitment types. If only rewards or voluntary mechanisms are available, efficiency will be low in the presence of even a small fraction of defectors. The paper's results are most directly applicable to settings where the game is symmetric, the population is large, and there is a possibility of commitment types. For the prediction task, the presence or absence of targeted punishment (punishment_tech), the nature of the incentive (reward_exists, punishment_cost), and the ability to distinguish defectors are critical moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) will be low in large groups with commitment types unless involuntary punishment is available; enabling such punishment can dramatically increase efficiency.

- source: Tverskoi, D., Ember, C. R., Gelfand, M. J., Jones, E. C., Skoggard, I., Toutee, L., & Gavrilets, S. (2024). Cultural tightness and resilience against environmental shocks in nonindustrial societies. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that group efficiency (average payoff) and cooperation are maximized at intermediate levels of cultural tightness when facing severe environmental shocks. Loose cultures (low tightness) are more resilient and efficient under mild, slow-onset shocks, while very tight cultures often fail to adapt to new shocks and experience lower efficiency. However, very tight cultures can maintain high efficiency in the face of regular, severe shocks if cooperative norms are already established. Increasing group size undermines cooperation and efficiency unless benefit parameters scale accordingly. Synergistic effects between different types of collective action (public goods) enhance both cooperation and efficiency. The model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning, but norm compliance is enforced via conformity in the utility function.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in public-goods-like environments with norm-based conformity (but no explicit punishment), efficiency is maximized at intermediate levels of norm strength (tightness) under severe shocks, and at low tightness under mild shocks. The absence of explicit punishment means the results are not directly transferable to settings where punishment is enabled or disabled, but the findings are highly informative for predicting efficiency as a function of group size, benefit structure, and norm strength. The model's structure and results are most relevant for public goods games with strong norm compliance or conformity-based incentives, and less so for games where punishment is the main enforcement mechanism. The model also shows that increasing group size reduces efficiency unless benefits scale up, and that synergy between collective actions increases the range of conditions for high efficiency.

- source: Buso, I. M., Di Cagno, D., Gueth, W., & Spadoni, L. (2025). Voluntary Partnerships for Equally Sharing Contribution Costs. *B E Journal of Theoretical Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Voluntary cost-sharing partnerships in a PGG-like environment significantly increase average contributions and prevent the typical decay of cooperation over repeated rounds. Partners contribute more than outsiders, and the presence of the partnership mechanism (whether endogenous or exogenous) leads to higher group contributions and payoffs than the standard PGG. The effect is strongest for larger partnerships. The mechanism does not involve punishment or reward, but rather a self-commitment to cost-sharing. Efficiency (in terms of group payoff relative to the social optimum) is not directly reported, but the data show that average contributions are about 50% of the endowment with partnerships, compared to about 32% in the standard PGG. The voluntary nature of partnership formation has a modest additional effect, especially for small partnerships.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that institutional mechanisms allowing voluntary cost-sharing partnerships can substantially increase group contributions and payoffs in PGG-like settings, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward. For prediction tasks, the presence of such a partnership mechanism should be expected to yield higher efficiency than standard PGGs with similar MPCR and group size. The effect is robust to whether partnership formation is endogenous or exogenous, except for very small partnerships. The findings are most directly relevant for settings where cost-sharing or coalition formation is possible, but do not directly inform about punishment effects. The results suggest that, in the absence of punishment, other institutional mechanisms can also enhance efficiency, and that the design dimensions (player count, MPCR, endogenous/exogenous group formation) interact with the mechanism's effectiveness.

- source: Harrell, A., & Traeger, M. L. (2025). Reputation-based reciprocity in human-bot and human-human networks. *Pnas Nexus*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists
  findings: The presence of bots in indirect reciprocity networks reduces human generosity, even when bots are programmed to be strong reciprocators. This reduction is driven by humans' reluctance to help bots, due to weaker norms about bots deserving help, not by a lack of reputational concern. Both 'reputational giving' (giving when observed by a third party) and 'rewarding reputations' (helping those with a reputation for helping) are less effective when bots are present. The paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but the reduction in giving implies lower group welfare in hybrid networks compared to human-only networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the presence of non-human agents (bots) in indirect reciprocity games reduces generosity and, by implication, group welfare. However, it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or payoff outcomes directly. For prediction tasks about punishment effects on efficiency, this paper is only adjacent: it suggests that social context (e.g., presence of bots) can moderate the effectiveness of reciprocity-based mechanisms, but does not provide direct evidence about punishment or efficiency. The design and findings may be informative for understanding how social norms and recipient identity affect cooperation, but not for quantitative prediction of punishment effects.

- source: Tan, J. H. W., & Bolle, F. (2023). Intragroup punishment and intergroup conflict aversion weaken intragroup cooperation in finitely repeated games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that intragroup cooperation is sensitive to the presence of intergroup rivalry and the payoff structure. Cooperation is lower when it inflicts losses on rivals, even when socially efficient, and higher when synergy within the group is increased. Participants use dynamic strategies, such as punishing partner defection with unyielding defection, but do not respond similarly to rival actions. Outgroup altruism (conflict aversion) further reduces cooperation that would harm rivals. Cooperation rates decline over time due to these mechanisms. The study does not include a costly punishment stage or analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of enabling costly punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The 'punishment' discussed is behavioral (trigger strategies, unyielding defection) rather than a costly punishment mechanism. The findings are relevant for understanding how dynamic strategies and group-specific preferences affect cooperation, but not for predicting efficiency changes due to the introduction of a punishment option. The game structure is close to a PGG, but the absence of a costly punishment treatment and lack of efficiency or payoff outcomes limit its direct applicability to the prediction task.

- source: Mitterbacher, K., Palan, S., & Fleiss, J. (2024). Intergroup cooperation in the lab: asymmetric power relations and redistributive policies. *Empirica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in an asymmetric intergroup setting, the threat or implementation of discriminatory redistribution (a form of institutionalized sanction) reduces cooperation by the disadvantaged group (migrants). When citizens choose equal sharing, migrants are more likely to contribute, and when migrants contribute, citizens are less likely to vote for discrimination. This reciprocity can lead to mutually beneficial cooperation, but discrimination or lack of cooperation can trigger downward spirals. The mere possibility of discrimination reduces cooperation compared to a baseline where only equal sharing is possible. The main outcomes are behavioral (contribution and voting rates), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment-like mechanisms (here, discriminatory redistribution) on efficiency in public-goods-like games with asymmetric power. The findings suggest that institutionalized sanctions (discriminatory redistribution) can reduce cooperation and thus would likely reduce efficiency, especially when the disadvantaged group perceives the system as unfair. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used for direct quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes. The design and behavioral results are informative for understanding the mechanisms by which punishment or sanctions may backfire in asymmetric settings, but the lack of payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

- source: Chen, W., Zhu, Q. L., & Wu, T. (2023). Unfairness promotes the evolution of cooperation. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reject unfair resource allocations (interpreted as a form of punishment) leads to higher levels of cooperation, especially when moderate unfairness is present. Both extreme fairness and extreme unfairness are suboptimal for cooperation. The mechanism is explained by cyclic dominance among strategy types. In continuous strategy settings, cooperators tend to act fairer than defectors, and clusters of cooperators can resist exploitation. The model is theoretical and simulation-based, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff, but rather focuses on the fraction of cooperators and the evolution of fairness/unfairness strategies.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that the ability to reject unfair offers (a form of costly punishment) can promote cooperation in public-goods-like games, especially when resource allocation is neither perfectly fair nor extremely unfair. However, because the main outcomes are cooperation rates and not efficiency or group payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also theoretical and does not manipulate or report most of the game design dimensions used in the prediction task. Thus, while the paper supports the general idea that punishment for unfairness can promote cooperation, it does not provide direct quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in empirical PGG experiments.

- source: Catola, M., D'Alessandro, S., Guarnieri, P., & Pizziol, V. (2025). Norms and Efficiency in a Multi-Group Society: An Online Experiment. *Journal of Public Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in a one-shot, online multilevel public goods game, personal normative beliefs are the main driver of contribution decisions, more so than empirical or normative expectations (social norms). Both personal and social norms are responsive to changes in the relative efficiency of the local and global public goods: as the efficiency of the global public good increases, contributions to it rise and personal norms become more aligned with contribution levels. There are also cross-norm effects, where empirical expectations about one public good negatively correlate with contributions to the other. The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. However, it is informative about how changes in the marginal per capita return (mpcr) affect contribution behavior and the role of personal and social norms in such settings. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper is not directly relevant, but it may provide useful context for understanding baseline behavior in multilevel public goods games without punishment.

- source: Lo Iacono, S., Sonmez, B., & Fairbrother, M. (2024). The effect of trusting contexts in social dilemmas with collective and individual solutions. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: Groups in high-trust contexts are more likely to coordinate on the collective solution, resulting in higher efficiency and less resource waste. In low-trust contexts, participants invest more in individual solutions, leading to lower efficiency and more waste. Free-riding rates are similar across trust contexts. The presence of both individual and collective solutions introduces coordination challenges and inefficiency, especially in low-trust settings. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. It is informative about how trust context affects efficiency and coordination in a threshold public goods game with individual fallback, but since no punishment or sanctioning is present, it does not inform the prediction of efficiency changes due to enabling punishment. The design and efficiency results may be useful as a control or baseline for similar games, but not for estimating the marginal effect of punishment.

- source: Mori, R., Hanaki, N., & Kameda, T. (2024). An outside individual option increases optimism and facilitates collaboration when groups form flexibly. *Nature Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that allowing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) in a threshold public goods game increases the efficiency (average payoff) and group success rate compared to mandatory participation, particularly when group formation is flexible and loners do not affect group outcomes. The mechanisms are (1) self-selection of optimistic individuals into groups and (2) belief updating that increases cooperation among those who stay. The positive effect on efficiency is strongest when the baseline (mandatory) group success rate is not already near ceiling. The effect reverses if loners' externality is high (i.e., if loners still affect group outcomes, as in fixed group boundaries). No punishment or reward mechanisms are present in the design.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in threshold public goods games with flexible group formation and no punishment or reward mechanisms, introducing an outside individual option (voluntary participation) increases efficiency and group success, except when the baseline success rate is already very high. The effect is mediated by self-selection and belief updating. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games with and without voluntary participation, but not for games with punishment. The absence of punishment or reward means the results are not directly transferable to settings where those mechanisms are present, but the paper is highly informative for the effect of voluntary participation and group boundary flexibility.

- source: Brown, C. L. (2024). Team production in endogenous networks. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that in a public-goods-like team production environment with endogenous network (team) formation, most efficiency loss is due to failures in the team formation stage, not the contribution stage. When both synergy heterogeneity and high link costs are present, teams are smaller, less productive, and network efficiency loss is highest. Contribution levels within teams are generally close to equilibrium predictions, and contribution inefficiency is relatively minor compared to network inefficiency. The absence of punishment or sanctioning means the results speak to the baseline efficiency of endogenous team formation, not to the effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on efficiency outcomes in a public-goods-like environment with endogenous team formation, but it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Therefore, it is not directly informative for predicting the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency. However, it does provide a strong baseline for efficiency in the absence of punishment, especially in settings where team formation is endogenous and costly. For prediction tasks, this paper can inform the expected efficiency of the control (no-punishment) condition in similar networked public goods games, but cannot inform the treatment effect of punishment.

- source: Janssen, M. A., DeCaro, D., & Lee, A. (2022). An Agent-Based Model of the Interaction between Inequality, Trust, and Communication in Common Pool Experiments. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial common-pool resource game with four players and no punishment or sanctioning, communication (chat) between rounds leads to a substantial increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. Trust is modeled as a key mediator: communication increases trust, which reduces overharvesting and increases group earnings. The agent-based model, calibrated to experimental data, shows that a population with a majority of conditional cooperators (who respond to trust and inequality) best explains observed group outcomes. The model and data both show that communication leads to higher group payoffs and more sustainable resource use, even after communication ceases, due to persistent increases in trust. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present or analyzed.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in the absence of punishment or reward mechanisms, enabling communication (chat) in a common-pool resource game with four players and nine rounds leads to a large increase in group efficiency and resource sustainability. The findings are directly relevant for predicting the effect of chat/communication on efficiency, but not for predicting the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning is present in the design. The model and empirical results suggest that communication alone can drive high efficiency via trust, but do not inform the marginal effect of adding punishment to such a game. For prediction tasks focused on punishment, this paper is not directly informative, but it is highly informative for the effect of communication and trust in similar environments.

- source: Szekely, A., Bruner, D., Steinmo, S., Todor, A., Volintiru, C., & Andrighetto, G. (2023). Preferences for honesty can support cooperation. *Journal of Behavioral Decision Making*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that individual honesty preferences, measured via incentivized and self-report tasks, are positively associated with higher contributions in a public goods game with asymmetric information. This association is stronger and more consistent in the Honesty Frame (where the language cues honesty) than in the standard Contribution Frame. However, the framing manipulation does not lead to a significant difference in average contributions between treatments. The study does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present and efficiency is not measured. However, it provides informative context about the role of honesty preferences and framing in influencing contribution behavior in asymmetric information public goods games. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is not directly informative, but it may be useful for understanding baseline contribution behavior in the absence of punishment.

- source: Ando, H., Nishi, A., & Handcock, M. S. (2025). Statistical modelling of networked evolutionary public goods games. *Journal of the Royal Statistical Society Series A-statistics in Society*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that participants preferentially form and maintain network ties with others who also cooperate, and avoid forming ties that would close triangles (i.e., transitive closure is disfavored in tie formation). Participants also prefer to connect with others of similar wealth. The modeling approach (STERGM) is validated for small, repeated networked public goods games. However, the experiment does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism, and does not analyze efficiency or group payoff as an outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present in the experimental design. It does, however, provide detailed information about a networked PGG variant with dynamic partner selection, which may be relevant for understanding baseline cooperation and network effects in the absence of punishment. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency outcomes, this paper offers only contextual or indirect value.

- source: Kirkland, K., Van Lange, P. A. M., Charalambous, N., Strauss, C., & Bastian, B. (2024). Beauty Builds Cooperation by Increasing Moral Concern for the Environment. *Environment and Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The studies show that when participants believe their extraction from a common pool will have negative consequences for the environment (e.g., less money to a tree preservation charity), they take fewer points, indicating more cooperative/conservation behavior. This effect is stronger when the environmental consequence is paired with a beautiful image of a tree. The effect is mediated by increased concern for tree preservation and reduced concern for personal financial gain. Manipulating the anthropomorphic qualities of trees (e.g., emphasizing mind-like traits) increases perceived mind but does not further increase cooperation. The studies do not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and do not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It does, however, provide informative evidence that non-monetary moral value and aesthetic framing can increase cooperative behavior in resource dilemma games similar to PGGs. Since no punishment or sanctioning is present, and no efficiency or group payoff is reported, the paper is not directly useful for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how non-monetary framing or environmental consequences can shift extraction behavior, but not for the core prediction task involving punishment.

- source: Ding, R., Wang, X. J., Zhao, J. H., Gu, C. L., & Chen, W. M. (2024). Evolutionary dynamics in spatial public goods games with environmental feedbacks. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study models a spatial public goods game with environmental feedback, where individuals can be cooperators (who pay a cost to improve local environmental quality) or defectors (who do not pay and degrade the environment). The model explores how the ability of cooperators to repair the environment (delta), the destructiveness of defectors (gamma), and the cost of cooperation (c) affect the prevalence of cooperation. Key findings include: (1) Cooperation can only dominate if cooperator repair ability slightly exceeds defector destructiveness; (2) Increasing cooperator repair ability beyond a threshold can paradoxically reduce cooperation due to defector invasions; (3) Both very low and very high defector destructiveness can undermine cooperation; (4) Increasing cooperation cost generally reduces cooperation, but at low defector destructiveness, higher cost can sometimes help cooperation by reducing isolated cooperators and making clusters more robust. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. It does, however, provide detailed theoretical insights into how environmental feedback, spatial structure, and cost parameters affect the evolution of cooperation. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative, but it may provide contextual understanding of how non-punishment mechanisms and environmental feedbacks shape cooperation dynamics in spatial PGG variants.

- source: Sotnik, G., Choporov, S., & Shannon, T. (2023). The Role of Social Identity in a Population's Adoption of Prosocial Common-Pool Behavior. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=close | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The model shows that social identity, especially when combined with high group commitment, expands the parameter space (pressure-synergy combinations) where prosocial common-pool behavior is fully adopted. At low population density, both high- and low-commitment social identity models increase adoption and speed to full adoption compared to the baseline. At high density, only high-commitment agents show a strong positive effect; low-commitment agents may even reduce adoption. The model does not include punishment, reward, or explicit payoff/efficiency outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral adoption rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding how social identity and group commitment might affect the adoption of prosocial behavior in PGG-like environments, but not for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The model does not manipulate or include punishment, nor does it report group payoff or efficiency. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Zong, J. C., de Jong, E., Qiu, J. Y., & Li, J. (2025). Socially appropriate intervention: A cross-country investigation of third-party norm enforcement. *China Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: This cross-country lab experiment uses a modified trust game with third-party punishment and reward. Third parties can spend up to 5 ECU per round to punish or reward the receiver, with a 1:1 cost-impact ratio. The experiment manipulates norm uncertainty by varying whether the third party is from the same country as the sender and receiver (same-country) or from a different country (cross-country). Third parties observe the sender's expectation about the receiver's return and use this as norm-relevant information. The main findings are: (1) third-party intervention (punishment/reward) is common and contingent on whether the receiver meets the sender's expectation; (2) both cooperation (transfers, returns) and efficiency (sender-receiver welfare, social welfare) are higher in the cross-country treatment; (3) third-party intervention is more responsive to norm-relevant information under higher norm uncertainty (cross-country); (4) the norm enforced by third parties shifts depending on context (trustworthiness in same-country, equal-sharing in cross-country). The presence and responsiveness of third-party punishment/reward increases group efficiency, especially when norm uncertainty is high.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling third-party punishment and reward in a trust-game-like environment increases efficiency (group welfare), especially when norm uncertainty is high (e.g., cross-group or cross-country settings). The effect is mediated by the responsiveness of third parties to norm-relevant information. The punishment mechanism here is costly (1:1 cost-impact), and the game is not a standard PGG but a trust game with continuous transfer and return choices. The findings suggest that, in environments with higher norm uncertainty or social salience, enabling punishment/reward can yield larger efficiency gains. The control (no punishment) condition is not directly present, but the same-country treatment with less responsive third-party intervention can serve as a lower bound. The results are most transferable to settings where third-party punishment is possible, norm cues are available, and the punishment/reward is not highly leveraged (1:1). For prediction, the presence of third-party punishment/reward, higher norm uncertainty, and norm-relevant information all increase the expected efficiency relative to control.

- source: Li, Y. Z., Luo, J., Niu, H., & Ye, H. (2023). When punishers might be loved: fourth-party choices and third-party punishment in a delegation game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: Across three lab experiments, the paper shows: (1) Helpers are more trusted and preferred as partners in trust games, but punishers are more likely to be offered higher amounts in ultimatum games due to their deterrence signal, resulting in higher actual payoffs for punishers. (2) In a delegation game, when the proposer's knowledge of the delegate's punishment history is made explicit, fourth-party players prefer to select punishers as their delegates, and this choice leads to higher expected payoffs for both the delegate and the principal. (3) The effect is contingent on the visibility of the punishment signal; when the proposer's information is limited, helpers are preferred. Thus, punishment increases efficiency/payoff in environments where its deterrence is visible and relevant.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in games adjacent to public goods games (third-party punishment, ultimatum, and delegation games), enabling punishment increases the efficiency (payoff) of punishers and those who select them as delegates, especially when the punishment signal is visible to others. The effect is context-dependent: punishment is less effective in trust-based settings but more effective in settings where deterrence is valuable and observable. For prediction tasks, the presence of a visible punishment mechanism, the structure of the game (ultimatum/delegation vs. trust), and the information available to other players are key moderators. Control efficiency (without punishment) is lower than with punishment in deterrence-relevant settings.

- source: Herne, K., Lappalainen, O., Setälä, M., & Ylisalo, J. (2022). Accountability as a Warrant for Trust: An Experiment on Sanctions and Justifications in a Trust Game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment used a 2x2 design (punishment/no punishment x justification/no justification) in a three-player trust game (two senders, one responder, repeated stranger matching, 6 rounds). The opportunity for senders to punish responders (at a cost: 1 point spent reduces responder's payoff by 3) did not significantly increase the amounts sent or returned, nor did it improve group efficiency. In fact, net earnings for responders were lower in punishment treatments due to the cost of punishment, and group efficiency was not improved. Justification requirements (responders must explain their choices) increased contributions and returns, but this effect was not robust when controlling for the amount received. The combination of punishment and justification did not outperform justification alone in terms of efficiency. The main effect of punishment was to reduce responder payoffs via costly sanctions, not to increase group welfare.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that, in a trust-game-like environment with costly punishment (punishment cost 1:3), enabling punishment does not increase group efficiency and may reduce it due to the cost of punishment. The design is adjacent to public goods games (not a standard PGG, but a trust game with two senders and one responder, continuous contributions, no communication, 6 rounds, known horizon, no rewards). The findings suggest that, for similar game designs, the predicted efficiency with punishment enabled should be lower than or similar to the control (no punishment), unless the punishment is costless or much more effective. The paper also shows that justification requirements can increase contributions, but this does not translate into higher efficiency when punishment is present. Thus, for prediction, enabling costly punishment in similar trust or PGG-adjacent games is likely to reduce or not improve efficiency relative to control.

- source: Pevnitskaya, S., & Ryvkin, D. (2022). The effect of options to reward and punish on behavior in bargaining. *Journal of Economics & Management Strategy*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that adding costly punishment and/or reward options for responders in ultimatum bargaining changes both proposer and responder behavior. When only reward is available, proposers make lower offers and efficiency drops. When only punishment is available, acceptance of low offers increases, and efficiency for low offers is higher than baseline. When both reward and punishment are available, both offers and efficiency are highest. The use of reward and punishment is rare and mostly symbolic, so efficiency losses from their direct use are minimal. The main efficiency effects come from changes in offer and acceptance behavior, not from the direct cost of sanctions. The results suggest that punishment options can increase efficiency in settings with low offers, while reward options alone may reduce efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence on how the availability and cost/effectiveness of punishment and reward options affect efficiency in a two-player bargaining environment. While not a public goods game, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in asymmetric, bilateral settings with costly sanctions. The results show that costly punishment (with 1:1 cost-impact ratio) can increase efficiency by increasing acceptance of low offers, especially when offers are low, but that reward alone can reduce efficiency. The combination of both instruments yields the highest efficiency. For prediction tasks, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency depends on the presence of reward, the cost/effectiveness of punishment, and the baseline offer/acceptance norms. The findings are most transferable to settings with costly, direct, one-to-one punishment and reward, and less so to multiplayer or public goods environments.

- source: De Geest, L. R., Kidwai, A. H., & Portillo, J. E. (2022). Ours, not yours: Property rights, poaching and deterrence in common-pool resources. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that allowing insiders to punish outsiders in a CPR setting increases efficiency by reducing poaching and aligning insider harvests with the social optimum. The effect of punishment is more pronounced when insiders have earned their property rights, as they coordinate more effectively to deter poaching through higher and more frequent punishment. However, even with punishment, insiders do not fully deter poaching, and some poaching persists. The presence of chat among insiders likely facilitates coordination. The study provides direct evidence on how punishment and the method of property rights allocation interact to affect group payoffs and efficiency in a CPR context with intergroup conflict.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that enabling punishment in a CPR game with communication among insiders increases efficiency, especially when property rights are earned. The efficiency gain is due to reduced poaching and better coordination among insiders. The design is adjacent to a public goods game (PGG) but differs in the presence of outsiders and the poaching stage. The findings suggest that, in similar environments, enabling punishment will increase efficiency relative to the control (no punishment), with a larger effect if group identity is salient (e.g., earned rights). The effect is not absolute—some inefficiency remains due to incomplete deterrence. The results are most applicable to CPR games with insider/outsider structure, chat among insiders, and a punishment technology with a 1:3 cost-to-impact ratio.

- source: Kamei, K., Sharma, S., & Walker, M. J. (2025). Collective sanction enforcement: New experimental evidence from two societies. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that third-party punishment of defectors is common in both the UK and India, but is much stronger in the UK. In the UK, adding multiple third parties increases aggregate punishment, and enabling higher-order punishment (punishment of other third parties for failing to punish or for anti-social punishment) further increases aggregate punishment. This transforms the game from a prisoner's dilemma to a stag-hunt, increasing the potential for efficient cooperation. In India, punishment levels are lower, and neither group size nor higher-order punishment opportunities are sufficient to change the incentive structure; defection remains the dominant strategy. The effect of punishment on efficiency is thus positive and substantial in the UK (especially with higher-order punishment), but weak in India. The results highlight the importance of cultural context and prevailing norms in determining the efficiency effects of punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that the efficiency impact of punishment in social dilemma games depends on the number of punishers, the availability of higher-order punishment, and the cultural context. In environments similar to the UK, enabling higher-order punishment among multiple third parties can transform the incentive structure to support efficient cooperation, while in environments like India, the same institutional changes have little effect on efficiency due to weaker punishment norms. For prediction, the paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is moderated by group size, higher-order punishment mechanisms, and societal norms. The control efficiency (without punishment) is not sufficient to predict treatment efficiency; the presence and effectiveness of punishment institutions and cultural context must be considered.

- source: Praxmarer, M., Rockenbach, B., & Sutter, M. (2024). Cooperation and norm enforcement differ strongly across adult generations. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=mixed
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment finds that seniors are both more cooperative and more willing to punish defectors than juniors. However, the presence or generation of the third-party punisher does not significantly increase cooperation rates for either generation. As a result, the efficiency (measured by per capita earnings or welfare) is higher in groups of seniors due to their higher baseline cooperation, not due to the effect of punishment. Punishment is costly and does not lead to higher cooperation, so its net effect on efficiency is limited or even negative within each generation. The main moderator of efficiency is the generation of the players, not the punishment treatment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper suggests that, in repeated two-player prisoner's dilemma games with third-party punishment, enabling punishment does not reliably increase efficiency, as cooperation rates are largely unaffected by the presence or type of punishment. Efficiency is higher among seniors due to their higher baseline cooperation, not because punishment increases cooperation. For prediction tasks, the presence of third-party punishment (with the parameters used here) should not be expected to increase efficiency relative to control, especially among juniors. The main design dimension affecting efficiency is the generation (age) of the players, not the punishment mechanism. The findings are most directly relevant to two-player PDs with binary third-party punishment, and less so to standard public goods games.

- source: Lec, F. L., Matthey, A., & Rydval, O. (2023). Punishing the weakest link - Voluntary sanctions and efficient coordination in the minimum effort game. *Theory and Decision*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that voluntary costly punishment opportunities in a repeated minimum effort game (with 8 players per group, 16 rounds, and a two-stage design) significantly increase both effort levels and group efficiency compared to a baseline with no punishment and a treatment with costless disapproval (communication). In the punishment treatment, after an initial drop, average group payoff (net of punishment costs) rises and eventually exceeds the control, with about a third of groups reaching or approaching the Pareto-optimal equilibrium. Punishment is mainly directed at minimum-effort players, and its use is frequent. Disapproval/communication alone produces only a temporary or weak improvement. The results show that decentralized sanctions can serve as an effective coordination device in Pareto-ranked coordination games, with a net positive effect on efficiency in the longer run.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in repeated weakest-link (minimum effort) games with large groups and a history of inefficient coordination, enabling voluntary costly punishment leads to a robust and lasting increase in group efficiency, even after accounting for the direct costs of punishment. The effect is stronger and more persistent than that of costless communication. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that, holding control efficiency and other design dimensions constant, enabling punishment (with a 2:1 fine-to-cost ratio and anonymous assignment) will increase average efficiency, especially in settings with multiple rounds, large groups, and no prior communication. The initial rounds after introducing punishment may see a drop in net efficiency, but this reverses as groups coordinate on higher efforts. The results are most directly applicable to coordination games with similar structure, but provide adjacent evidence for public goods games.

- source: Jia, S. H., & Wang, Z. Y. (2025). An evolutionary game model with reputation threshold and reputation score to promote trust in the sharing economy. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that implementing a dynamic, reputation-based reward and punishment mechanism in a trust game on a square lattice network increases the proportion of trustworthy participants and can increase total group revenue (efficiency) compared to a baseline without such mechanisms. The effect depends on the reputation threshold: moderate thresholds maximize both trust and total revenue, while very high thresholds reduce revenue due to excessive punishment. The model demonstrates that, for a wide range of initial conditions, appropriate punishment/reward settings can drive the system to full trust and higher efficiency. The results are robust to population size. The model is not a standard PGG but is adjacent, and the efficiency outcome is directly comparable.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that, in networked trust games with dynamic reputation-based punishment and reward, enabling punishment (and reward) can increase group efficiency compared to a baseline with no sanctions, provided the punishment/reward parameters are set appropriately.
