  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment studies how participants in the role of 'Player C' choose to punish others after allocations that may be unfair (intentional or by chance) or fair. Punishment is costly: for each point given up, 5 points are deducted from the target. The main findings (from the main paper, not this appendix) concern when and whom participants punish, and how this varies by the intentionality of the unfairness and by subject pool. Theoretical predictions using the Fehr-Schmidt model suggest punishment should only occur in unfair allocations, and only against those with higher payoffs. The appendix does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides detailed evidence on punishment behavior in a non-PGG allocation game, with a strong manipulation of intentionality and target of punishment. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. The punishment technology (cost and impact) is clearly specified, which may be useful for mapping to other studies. The design is adjacent to PGGs but not a direct variant.

- source: Philippsen, A., Mieth, L., Buchner, A., & Bell, R. (2023). Communicating emotions, but not expressing them privately, reduces moral punishment in a Prisoner's Dilemma game. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost
  findings: Across two experiments, the authors find that giving participants the opportunity to communicate their emotions to their partner after each round of a Prisoner's Dilemma game with costly punishment reduces the rate of moral punishment compared to a control condition. However, this reduction is small, and moral punishment remains common. Privately expressing emotions (without communication) does not reduce moral punishment. Both emotion communication and private expression can reduce cooperation and increase other forms of punishment (hypocritical punishment, punishment bias). The study does not report efficiency or group payoff as a function of punishment, so the direct effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be determined from this paper.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides high-quality evidence on how emotion communication and private expression affect punishment behavior in a repeated one-shot Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment. However, it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings as a function of punishment, so it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings suggest that punishment rates (especially moral punishment) can be reduced by allowing emotion communication, but the effect is small. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it uses a two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with a punishment stage. The results are most useful for understanding moderators of punishment behavior, not for predicting efficiency outcomes.

- source: Migheli, M., & Saraceno, M. (2023). On the propensity to settle or litigate in laboratory disputes. *Economia Politica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that the threat of costly, uncertain punishment (litigation) is a strong motivator for offenders to restore what they took, more so than fairness concerns about the victim's effort. Offenders are more likely to restore when they learn the victim worked for their endowment, but this effect is mainly present among female offenders. Higher litigation fees increase the probability that offenders restore to avoid litigation and decrease the probability that victims initiate litigation, with gender moderating these effects. The study does not report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral responses to punishment threats and fairness cues.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the behavioral effects of costly, uncertain punishment threats (litigation) in a two-player dispute context, not a public goods game. It shows that punishment threats (here, litigation) increase the likelihood of restoration (settlement), especially when the cost of punishment is high. However, the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The design is adjacent to PGGs, as it involves second-party costly punishment with the possibility of redress, but lacks the multi-player, repeated, or contribution structure of PGGs. The main relevance is for understanding how punishment cost and fairness cues affect compliance and dispute resolution, not efficiency.

- source: Cabrales, A., Kendall, R., & Sánchez, A. (2022). The effectiveness of prosocial policies: Gender differences arising from social norms. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that policy interventions (framing, exogenous punishment, endogenous punishment) do not reduce the overall average speed (a proxy for collective safety) compared to control. However, there are strong gender differences: policies reduce risky (Fast) choices and average speed among female participants, but increase them among males. This results in no net improvement in group-level outcomes and increases gender inequality in payoffs. The effect is mediated by differences in social norms and normative expectations. The most effective policy for changing female behavior is endogenous punishment, but male participants respond by increasing risky behavior, offsetting the gains. The study does not report efficiency or group payoff in the public goods sense, and the main outcomes are behavioral and norm-related.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that the effect of punishment mechanisms (both exogenous and endogenous) on group-level efficiency or payoff in public-goods-like environments may be highly heterogeneous and context-dependent, especially along gender lines. In this specific driving game, punishment does not improve group-level efficiency (as proxied by average speed or safety) because positive effects for one subgroup (females) are offset by negative effects for another (males). The design is not a standard PGG, and efficiency is not directly measured, so the findings should be transferred with caution. The main implication for prediction is that punishment may not always increase efficiency, especially if there are strong heterogeneities in norm compliance or behavioral response. The study highlights the importance of considering subgroup responses and social norms when predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency.

- source: Cheung, G., & Siegel, D. A. (2025). The Costs of Collective Action: An Experimental Study of Risk, Punishment, and Immunity. *Journal of Politics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment finds that higher punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success among nonimmune individuals. Immune individuals are more likely to participate, but their participation is not affected by punishment risk or cost. As the number of immune individuals increases, nonimmune individuals participate less, consistent with free riding. Group size has no significant effect on nonimmune participation or group success, but immune individuals participate more in larger groups. The main outcomes are participation and group success, not efficiency or total group payoff. Average payoffs are reported, but not analyzed as a function of the experimental treatments. The findings support the theoretical model's predictions about the effects of punishment risk, cost, immunity, and group size on participation and collective action success.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides informative evidence about how external punishment risk and cost reduce participation and group success in a collective action setting, and how immunity and group size moderate these effects. However, the game is not a standard public goods game: the group benefit is all-or-nothing and depends on a random pivotal actor, and only participants can be punished by an external agent. There is no within-group punishment or contribution to a divisible public good. Efficiency as defined in public goods games (total group payoff relative to full cooperation) is not directly analyzed, though average payoffs and group success rates are reported. The results are most relevant for predicting how external punishment risk and cost affect participation and group-level success in threshold-like or all-or-nothing collective action settings, especially when some individuals are immune to punishment. The findings are less directly transferable to standard PGG efficiency predictions, but may inform models where external sanctions or risk of punishment for cooperation are present.

- source: Yin, X. L., Li, J. B., Li, D. H., & Chen, S. Y. (2023). When emotional responses conflict with self-interested impulses: A transcranial direct current stimulation study of cognitive control in cooperative norm compliance. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishment (TPP) is higher when punishment is costless and right DLPFC activity is inhibited (cathodal tDCS), driven by stronger negative emotional responses to norm violations. When punishment is costly, neither tDCS nor emotional response significantly affects TPP. The results suggest that the cognitive control process for norm compliance involves a conflict between emotional impulses to punish and self-interested motives to avoid cost. However, the paper does not report efficiency, group payoff, or any direct payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence about the psychological mechanisms of third-party punishment in a prisoner's dilemma variant, especially the role of cost in moderating punishment behavior. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. It does suggest that costly punishment may be less frequent and less sensitive to emotional impulses, which could imply a limited effect on efficiency, but this is not measured. The design and findings are most relevant for understanding when and why punishment is used, not its impact on group welfare.

- source: Jiang, X. H., & Zheng, W. Q. (2024). Dynamic strategies for collaborative governance of rural environments: a simulation study on the most effective mechanisms. *Environment Development and Sustainability*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that under a static reward and punishment mechanism (SRPM), the system does not reach a stable equilibrium; strategies of all parties fluctuate periodically. Under a dynamic reward and punishment mechanism (DRPM), the system converges to a stable mixed-strategy Nash equilibrium (MSNE), interpreted as an evolutionarily stable strategy (ESS). Increasing the upper limit of punishments for illegal emissions by village enterprises significantly increases the probability of legal emissions and reduces the need for strict government regulation and active supervision by villagers. Increasing the upper limit of rewards for villagers' active supervision increases their supervision probability but may reduce legal compliance by enterprises and government regulatory effort. The DRPM is recommended for more effective and stable governance.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that dynamic, behavior-dependent punishment mechanisms (analogous to more flexible or responsive punishment rules in public goods games) can stabilize cooperative behavior (legal emissions) and reduce the need for costly monitoring and enforcement. However, the model is not a standard public goods game and does not report efficiency or group payoff. The main outcomes are strategy probabilities, not payoffs. The findings suggest that increasing punishment effectiveness (punishment_tech) and using dynamic punishment rules may improve stability and compliance, but the effect on efficiency is not directly quantified. The results are most relevant for understanding how punishment design (cost, tech, dynamic vs static) may affect behavioral outcomes in multi-agent governance games.

- source: Wu, Y. E., Li, Z. Q., Liu, Z. Y., Cui, B. W., & Yin, A. R. (2024). Third party interventions promote cooperation on the interdependent networks: A perspective based on prospect theory. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The introduction of third-party intervention (punishment and/or reward) in a networked PD/SD game increases the fraction of cooperators in both the intervention and dispute layers. The effect is stronger when the intervention cost is lower and the intervention intensity (punishment/reward coefficients) is higher. The results are robust across different network topologies (square lattice and ER random networks) and game types (PD and SD). However, the paper does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare; all results are in terms of cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that third-party punishment and reward mechanisms can increase cooperation rates in networked social dilemmas, and that the effect is moderated by the cost and intensity of intervention. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The results are most useful for predicting changes in cooperation rates, not payoffs. The model is also based on the PD/SD rather than a standard PGG, and is simulation/theory-based rather than empirical.

- source: Rostovtseva, V. V., Puurtinen, M., Salinas, E. M., Cox, R. F. A., Groothuis, A. G. G., Butovskaya, M. L., & Weissing, F. J. (2023). Unravelling the many facets of human cooperation in an experimental study. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that individuals are consistent in their prosocial and punishment-related decisions across a variety of economic games, but that punishment is not correlated with other prosocial tendencies. The appearance of interaction partners (as judged by facial attributes) systematically affects prosocial behavior in most games, but not in the punishment game. There is no evidence that facial appearance predicts actual prosocial behavior. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, and thus does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like environments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a suite of economic games including a punishment game, the tendency to punish is not associated with other prosocial behaviors and is not influenced by partner appearance. However, it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, so it cannot be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its main value is in showing that punishment behavior is psychologically and behaviorally distinct from other forms of cooperation, and that its drivers may differ from those of prosociality. For the downstream prediction task, this paper suggests that the presence of punishment may not always align with higher prosociality, but provides no direct evidence about efficiency effects.

- source: Im, C., & Lee, J. (2022). On the fragility of third-party punishment: The context effect of a dominated risky investment option. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when a third party in a dictator game is offered a dominated risky investment option in addition to the punishment and safe options, both the frequency and amount of punishment decrease significantly compared to when only punishment and safe options are available. The demand for investment increases when all options are available, indicating a substitution effect. The results are robust to risk attitudes and to making the investment option strictly dominated (negative expected return). The findings support the hypothesis that context effects (specifically, the compromise effect) can undermine third-party punishment, even when the alternative is unattractive in both material and psychological terms. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that the presence of additional, even irrelevant, choice options can reduce the use of third-party punishment in dictator-game-like settings. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the design of the choice set (e.g., presence of unrelated alternatives) can moderate the effect of punishment on cooperation or norm enforcement. However, because the study does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict the effect of punishment on efficiency. The findings are most relevant for understanding behavioral mechanisms and the fragility of punishment as an enforcement tool in the presence of context effects.

- source: Goodman, J. R. (2023). The problem of opportunity. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that in agent-based models of the Prisoner's Dilemma with punishment (strong reciprocity), cooperation can be maintained at high levels when only overt defectors are present. However, when agents can mimic cooperators or defect covertly (evading detection and punishment), the population can be dominated by individuals who are not true cooperators, even though the observed rate of cooperation remains high. The models highlight that the opportunity to defect undetected undermines the effectiveness of punishment-based strategies in ensuring genuine prosociality. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but raises concerns that high observed cooperation rates in experimental games may not indicate high group welfare or internalized prosocial preferences.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical caution for interpreting high cooperation rates in punishment-enabled games as evidence of increased efficiency or group welfare. It shows that punishment can maintain high cooperation in simple models, but that the introduction of undetectable defection strategies (mimicry, covert defection) can allow free-riding to persist without reducing the observed cooperation rate. For prediction tasks, this suggests that the presence of punishment may not always translate to higher efficiency, especially if the game design allows for undetected defection. However, the models are dyadic PDs, not public goods games, and do not report efficiency or group payoff, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible from this paper.

- source: Zhao, X., & Zou, H. (2025). Research on the Evolutionary Game of Multi-Body Co-Innovation in Green Innovation Ecosystems. *Polish Journal of Environmental Studies*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The paper finds that government-imposed rewards (subsidies) and punishments (fines) both promote the stability of collaborative innovation among the three actors. There is an optimal benefit-sharing ratio for collaboration, and the effect of government intervention is strongest when both rewards and punishments are used together. The model shows threshold effects for punishment severity, and that the stability of cooperation is sensitive to the distribution of benefits and the magnitude of policy interventions. However, the main outcomes are the evolutionary stability of cooperation, not explicit efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task. It shows that introducing punishment (and/or reward) mechanisms increases the stability and likelihood of cooperation in a multi-actor game with public-goods-like features. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff directly, so it cannot be used to quantitatively predict the change in efficiency from enabling punishment. The results suggest that, in similar multi-actor public-goods-like environments, punishment (especially when combined with reward) is likely to increase cooperation and system stability, which may be associated with higher efficiency, but this is not directly measured.

- source: Alam, R., & Rai, T. S. (2025). Profitable third- party punishment destabilizes cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that profitable third-party punishment (i.e., paying punishers to punish) destabilizes cooperation: dictators are less likely to share when punishers are paid, both before and after any punishment is experienced. This effect is robust across multiple experimental designs, including repeated games and conditions optimized for reinforcement learning. Even when receivers (who benefit from cooperation) can choose the punishment regime, they systematically select profitable punishment, reducing their own expected earnings by about 20%. The negative effect of profitable punishment on cooperation is only eliminated when the game structure makes antisocial punishment impossible and this is clearly signaled. The main outcome is cooperation rate, but expected receiver earnings are also reported, showing a negative welfare effect. The study highlights the importance of the communicative function of punishment and the dangers of introducing profit motives into punishment systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing profit motives for punishment (i.e., making punishment profitable for the punisher) can reduce cooperation and lower welfare, even when punishment is otherwise available. While the experiments use dictator game variants rather than standard public goods games, the mechanisms are closely related: the presence and structure of punishment, and especially the motives signaled by punishment, are critical moderators of its effect on cooperation and efficiency. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that enabling punishment is not always beneficial for efficiency—if punishment is profitable for the punisher, it may reduce efficiency compared to both unpaid punishment and no punishment. The effect is robust to repeated play and persists even when learning is optimized. The results also show that the structure of punishment (e.g., whether antisocial punishment is possible) is a key moderator. However, because the main outcome is cooperation rate rather than group efficiency, and the game is a dictator game variant, direct quantitative predictions for public goods games should be made with caution, but the qualitative direction is clear: profitable punishment can backfire.

- source: Li, J. Y., Wu, W. H., Li, Z. Z., Wang, W. X., & Zhang, B. Y. (2023). Data-driven evolutionary game models for the spread of fairness and cooperation in heterogeneous networks. *Frontiers in Psychiatry*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that costly punishment, when amplified by network heterogeneity and increased inequality aversion (e.g., via oxytocin), can spread through a network and promote higher levels of fairness and cooperation. This effect is observed in both the ultimatum game and the two-stage prisoner's dilemma with punishment, but not in the trust game, where the absence of punishment means that increased trust does not diffuse. The models fit experimental data well for behavioral outcomes (offers, cooperation rates, punishment rates), but do not directly analyze or report group efficiency or total payoff. The findings suggest that punishment mechanisms are more effective than reward/trust mechanisms for promoting prosocial behavior in networks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction that enabling costly punishment in networked social dilemmas (such as public goods or prisoner's dilemma variants) increases cooperation and fairness, especially when network heterogeneity allows central nodes to amplify the effect. However, because the paper is theoretical and focuses on behavioral outcomes (not efficiency or group payoff), it does not provide direct quantitative evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. The models suggest that punishment is more effective than reward in promoting prosocial outcomes, but efficiency implications must be inferred from increased cooperation and fairness, not directly measured. The paper is most useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators (e.g., network structure, inequality aversion), but less so for direct efficiency prediction.

- source: Wen, Y. J., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2025). Indirect punishment can outperform direct punishment in promoting cooperation in structured populations. *Plos Computational Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that, in structured populations modeled as a square lattice, indirect punishment (punishing defectors who harm one's neighbors) can outperform direct punishment (punishing one's own defectors) in promoting cooperation, but only when both the cost and fine of punishment are low. Outside this narrow parameter region, direct punishment may be more effective, both may be equally effective, or neither may improve cooperation. The mechanism is that indirect punishment can impose more total fines on defectors without greatly increasing the cost to punishers, but only when punishment is cheap. The main reported outcomes are cooperation rates and the distribution of strategies; group efficiency or total payoff is not directly measured.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how the cost and scope of punishment affect cooperation rates in spatial social dilemmas. While it does not report efficiency or group payoff, it suggests that indirect punishment can be more effective at promoting cooperation than direct punishment under low-cost, low-fine conditions, but not otherwise. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, the results are only adjacent, as the main outcomes are behavioral. The findings may inform expectations about when punishment is likely to increase efficiency, but direct quantitative mapping is not possible from this paper alone.

- source: Li, B. F., Zhang, X., Shi, Z. Y., & Wei, W. (2023). Justice strategy can promote cooperation with the joint of dynamic aspiration in social dilemma. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a justice strategy (where players punish defectors at equal cost) alongside dynamic aspiration updating can suppress or eliminate defectors and promote cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The effectiveness depends on the initial aspiration level: higher aspirations lead to fewer defectors and more justicers. The justice strategy can dominate even when initially rare, especially when defectors form clusters. The model is agent-based and simulation-driven, with outcomes reported as the stable fractions of each strategy. No aggregate payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that punishment (even when costly to the punisher) can reduce defection and promote cooperation in spatial social dilemmas, especially when combined with dynamic aspirations. However, because the outcomes are reported as strategy fractions rather than group efficiency or payoff, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment. The model is also a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game, though the mechanisms are related. The findings may be useful for understanding the behavioral pathways by which punishment can affect cooperation, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments.

- source: Wang, W., He, Z. X., Li, X. G., & Shi, L. (2023). Adaptive exit facilitates the evolution of cooperation in the spatial prisoner's dilemma game with punishment. *Epl*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to adaptively exit the game based on their payoff relative to aspiration can significantly increase the prevalence of cooperation, even when punishment is weak. There is an optimal aspiration level at which adaptive exit best promotes cooperation. Adaptive exit protects cooperators from exploitation by defectors and reduces the burden of punishment on punishers by increasing defectors' exit probability. The model shows that adaptive exit can eliminate second-order free-riding and that the interplay between exit and punishment is crucial for the evolution of cooperation. However, the paper does not report on group efficiency, welfare, or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that adaptive exit mechanisms and the interaction between exit and punishment can strongly affect the prevalence of cooperation in spatial social dilemmas. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of strategy frequencies and not group efficiency or total payoff, the findings are only adjacent to the prediction task. The results suggest that enabling exit (or similar mechanisms) may increase cooperation and reduce the cost of punishment, but do not provide quantitative guidance for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Yan, Z. Y., Zhao, H., & Li, L. (2025). Dynamic role-switching in hypergraphs: Enhancing cooperation via adaptive punishment and reinforcement learning. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that periodic update rules with Q-learning and a punishment mechanism can substantially increase the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. Lower punishment costs and shorter update intervals are especially effective at promoting cooperation. The model is robust across different network structures. However, the main outcome is the fraction of cooperators, not group efficiency or total payoff, and the results are based on agent-based simulations rather than empirical experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that punishment mechanisms, when combined with learning and periodic update rules, can increase cooperation rates in spatial games. However, because the main outcome is cooperation frequency rather than efficiency or group payoff, the findings should be used with caution for predicting efficiency effects. The results suggest that lower punishment costs and more frequent updating are likely to make punishment more effective at promoting cooperation, which may translate to higher efficiency in related models, but this is not directly demonstrated.

- source: Guo, T., He, Z. X., & Shi, L. (2023). Self-organization in mobile populations promotes the evolution of altruistic punishment. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that migration in spatially structured populations promotes the evolution and prevalence of pro-social punishment (altruistic punishment) and cooperation, especially when individuals migrate to maximize potential gains rather than avoid losses. There is an optimal population density for this effect. Migration can sustain pro-social punishment even when punishment efficiency (fine/cost ratio) is low, as long as the absolute amount of punishment is high. The model does not report efficiency or group payoff, but the prevalence of pro-social punishment and cooperation is interpreted as a positive outcome for the evolution of cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the idea that enabling migration or spatial self-organization can enhance the effectiveness of punishment in promoting cooperation, especially under certain migration preferences and population densities. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The findings are most relevant for understanding the mechanisms by which migration and spatial structure interact with punishment, rather than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Guan, K. X., Chen, Y. Y., Zheng, W. J., Zeng, L. L., & Ye, H. (2022). Costly signals can facilitate cooperation and punishment in the prisoner?s dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that in a prisoner's dilemma with costly signaling, punishment as a costly signal can become prevalent if the signal is strong and noise is low, but this does not necessarily increase cooperation. Without punishment, cooperation can dominate if the cost-benefit ratio is favorable and noise is low. The introduction of punishment (when its cost is converted into a strong costly signal) increases the frequency of punishment and reduces defection, but does not increase cooperation. The model does not report on efficiency or group payoff, so the effect of punishment on efficiency cannot be directly inferred.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effects of punishment in two-player prisoner's dilemma games with costly signaling, but does not directly address efficiency or group payoff. The findings suggest that punishment can reduce defection and increase the prevalence of punishment strategies if the punishment signal is strong, but does not necessarily increase cooperation or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games, the relevance is limited and indirect, as the model is not a PGG and does not report efficiency outcomes.

- source: Huo, H., & Liu, X. Y. (2024). Behavioral decision-making of government, agricultural product producers, and consumers on agricultural product quality and safety regulation in a digital environment. *Frontiers in Public Health*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that severe government penalties for non-compliance (punishment), sufficient incentives, and reduced costs for producers to build traceability systems are key to achieving stable cooperation among stakeholders. Depending on the relative size of these parameters, the system can stabilize in different equilibria: (Not regulate, Not build, Satisfied), (Regulate, Not build, Satisfied), or (Regulate, Build, Satisfied). The presence and strength of punishment (penalties) are critical in shifting the equilibrium toward higher compliance by producers. However, the outcomes are reported as stable strategy profiles, not as explicit efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect support for the prediction task by showing that the presence and severity of punishment (penalties) can shift the equilibrium from non-compliance to compliance in a multi-actor regulatory game. However, because the model does not report group efficiency or explicit payoff outcomes, it cannot be used to directly predict efficiency changes due to punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings suggest that increasing punishment can increase compliance, but the translation to efficiency or group payoff is not quantified. The model structure and parameter dependencies may inform qualitative expectations about the effect of punishment in similar multi-actor regulatory environments.

- source: Wang, J., Liu, Z., Xu, Y., & Li, X. P. (2025). Dynamic evolution in multi-player networked trust games with graded punishment. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that introducing a graded punishment mechanism in a networked trust game simulation can effectively reduce untrustworthy behavior and increase the density of investment and trust. The effect of punishment depends on both the cost to punishers and the severity of punishment. Graded punishment outperforms traditional punishment in eliminating untrustworthy agents and increasing simulated group trust and total wealth. However, if punishment costs are too high, investment and trust collapse. The results are sensitive to network topology, with regular lattices supporting higher total wealth than scale-free networks. All findings are based on agent-based simulations, not real-world or laboratory experiments.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theory-based evidence that graded punishment mechanisms can increase simulated group trust and total wealth in a networked trust game, especially when punishment is not too costly. However, because the study is simulation-based and uses a trust game variant (not a standard PGG), its findings should be interpreted as suggestive rather than directly predictive for real-world or experimental PGGs. The results highlight the importance of the cost and effectiveness of punishment, and suggest that graded (rather than uniform) punishment may be more effective at sustaining cooperation and group payoff in networked environments. The paper does not provide empirical data for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but its modeling results may inform hypotheses about the role of punishment cost, punishment effectiveness, and network structure.

- source: Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, Q. W. (2025). Evolutionary dynamics of a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback in regular networked Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that a probabilistic punishment mechanism with environmental feedback can lead to three types of system states: (1) stable full defection with low probability of effective punishment, (2) stable full cooperation with high probability of effective punishment, and (3) bi-stability where both outcomes are possible depending on initial conditions. Increasing the speed of environmental feedback (i.e., how quickly the probability of effective punishment updates in response to behavior) can shift the system from defection to cooperation in the bi-stable regime. The model highlights the importance of punishment effectiveness, punishment cost, network degree (number of neighbors), and feedback speed in shaping cooperation dynamics. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how probabilistic punishment and environmental feedback affect cooperation in a networked Prisoner's Dilemma, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that punishment mechanisms with high effectiveness and fast feedback can promote cooperation, especially in structured populations. However, because the outcomes are reported in terms of cooperation frequency and not efficiency or group payoff, the paper offers only indirect support for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public-goods-like games. The findings may inform the expected direction of efficiency changes (via increased cooperation), but quantitative predictions for efficiency require caution.

- source: Yang, S. S., Chen, S. J., & Liu, Y. F. (2025). Perceptions of Economic Fairness Positively Affect Altruistic Punishment. *European Journal of Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost
  findings: Across five studies (including lab experiments with TPP games), the paper finds that individuals with higher perceptions of economic fairness are more likely to engage in costly punishment of norm violators. This effect is causal and robust to different manipulations of fairness perceptions. However, the effect is moderated by outcome efficacy: the positive effect of fairness perceptions on punishment only appears when punishment is expected to be effective at changing behavior or upholding norms. The main dependent variable is the amount of punishment assigned (tokens spent or likelihood of sending a disapproval message), not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence about psychological moderators of punishment behavior (specifically, perceptions of economic fairness and outcome efficacy), but does not report or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes in public-goods-like games. The experimental paradigm is adjacent to PGGs (third-party punishment of unfair dictator allocations), but the findings cannot be directly used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGGs. The results may inform models of when punishment is more likely to be used, but not its effect on group efficiency. Game design dimensions such as player count, MPCR, or punishment cost are not systematically manipulated or reported, and efficiency is not measured.

- source: Bosworth, W. (2022). Social Power and Non-cooperative Game Theory. *Journal of Theoretical Politics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The paper argues that the credibility of threats and offers (punishments and rewards) is central to understanding social power and governance in non-cooperative games. It shows that resources, reputation, and coordination problems determine the ability to credibly punish or reward, and thus to exercise power. The analysis highlights the importance of commitment mechanisms and tipping points in reputation for sustaining credible threats. However, the paper does not provide empirical findings or quantitative predictions about efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical insights into how punishment and reward mechanisms may affect cooperation and social power in collective action settings, emphasizing the importance of credibility, resources, and coordination. However, it does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence that can be directly used to predict efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. Its value for prediction is limited to high-level mechanism discussion and may inform the interpretation of empirical results from other sources.

- source: Li, B. Q., Wu, Z. X., & Guan, J. Y. (2022). Critical thresholds of benefit distribution in an extended snowdrift game model. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists, reward_tech
  findings: The main findings are: (1) In both infinite and finite structured populations, the critical proportion of benefits that defectors can exploit (while still maintaining stable cooperation) increases with the total resource available, but saturates at a limit. (2) Introducing incentives for cooperators and punishments for defectors increases this critical threshold, meaning that inequality (the share defectors can take) can become worse with such mechanisms. (3) In more highly connected populations, the critical threshold is lower, so inequality is less severe. (4) There is a critical value of incentive/punishment magnitude above which the relationship between resource size and critical threshold reverses. The paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, but rather the conditions for stable coexistence of cooperation and defection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the effect of punishment (and reward) on the sustainability of cooperation under unequal benefit distributions in a snowdrift game, which is adjacent to public goods games. It suggests that introducing punishment (and reward) can increase the threshold of tolerated inequality, potentially making outcomes less fair for cooperators. However, because the paper does not report efficiency or group payoff, it does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that punishment can have counterintuitive effects on the distribution of benefits and the persistence of cooperation, especially under varying network structures and resource levels.

- source: Ibrahim, A. M. (2022). The conditional defector strategies can violate the most crucial supporting mechanisms of cooperation. *Scientific Reports*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that conditional defector strategies—cheaters who sometimes cooperate (e.g., to share dispersal costs or pay to escape punishment)—can successfully invade and dominate populations even when mechanisms like punishment, kin selection, and spatial structure are present. In the models, when cheaters can share dispersal costs, they can migrate and outcompete cooperators, leading to cooperator extinction. Similarly, when cheaters reduce their own payoff to avoid detection and punishment, they can dominate despite the presence of punishment mechanisms. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of agent types and extinction events, not direct efficiency or group payoff measures.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary theoretical perspective: the presence of punishment mechanisms does not guarantee the maintenance of cooperation or high efficiency if conditional defector strategies are possible. The models are adjacent to public goods games and do not report efficiency as a ratio to the fully cooperative outcome, so direct quantitative prediction is not possible. However, the findings suggest that in environments where agents can adaptively evade punishment (e.g., by reducing their own payoff to avoid detection), the expected efficiency gain from enabling punishment may be much lower or even negative, as cheaters can still dominate. For prediction tasks, this paper highlights the importance of considering the sophistication of defector strategies and the limitations of punishment as a stabilizing mechanism.

- source: Wylie, J., & Gantman, A. (2024). Cooperation, domination: Twin functions of third-party punishment. *Social and Personality Psychology Compass*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=N/R | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that codified rules and third-party punishment have 'twin functions': they can promote cooperation and social order, but also serve as tools for domination, discrimination, and oppression. The authors review literature showing that third-party punishment can increase cooperation and group payoffs in experimental games, but also highlight evidence that punishment is often motivated by self-interest, ingroup favoritism, and can be used to harm outgroups or marginalized individuals. The paper calls for a broader theoretical understanding of punishment that includes its role in maintaining social hierarchies and facilitating discrimination, not just cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. However, it is useful for contextualizing the dual roles of punishment mechanisms: while they can increase cooperation and efficiency under some conditions (as shown in the literature it reviews), they can also be used in ways that undermine fairness or group welfare, especially when institutionalized. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effect of punishment on efficiency is context-dependent and may be moderated by group composition, institutional legitimacy, and the potential for discriminatory enforcement. It does not provide parameter-level evidence for any specific game design dimension.

- source: Kupfer, T. R., & Tybur, J. M. (2023). Third-party punishers who express emotions are trusted more. *Proceedings of the Royal Society B-biological Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that third-party punishers who express moral emotions (anger or disgust) are trusted more by others than those who punish financially or do not express emotions. Expressing emotions in addition to financial punishment further increases trust. The main outcome is the amount of money entrusted in a trust game, which is a behavioral measure of trust and reputation, not efficiency or group payoff. The results suggest that emotion expression can enhance the reputational benefits of third-party punishment, potentially facilitating cooperation, but the study does not measure or report on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or their variants. Its findings are relevant for understanding how punishment and emotion expression affect trust and reputation, which may be indirectly related to cooperation and efficiency, but no efficiency or payoff data are reported. Therefore, this paper should not be used as primary evidence for predicting efficiency outcomes in PGG-like environments with or without punishment, but may be useful for understanding reputational mechanisms and behavioral trust effects.

- source: Dato, S., & Friehe, T. (2025). Punishment for intentions or outcomes: the role of gender and social norms. *Social Choice and Welfare*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The experiment shows that second movers punish first movers more harshly for selfish intentions than for considerate ones, even when the outcome is held constant. The effect of the realized outcome (equal vs. unequal payoffs) on punishment is weak or absent overall, but women are more likely than men to increase punishment in response to unequal outcomes, while men are more responsive to unkind intentions. Social norm elicitation reveals that punishment is seen as more appropriate after norm violations (selfish choices or unequal outcomes), and these perceptions partially explain gender differences in punishment. The study does not report on efficiency, group welfare, or total payoffs, and the game is not a public goods game but a two-player lottery allocation game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on the psychological and social-norm drivers of costly punishment in a two-player sequential lottery game, not a public goods game. While it demonstrates that punishment is sensitive to intentions and outcomes (with gender differences), it does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes, nor does it use a multi-player or repeated public goods game structure. Thus, its direct value for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games is limited. However, it may inform mechanism design by highlighting that punishment is more likely when norm violations are perceived as intentional, and that gender composition may moderate punishment behavior. The absence of efficiency or group payoff analysis means it cannot be used to predict treatment efficiency from design dimensions or control efficiency.

- source: Otten, K., Buskens, V., Przepiorka, W., & Ellemers, N. (2025). Protecting Public Goods or Helping Free Riders? A Real-Life Moral Dilemma in Interethnic and Intraethnic Encounters. *Psychological Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds robust evidence that bystanders are more likely to help in-group (native-majority) free riders and more likely to sanction out-group (ethnic-minority) free riders in a real-life public transport context. The behavioral outcome is whether the bystander helps the free rider (enabling norm violation) or sanctions (refuses or disapproves). The effect is consistent across locations, age groups, and sessions. The study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or any explicit payoff-based outcome. The findings highlight the role of group membership in informal norm enforcement and the potential for double standards in sanctioning free riders.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence on informal sanctioning of free riders in a real-world context, but does not report efficiency or payoff-based outcomes. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves a public good (fare compliance) and the possibility of informal punishment (sanctioning) or anti-punishment (helping). The main insight for prediction is that the presence and effectiveness of informal sanctions (and their social selectivity) can depend strongly on group membership, and that in some real-world settings, bystanders may choose to help free riders rather than sanction them, especially for in-group members. However, because no efficiency or group payoff is measured, the paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in standard PGG-like games. Its main value is in highlighting the complexity and social contingency of informal punishment in naturalistic settings.

- source: Mitsuishi, K., & Kawamura, Y. (2025). Avoidance of altruistic punishment: Testing with a situation-selective third-party punishment game. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Across three preregistered experiments, participants consistently avoided situations where they might have to punish unfairness, especially when direct punishment (with a real-effort cost) was possible. Avoidance was less pronounced when only indirect punishment (e.g., negative feedback) was available, and lowest when no punishment was possible. When participants could not avoid observing unfairness, they were likely to punish unfair behavior, regardless of their prior avoidance. The study provides evidence that the prevalence of third-party punishment in standard lab games may overstate its real-world frequency, as people often avoid such situations if possible. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, welfare, or total payoffs, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-like games, as it does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that the opportunity for punishment (especially direct, costly punishment) leads to avoidance of situations where punishment decisions are required, and that indirect punishment options reduce this avoidance. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency, this paper suggests that the structure of the punishment opportunity (direct vs indirect, costliness) may affect participation rates in punishment, but does not provide quantitative or qualitative evidence about efficiency outcomes.

- source: Fischborn, M. (2023). The Evolutionary Roots of Moral Responsibility. *Philosophy of Science*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that moral responsibility judgment evolved as an adaptation that enabled groups of cooperative individuals to hold free riders responsible more safely by acting in a coordinated way. It distinguishes between two senses of justification in responsibility judgment (demand and permission) and hypothesizes that the permission sense evolved to allow individuals to assess social support for punishment, making collective punishment episodes safer and more stable. The account is situated within Tomasello's two-step theory of the evolution of morality and Boehm's ethnographic work on hunter-gatherer punishment practices. The paper critiques Mameli's account of second-order punishment and meta-emotional dispositions, arguing that coordinated group punishment (rather than second-order punishment) is the key evolutionary innovation. The paper does not provide empirical evidence or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes, but offers a conceptual framework for understanding the robustness and negative bias of responsibility practices.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence or data for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It is a theoretical account of the evolutionary function of punishment and responsibility judgment, emphasizing the importance of coordination and social support in making punishment episodes safer and more stable. While it discusses the role of punishment in stabilizing cooperation and addressing free rider problems, it does not analyze or report on efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Therefore, its value for the downstream prediction task is limited to providing conceptual context about why punishment may have evolved to support cooperation, but it does not inform quantitative or design-specific predictions about efficiency effects in experimental games.

- source: Shen, Y., Deng, Z. X., Kang, H. W., Sun, X. P., & Chen, Q. Y. (2025). The impact of tax-based rewards and mercenary punishment on public goods games. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that among German teachers and headmasters, willingness to sanction violations of the mask-wearing norm is significantly predicted by higher conscientiousness and neuroticism, but not by economic preferences such as risk or time. Leadership (headmaster) willingness to sanction is a strong predictor of teachers' willingness to sanction. Females are less willing to impose high fines than males. The outcome is self-reported fine size, not actual behavior or group payoff. The context is adjacent to public goods games, as mask-wearing is framed as a public good, but the study does not use a PGG or measure efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. It is relevant for understanding individual differences in willingness to sanction in real-world cooperation norm contexts, especially newly introduced norms, and highlights the importance of personality and leadership. However, it does not report on actual punishment behavior, group cooperation, or efficiency outcomes, so it cannot be used to calibrate the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. Its main value is in identifying predictors of sanctioning attitudes, not payoff consequences.

- source: Zhang, Z., Li, M. H., Liu, Q. Y., Chen, C., & Qi, C. H. (2023). Group membership and adolescents' third-party punishment: a moderated chain mediation model. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Adolescents are more willing to assign harsher third-party punishment to outgroup (stranger) offenders than to ingroup (classmate) offenders in a hypothetical unfair allocation scenario. This ingroup favoritism is stronger for boys than for girls. The effect of group membership on punishment is mediated by perceived unfairness and anger. The study uses a self-report, costless punishment paradigm and does not involve real monetary incentives or actual group payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods or similar games. Its findings are limited to psychological and behavioral responses (punishment, unfairness, anger) in a third-party punishment context, using hypothetical scenarios. Therefore, it should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff-based outcomes in public goods games or their variants. However, it may inform expectations about the social and psychological moderators of punishment behavior, such as group membership and gender.

- source: White, C. J. M., & van de Vondervoort, J. W. (2025). Third-party punishment, vigilante justice, or karma? Understanding the dynamics of interpersonal and cosmic justice. *Journal of Experimental Social Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: Across three vignette-based experiments, the paper finds that: (1) third-party punishment (i.e., antisocial behavior directed at a transgressor) is evaluated more negatively than impersonal misfortune or reprimand, even when the punisher knows the victim's past bad behavior; (2) knowledge of the victim's past transgression makes punishment slightly less negative, but not positive; (3) reprimand is evaluated much more positively than actual punishment; (4) belief in karma predicts more positive evaluations of impersonal misfortune for bad actors, but does not increase approval of interpersonal punishment; (5) costliness of punishment to the punisher does not significantly affect evaluations. No efficiency, payoff, or group outcome measures are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games or similar economic games with or without punishment. Its findings are about social and moral evaluations of punishment, not about behavioral or payoff consequences. It does, however, suggest that third-party punishment is often viewed negatively by observers, and that alternative responses (reprimand, impersonal punishment) are preferred, but this is not linked to efficiency or group payoff. The paper is not informative for the downstream prediction task of treatment efficiency.

- source: Shen, J. Y. (2024). Preferences for punishments: explorations from a stated-choice experiment in Japan. *Applied Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main finding is that there is substantial heterogeneity in preferences for different types of punishment (e.g., length of must-attend meetings, number of reprimands, pay cut duration, scope of name disclosure) that could be imposed on uncooperative team members. Four latent classes of punishment preference are identified, ranging from those with positive preferences for all punishments to those with negative preferences for all. Socioeconomic characteristics such as age, employment status, management experience, fairness, and trust are associated with class membership. The study does not measure or report on actual cooperation, contribution, or efficiency outcomes, nor does it manipulate or observe real punishment behavior in a public goods or similar game.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. It only reports on stated preferences for punishment types in a hypothetical scenario. No data is provided on actual game design dimensions, control efficiency, or treatment efficiency. Therefore, it should not be used to inform quantitative predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games.

- source: Suzuki, K., & Ishiwata, R. (2022). Impact of a Carbon Tax on Energy Transition in a Deregulated Market: A Game-Based Experimental Approach. *Sustainability*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study used a 4-player, 30-round online game simulating energy companies in a deregulated market, with and without a carbon tax (punishment for fossil fuel use). The carbon tax was introduced in the second half of the game and was known to players in advance. The main findings are: (1) The carbon tax significantly increased renewable energy usage after its implementation, accelerating the energy transition. (2) Total group profit (efficiency) was higher with the tax, but the difference was not statistically significant. (3) The tax did not significantly affect R&D investment or market price in aggregate, but did increase market price after the tax was imposed. (4) Information about future taxation alone did not change behavior or reduce uncertainty; only actual taxation affected actions. (5) Players' perceptions of uncertainty and obstacles to transition were not reduced by the tax. Overall, the carbon tax improved efficiency-related outcomes by promoting renewables and increasing group profit, but only after its implementation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that introducing a punishment mechanism (carbon tax) in a competitive, multi-player, repeated game increases group efficiency (total profit) and accelerates the transition to socially optimal behavior (renewable energy use). The effect is most pronounced after the punishment is actually implemented, not merely announced. For prediction tasks, this suggests that enabling punishment in similar games is likely to increase efficiency, especially when the punishment is salient and directly affects payoffs. The game structure is adjacent to a public goods game, with competition and investment choices, so the findings are most applicable to PGG variants with market competition and investment dynamics. The effect size on efficiency is positive but may not always reach statistical significance, especially in small samples.

- source: Chávez, C. A., Murphy, J. J., Quezada, F. J., & Stranlund, J. K. (2023). The endogenous formation of common pool resource coalitions. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that endogenous coalition formation, combined with the ability to monitor and sanction outsider poaching (via government-imposed fines), leads to larger coalitions, reduced resource exploitation, and higher individual and group earnings compared to open access. These effects are strongest when monitoring costs are low, enabling the grand coalition to form and fully deter poaching. When monitoring is not possible or is too costly, only small coalitions form and efficiency gains are minimal. Among fishers, coalition formation and monitoring are frequent and effective, while among students, coalitions are smaller, monitoring is rare, and efficiency gains are weak or absent.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that enabling punishment (here, government-imposed fines for poaching, triggered by coalition monitoring) increases efficiency in a CPR game, especially when monitoring costs are low. The effect is mediated by coalition size and the ability to deter outsiders. The design dimensions most relevant for prediction are the cost and effectiveness of monitoring and punishment, the ability to form coalitions, and the baseline efficiency under open access. The results suggest that, in environments where monitoring and punishment are feasible and not too costly, enabling punishment will substantially increase efficiency relative to control. However, if monitoring is impossible or prohibitively expensive, the efficiency gains from punishment are minimal. The findings are most applicable to real-world user groups with experience in collective management.

- source: Lie-Panis, J., Fitouchi, L., Baumard, N., & André, J. B. (2024). The social leverage effect: Institutions transform weak reputation effects into strong incentives for cooperation. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model demonstrates that institutions, by leveraging contributions from individuals (motivated by reputation), can create strong enough incentives (via punishment, monitoring, or rewards) to sustain cooperation in settings where reputation alone would fail. The efficiency of the institution (how much incentive is generated per unit contribution) is a key moderator: high efficiency greatly expands the parameter space where cooperation and high payoffs are possible. The model also shows that when the population is patient (values future reputation), more individuals contribute, further increasing institutional effectiveness. However, when cooperation is easy, the institution can be wasteful, as its costs are not offset by additional gains in cooperation or payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical support for the prediction that enabling efficient institutional punishment or monitoring in a public-goods-like environment will increase group efficiency and payoffs, especially when baseline (control) efficiency is low due to hard cooperation problems. The model clarifies that the effect of punishment depends critically on the efficiency of the institution (punishment technology), the cost of contributing to the institution, and the patience of the population. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that when institutional punishment is efficient and the population is sufficiently future-oriented, enabling punishment will lead to large increases in efficiency relative to control. However, if cooperation is already high in control, the marginal benefit of punishment may be negligible or even negative due to unnecessary costs.

- source: Chassy, P., Cole, J., & Brennan, C. (2025). Evolution of confrontation and cooperation in simple organisms as a function of environmental resources and cost of a conflict. *Expert Systems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=close | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, reward_exists
  findings: The simulations show that the confrontation rate in a population is highly sensitive to the magnitude of punishment (cost of conflict) and reward (resource availability). As punishment increases, confrontation rates decrease, and organisms become more cooperative. The adaptation is driven by the evolution of gene profiles that determine the probability of confrontation. The model also finds that memory (even a simple one-step memory) has a limited but significant effect on confrontation rates, especially as punishment increases. The study provides evidence that risk-taking and confrontation are shaped by both environmental parameters and genetic/cognitive factors, but does not directly measure efficiency or group payoff as in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence that increasing the cost or effectiveness of punishment in a confrontation/cooperation environment reduces the frequency of confrontational (defecting) behavior and increases cooperation. While the model is not a public goods game, the findings suggest that in environments where punishment is more costly or effective, populations evolve to be more cooperative, which would likely increase efficiency in analogous PGG-like settings.
