However, it suggests that the effectiveness of punishment (via external supervision) is highly sensitive to the cost of supervision and the mutation rates of enforcers and players. The model is adjacent to public goods games, but is not a standard PGG, and the main outcomes are behavioral (strategy fractions), not efficiency. Thus, the paper is more useful for understanding mechanisms and moderators of punishment and corruption than for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

- source: Kaba, M., Koyuncu, M., Schneider, S. O., & Sutter, M. (2024). Social norms, political polarization, and vaccination attitudes: Evidence from a survey experiment in Turkey. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that both pro- and anti-vaxxers discriminate against each other in allocation tasks, with pro-vaxxers showing stronger outgroup bias (interpreted as 'punishment' of anti-vaxxers for norm violation). Political polarization amplifies this discrimination. Randomized informational treatments (reminders of pandemic health/economic costs or the Russia-Ukraine war) reduce outgroup bias, especially the war treatment among pro-vaxxers. However, the main outcome is behavioral (allocation bias), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The allocation tasks and modified public goods game measure outgroup discrimination, not group efficiency or total earnings. The findings may be useful for understanding how social identity and polarization affect norm enforcement and discrimination, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment mechanisms in PGG-like environments.

- source: Cao, N. M., Miao, R. R., Sun, B. H., Ren, Z. R., & Yue, G. A. (2024). Exemplary Self-Discipline, Leniency towards Others: Competitive Contexts Amplify the Black Sheep Effect in Restoring Ingroup Trust. *Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that after a trust violation and apology, participants are more willing to restore trust (measured by increased investment) with out-group members than with in-group members, but this effect is only present in competitive contexts. In cooperative contexts, trust is restored equally for both in-group and out-group members. The results are interpreted as evidence for the 'black sheep effect' in trust repair, moderated by the nature of the interaction (competition vs. cooperation). The outcome is investment behavior, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It uses a repeated trust game (not a PGG), and the main outcome is trust restoration (investment behavior) rather than efficiency or group payoff. There is no explicit punishment mechanism; the 'punishment' is implicit in reduced trust/investment after a violation. The findings may be relevant for understanding how group identity and context affect willingness to restore cooperation after norm violations, but they do not provide quantitative or direct evidence about efficiency or payoff outcomes in PGG-like games with explicit punishment options.

- source: Zhang, X. C., & Lu, S. C. (2024). The effect of survival pressure-based defector reward behavior on cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma games. *Plos One*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost, reward_tech
  findings: The study finds that when defectors are forced by survival pressure to reward cooperators, the frequency of cooperation in the population can increase, especially when the survival pressure threshold is low (players are more sensitive to pressure), the reward cost is low, and the reward amount is sufficiently high. The effect of these parameters is non-linear and depends on their combination with the temptation to defect. The model shows that rewarding behavior by defectors acts as a transitional strategy that can facilitate the emergence and maintenance of cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on agent-based simulations on a lattice network.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. Its findings are limited to the effect of a specific form of reward (not punishment) on cooperation rates in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The model and results are adjacent to the target prediction task, as they do not include payoff-based outcomes or public goods game structures, nor do they manipulate or analyze punishment. The main relevance is in showing that certain forms of reward can increase cooperation rates under specific parameter regimes, but this cannot be directly translated into efficiency predictions for PGGs with punishment.

- source: Hoffmann, L. (2022). Cooperation in the name of God? Experimental evidence from Ghana and Tanzania. *Journal of Economic Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that religious reminders of punishment or reward decrease cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma among Christian and Muslim participants in Ghana and Tanzania. The punishment prime reduces cooperation by about 8 percentage points, and the reward prime by about 6 percentage points, relative to the control. There is no evidence that religious primes increase cooperation. The study does not report group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on cooperation rates and beliefs/expectations.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. The design is a one-shot prisoner's dilemma (not a repeated PGG), and the 'punishment' and 'reward' treatments are religious primes, not institutionalized peer punishment or reward mechanisms. The main outcome is cooperation rate, not efficiency. Thus, the paper is only weakly informative for the downstream prediction task, except as a caution that not all forms of punishment/reward framing increase cooperation, and that context and mechanism matter.

- source: Lu, X. Q., Li, T., Li, B. M., & Yang, Z. (2025). The influence of third-party's power on multi-player fairness decision-making. *Acta Psychologica*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The study finds that participants care about fairness towards third parties in three-person ultimatum games, but their concern is modulated by whether the third party has decision power. When the third party is powerless, participants enforce fairness norms and show both disadvantageous and advantageous inequity aversion. When the third party has power, participants incorporate both fairness and social comparison norms, showing greater disadvantageous inequity aversion and even advantageous inequity preference towards the third party. The presence of a powerful third party leads to diffusion of responsibility, reducing the participant's willingness to punish unfairness. However, the study does not measure or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, so its findings are only indirectly relevant to efficiency prediction in public-goods-like games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about how punishment and norm enforcement mechanisms may operate in multi-player bargaining contexts, especially regarding the psychological mechanisms (inequity aversion, responsibility, social comparison) that underlie acceptance/rejection of unfair offers. However, because the study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its main value is in highlighting that the structure of power and responsibility among players can modulate willingness to punish norm violations, which may, in turn, affect efficiency in related but not identical game structures.

- source: Liu, W. X., Wang, H., Zhu, H. J., Zhu, X. Y., He, X. Y., & Zhang, W. (2022). Morality is Supreme: The Roles of Morality, Fairness and Group Identity in the Ultimatum Paradigm. *Psychology Research and Behavior Management*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that in the ultimatum game, responders are more likely to accept offers (including unfair ones) from proposers with positive moral backgrounds than from those with negative moral backgrounds. This effect is robust across fair, moderately unfair, and extremely unfair offers. Group identity, manipulated via moral priming, has only a partial effect: positive or negative moral priming increases acceptance rates for positive moral proposers in unfair allocations, but does not significantly affect responses to negative moral proposers. The gender of the proposer does not significantly affect acceptance rates. The study does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, so its findings are not directly transferable to efficiency prediction tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that in ultimatum game settings, the perceived morality of the proposer strongly influences acceptance rates, even for unfair offers. However, because the outcome measured is acceptance rate (not efficiency or group payoff), and because the ultimatum game is not a public goods game or a direct variant, the findings are only indirectly relevant for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. The study does not manipulate or analyze punishment mechanisms, costs, or efficiency, so it does not provide direct evidence for the prediction task. Its main value is in highlighting the importance of social/moral context in responder behavior, which may inform broader models of cooperation and norm enforcement.

- source: Yuan, H. R., Meng, X. Z., & Alzahrani, A. K. (2023). Analysis of dynamic evolution process of the N-player division of labor game model. *Mathematical Modelling of Natural Phenomena*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The model shows that high toxicity rates (especially against cheaters), high toxin levels, and smaller group sizes promote the disappearance of cheaters and the persistence of cooperation (cooperators and/or toxin producers). The system exhibits bistability: depending on initial conditions and parameters, the population may converge to all cooperators, coexistence of cooperators and toxin producers, or coexistence of cooperators and cheaters. The interior equilibrium (all three strategies coexisting) is always unstable. Bifurcation analysis reveals thresholds in absorption rates and toxin parameters that determine which strategies persist. The main reported outcomes are the frequencies of each strategy at equilibrium, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how costly sanctioning (here, toxin production) can suppress free riding and promote cooperation in N-player games. However, the model is not a standard public goods game with explicit peer punishment, and does not report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. The findings suggest that increasing the effectiveness or prevalence of costly sanctions (analogous to punishment) can reduce free riding and increase the fraction of task-performers, especially in smaller groups and with higher sanction effectiveness. For prediction tasks, this paper supports the idea that punishment-like mechanisms can increase cooperation, but does not provide quantitative estimates of efficiency changes or direct mapping to standard PGG dimensions.

- source: Bica, A., & Cretu, R. Z. (2023). The carrot and the stick: How guilt and shame facilitate reciprocity-driven cooperation. *Cognition & Emotion*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: Inducing guilt in participants led to more generous offers to fair partners (positive reciprocity), while inducing shame led to higher rejection rates of unfair offers and lower offers to unfair partners (negative reciprocity). Both emotions influenced fairness-related decision-making, but via different mechanisms. The study does not report on efficiency or group payoff, focusing instead on behavioral responses (acceptance, proposal amounts) in the Ultimatum Game. The findings suggest that moral emotions can facilitate cooperation, but the pathways differ: guilt promotes generosity toward fair actors, while shame motivates punishment of unfairness.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about the behavioral mechanisms (reciprocity, punishment, reward) that can affect cooperation in economic games, but does not directly inform predictions about efficiency or group payoff in public goods or punishment-enabled games. The Ultimatum Game is adjacent to public goods games, and the punishment here is endogenous (rejection of unfair offers) rather than a separate costly punishment mechanism. The findings may inform expectations about how emotional states or social context could moderate the impact of punishment or reciprocity on cooperation, but do not provide quantitative or direct evidence for efficiency outcomes under different game design dimensions.

- source: Wang, Q., & Mao, C. M. (2024). Research on Cooperative Water Pollution Governance Based on Tripartite Evolutionary Game in China's Yangtze River Basin. *Water*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The study finds that the evolution toward cooperative governance (strict regulation, green production, active public participation) is promoted by lower regulatory costs, higher fines for non-compliance, appropriate subsidies for green production, and material rewards for public participation. Excessively high or low subsidies or rewards can destabilize cooperation. The model identifies five equilibrium points, with the ideal being all stakeholders choosing the cooperative strategy. The findings are based on theoretical modeling and simulation, not empirical data or observed payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence about the mechanisms by which punishment (fines), rewards, and regulatory costs affect the evolution of cooperation in a multi-stakeholder environmental governance setting. While the model structure is related to public goods games and includes punishment and reward analogues, it does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes, nor does it provide empirical estimates. The results may inform qualitative expectations about the effects of punishment and reward mechanisms, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency changes in PGG-like experiments.

- source: Testori, M., Giardini, F., Cruz, T. D. D., & Beersma, B. (2023). What Matters Most in Supporting Cooperation, the Gossip Content or the Gossiper's Intention? Simulating Motive Interpretation in Gossip Dynamics. *Jasss-the Journal of Artificial Societies and Social Simulation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_other_summaries
  findings: The model shows that cooperation can be sustained or undermined depending on how agents interpret the motives behind gossip and how they update reputations. If gossip is interpreted as pro-social and agents use a single reputation system, cooperation can rise to high levels. If gossip is interpreted as selfish or negative, a double reputation system (where agents discount unreliable gossip but do not punish the gossiper by refusing to cooperate) helps maintain initial cooperation levels. The model highlights the importance of motive interpretation and the structure of reputation systems for sustaining cooperation, but does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in public goods games. However, it offers indirect insights into how information transmission, reputation updating, and the interpretation of motives can moderate the effects of social information on cooperation. For prediction tasks, the findings suggest that mechanisms which allow agents to discount unreliable information without ostracizing the source may help sustain cooperation, but the model's outcomes are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.

- source: Huang, S. X., Liu, X. S., Hu, Y. H., & Fu, X. (2023). The influence of confidence in aspiration-fulfillment strategy on prisoner's dilemma evolution. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that a confidence mechanism—where confidence in a strategy is updated based on the difference between payoff and aspiration—can promote and stabilize cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game. The effect of the confidence increment parameter (alpha) is non-monotonic: smaller alpha can lead to higher cooperation under some conditions, but too small an alpha or high temptation to defect undermines cooperation. Common aspirations in the group support higher cooperation than heterogeneous aspirations. There is a threshold effect for aspiration: below the threshold, increasing aspiration increases cooperation, but above it, cooperation collapses. However, all findings are in terms of cooperation rates, not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Its findings on confidence and aspiration mechanisms in spatial prisoner's dilemma games may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how psychological or updating mechanisms affect cooperation, but not for the efficiency prediction task as defined. The absence of payoff or efficiency outcomes and the lack of punishment or reward mechanisms mean this paper should not be weighted for the core prediction task.

- source: Gomes, C. M., Mulhinch, M. R., & McCullough, M. E. (2025). People decide whether to share social information-and whether to share it accurately-on the basis of self-interest. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment shows that people are more likely to share reputational information (gossip) when they have behaved generously in a prior economic game, and less likely when they have behaved greedily. The tendency to share information honestly or dishonestly is strongly influenced by whether the participant stands to benefit from being chosen as a partner for a subsequent economic game. When lying is possible and being chosen is incentivized, greedy or indifferent participants are more likely to share dishonest, self-serving gossip. Generous participants are more likely to share honest information, especially when honesty is required and being chosen is incentivized. The results support reputation-based partner choice and social competition theories over indirect reciprocity theory. No direct measures of group efficiency or total payoff are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. However, it offers informative context about the strategic use of social information (gossip) in economic games with partner choice and reputation. The findings suggest that the effectiveness of information-sharing mechanisms (and their honesty) depends on participants' incentives and prior behavior, which may be relevant for understanding the role of reputation and information in environments with punishment or partner choice. Since no efficiency or payoff outcomes are reported, this paper should not be used as a primary source for predicting efficiency effects of punishment, but may inform mechanistic expectations about when information-based interventions (including punishment or reputation) are likely to be effective or undermined by dishonesty.

- source: Khatun, K., Shen, C., Shi, L., & Tanimoto, J. (2024). Stability of pairwise social dilemma games: Destructive agents, constructive agents, and their joint effects. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in pairwise social dilemma games, introducing destructive agents (who opt out and harm others) does not promote cooperation, in contrast to their effect in public goods games. Instead, destructive agents destabilize or replace defection (in prisoner's dilemma and stag-hunt) or undermine cooperation (in harmony and chicken games). Constructive agents (who opt out and benefit others) can have similar destabilizing effects if their own payoff is high, but if their payoff is lower than what they give, they mainly destabilize defection without harming cooperation. When both agent types are present, the system exhibits complex multistable equilibria, but cooperation is not promoted. The analysis is entirely theoretical, using replicator dynamics and stability analysis. No efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes are reported.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not model punishment, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes, and its focus is on pairwise (dyadic) games rather than multiplayer public goods games. The closest relevance is in its discussion of how certain opt-out strategies (destructive/constructive agents) affect the stability of cooperation, but not efficiency or group payoff. Therefore, this paper should not be weighted strongly in predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding the limits of voluntary participation and opt-out mechanisms in pairwise games, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Zhang, Q. W., Liu, J. Q., & Zhang, X. R. (2024). Reputation-based disconnection-reconnection mechanism in Prisoner's Dilemma Game within dynamic networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=other_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that a reputation-based mechanism, where players can disconnect from low-reputation neighbors and reconnect with others, significantly increases the frequency of cooperation in repeated PDG simulations on dynamic networks. The effect is stronger when the reputation factor (alpha) is high and when the network's information connectivity (environmental degree r, rewiring probability p) is high. Conversely, when the selection intensity (sigma) is high, the effect of reputation is diminished and cooperation collapses. The results are consistent across both square lattice and small-world network topologies. However, the study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes, focusing instead on behavioral cooperation rates.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence relevant to the prediction task. While it does not study public goods games or efficiency/payoff outcomes directly, it shows that reputation-based dynamic linking mechanisms can strongly increase cooperation rates in repeated PDG environments. The findings suggest that, in analogous PGG-like settings, mechanisms that allow for social selection or partner choice based on reputation may increase cooperation, which could plausibly translate into higher efficiency if payoffs are aligned with cooperation. However, the lack of direct efficiency or payoff data limits the ability to make quantitative predictions about efficiency changes. The results are most relevant for settings where dynamic partner selection or reputation-based exclusion is possible.

- source: Lu, S. N., & Wang, Y. (2024). Adjustment of link weights based on tolerance promotes cooperation in spatial prisoner's dilemma game. *Biosystems*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that allowing individuals to reduce interaction intensity with neighbors who defect beyond a tolerance threshold increases the frequency of cooperation in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. However, if the tolerance threshold is too high (i.e., individuals are too forgiving), cooperation is undermined. The effect is stronger when more individuals in the population are 'environmental adaptors' who adjust their interaction intensity. The main outcome is the equilibrium frequency of cooperators, not group payoff or efficiency. The findings are robust to changes in network size and initial cooperator fraction.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about mechanisms that may promote cooperation in repeated social dilemmas, specifically through adaptive partner selection or ostracism based on tolerance for defection. However, it does not report efficiency or group payoff, and the game is a spatial prisoner's dilemma, not a public goods game. The results suggest that mechanisms allowing individuals to reduce interaction with defectors (after a tolerance threshold) can increase cooperation rates, but excessive tolerance undermines this effect. For prediction tasks focused on efficiency in public goods games with punishment, this paper is only weakly informative, as it does not analyze punishment costs, efficiency, or public goods structures.

  findings: The study finds that incorporating both direct and indirect experience in interaction and strategy update rules, as well as introducing tolerance in link weight adjustment, significantly promotes cooperation in spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulations. However, the model does not include explicit punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and does not report group efficiency or total welfare outcomes. The findings are based on simulation results and are not directly transferable to public goods games with punishment.

  findings: The study finds that incorporating local loyalty to both cooperation and defection strategies in a spatial prisoner's dilemma game can promote the emergence and stability of cooperation, especially when the strength index of local loyalty is positive and the weighting between payoff and loyalty is balanced. - source: Feng, S. N., & Liu, X. S. (2024). Effects of three-faced strategy on the evolution of cooperation in social dilemma. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that the introduction of a 'three-faced' strategy—one that defects against cooperators, punishes defectors, and cooperates with its own kind—can suppress defection and promote cooperative behavior in a population, depending on parameter values. Low role-switching costs or high government rewards for punishing defectors favor the spread of the three-faced strategy and/or cooperators. High punishment costs or high temptation to defect favor defectors. The model identifies parameter regimes where cooperators, defectors, and three-faced individuals coexist, or where one strategy dominates. However, the paper does not report group efficiency or total payoff, focusing instead on the evolutionary stability and proportions of each strategy.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, theoretical evidence about the effects of punishment-like mechanisms in a prisoner's dilemma context with a novel adaptive strategy. It does not report efficiency or group payoff, so it cannot be used directly for predicting efficiency outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it suggests that punishment-like behaviors (when not too costly and/or when subsidized) can suppress defection and support the maintenance of cooperation, especially when adaptive strategies are possible. The findings may inform qualitative expectations about the role of punishment cost, effectiveness, and incentives, but do not provide quantitative efficiency data for prediction.

  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the probability of enterprises joining a pollution control coalition depends on the relative costs and benefits, the sharing of profits, and the initial willingness to cooperate. Under certain parameter conditions, all enterprises will join the coalition (full cooperation), while under others, none will join. The model shows that the presence of random disturbances (stochasticity) leads to fluctuations in strategies but does not fundamentally alter the stable outcomes. The analysis highlights the importance of effective incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, but these are discussed at a conceptual level rather than modeled as explicit game interventions. The main findings are about the conditions for stable coalition formation, not about the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. While it discusses the importance of incentive and punishment mechanisms for coalition stability, it does not model or experimentally test the effect of enabling or disabling punishment on group efficiency or payoff. The model is adjacent to PGGs in that it studies cooperation among multiple agents with shared benefits and costs, but the main outcomes are behavioral (coalition formation) rather than efficiency. The paper may inform mechanistic expectations about the role of incentives and punishment in sustaining cooperation, but it does not provide quantitative evidence for the prediction task.

- source: Yamamoto, H., Okada, I., & Suzuki, T. (2025). Gradual reputation dynamics evolve and sustain cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: all_or_nothing
  findings: The experimental results show that people use a three-level (good, neutral, bad) reputation system and update reputations gradually: cooperation increases reputation by one level, defection decreases it by one, except that defection against a bad recipient (justified defection) leaves reputation unchanged. Theoretical evolutionary game modeling demonstrates that, under certain conditions, tolerant norms that cooperate with good and neutral and defect only against bad can dominate and sustain high cooperation rates. However, the study does not use a public goods game or measure group efficiency or payoff; it focuses on reputation dynamics and the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. Its findings are adjacent: it informs how nuanced reputation and justified defection (a form of social sanction) are perceived and how these perceptions can support cooperation in indirect reciprocity. However, it does not manipulate or measure punishment costs, punishment effectiveness, or group payoffs, nor does it use a PGG or similar economic game. Thus, it should be used only as indirect, contextual evidence about the psychological and evolutionary underpinnings of social sanctions and cooperation.

- source: Gong, H. W., Xiang, H. J., Wang, Y. F., Gao, H. J., & Meng, X. Z. (2024). Strategy evolution of a novel cooperative game model induced by reward feedback and a time delay. *Aims Mathematics*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: reward_exists
  findings: The paper finds that in a theoretical evolutionary game with dynamic, feedback-based rewards (but no punishment), the system can exhibit multiple stable equilibria, bistability, and oscillatory dynamics depending on parameters and time delay. Increasing the reward does not always increase cooperation; under some conditions, even maximal rewards do not induce cooperation. The initial state can determine which equilibrium is reached. Introducing time delay can destabilize previously stable equilibria and induce stable limit cycles (oscillations) in the proportion of cooperators and reward intensity. The model does not analyze group payoff, efficiency, or welfare, and does not include punishment or sanctions.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is a theoretical analysis of a different class of evolutionary games (general 2x2 matrix) with dynamic rewards and time delay, focusing on the evolution of cooperation and reward intensity. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and no implementation of punishment or sanctions. The findings may be of limited, indirect interest for understanding how dynamic incentives and time delays affect cooperation dynamics, but they do not inform the prediction task regarding punishment effects on efficiency.

- source: Martinez-Vaquero, L. A. (2023). Inequality leads to the evolution of intolerance in reputation-based populations. *Chaos*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The paper finds that economic inequality is a strong enhancer of intolerance in reputation-based populations. Intolerant strategies can invade tolerant groups more easily when inequality is high, even if overall economic stress is moderate. Redistribution of wealth can prevent or reverse the spread of intolerance, but only if implemented before intolerance becomes widespread. The model shows that tolerant minorities under economic stress may reduce their willingness to punish defectors or may even help wealthy discriminators. The main reported outcomes are the intention to cooperate and the evolutionary stability of strategies, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, contextual evidence for the prediction task. It suggests that in reputation-based cooperation environments, inequality and group structure can strongly moderate the effectiveness of punishment-like mechanisms (here, reputation-based exclusion) in sustaining cooperation. However, because the model does not report efficiency or group payoff, it cannot be used directly to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. The findings are most useful for understanding how inequality and group structure might interact with punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, but not for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Pandula, N., Akcay, E., & Morsky, B. (2024). Indirect reciprocity with abductive reasoning. *Journal of Theoretical Biology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that abductive reasoning in indirect reciprocity models can, under certain social norms and low error rates, promote higher levels of cooperation than non-reasoning models. For the Scoring norm, abductive reasoning enables stable cooperative states that are not possible without reasoning. For other norms (Simple Standing, Staying, Stern Judging), abductive reasoning can outperform or underperform non-reasoning depending on the error rates and the degree of confirmation/falsification bias. Under the Shunning norm, abductive reasoning leads to only defection. The model is more robust to errors in observation/assessment than to errors in action. The main reported outcomes are the equilibrium frequencies of cooperative strategies and intended cooperation rates, not group efficiency or total payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect, mechanistic insight into how reasoning about errors and reputation assignment can affect the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity settings. While it does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes, it suggests that the cognitive process for assigning reputations (abductive reasoning) and the social norm in use can be critical moderators of cooperation rates. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like games with punishment, this paper is only adjacent: it does not model explicit PGGs, group-level payoffs, or direct costly punishment, but it does highlight the importance of error handling and norm choice for sustaining cooperation. Its findings may inform mechanistic priors about the robustness of cooperation to errors and the role of cognitive biases, but cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency.

  findings: The review finds that both social and nonsocial uncertainty can reduce prosocial behaviors by providing excuses for selfishness or increasing loss aversion, but in some contexts, uncertainty can also increase prosociality (e.g., by promoting forgiveness or overestimating one's impact on others). Coping strategies for social uncertainty include impression formation, impression updating, and compliance with social norms. Punishment and reward are discussed as norm enforcement tools, but the review does not analyze their effects on efficiency or group payoff. The review highlights the complexity and context-dependence of uncertainty's effects on cooperation and norm enforcement.

However, it does not provide quantitative or direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff, nor does it systematically analyze how game design dimensions moderate these effects. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions and control efficiency, but it may be useful for understanding mechanisms and generating hypotheses about the role of uncertainty and norm enforcement.

  findings: The introduction of exemplary cooperation (a strategy that pays an unconditional cost and exerts a positive influence on defectors) can prevent the collapse into full defection, even under high temptation to defect. The model shows that, depending on parameters (cost of exemplary cooperation, unforced-feedback from defectors, benefits of mutual cooperation, temptation to defect), the population can reach stable states of coexistence between strategies, or even full cooperation. The presence of exemplary cooperators increases the resilience of cooperation and can enhance the 'quality' of cooperation, but the paper does not quantify efficiency or group payoff. The results are based on theoretical analysis and numerical simulations of replicator dynamics and spatial evolutionary games.

The findings may be relevant for understanding how non-punitive, positive influence strategies affect cooperation, but are only adjacent to the prediction task of estimating efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.   findings: The paper finds that Indonesia's NRM practices are primarily allocation-focused and legal-normative, which limits their effectiveness in promoting sustainable, cooperative behavior. By integrating the PD/game theory approach into a socio-ecological governance framework, the authors argue that institutions can be designed to foster trust, reciprocity, and cooperation. They highlight that social punishment (e.g., social disapproval) may be more effective than financial sanctions in promoting norm compliance and cooperation, and that non-utilitarian values (e.g., stewardship, feelings of guilt) should be incorporated into NRM incentive structures. However, the paper does not present new empirical data or analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes directly.

However, it does not provide empirical estimates or quantitative predictions of efficiency or payoff changes due to punishment in PGG-like games. For downstream prediction tasks, this paper suggests that the effectiveness of punishment may depend on whether it is social or financial, the presence of trust and reciprocity, and the integration of non-utilitarian values, but it does not provide direct evidence to calibrate efficiency outcomes. Its main value is in highlighting relevant mechanisms and moderators for consideration in empirical synthesis.

  findings: The paper finds that social preference models (such as inequity aversion and strong reciprocity) are designed to explain prosocial behaviors observed in games like the public goods game, ultimatum game, and dictator game. These models posit that individuals are motivated by concerns beyond self-interest, such as fairness or social image, and that such motives can explain costly prosocial behaviors, including punishment of defectors. However, the chapter argues that these models often use the standard self-interest model as a normative benchmark (i.e., efficiency is still defined in terms of material payoffs), and that the assumption of perfect rationality is retained more for model simplicity than for psychological realism. The chapter also notes that the evidence for perfect rationality in these contexts is weak, and that cognitive biases may play a significant role in observed behaviors.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct empirical evidence or quantitative findings that can be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. However, it is useful for understanding the theoretical underpinnings of why punishment is often assumed to increase efficiency (by sustaining cooperation and public goods provision) in the literature, and why efficiency is typically measured in terms of material payoffs. The chapter also cautions that the actual psychological mechanisms may be more complex than standard models assume, and that the effect of punishment on efficiency may depend on factors not captured by simple social preference models. For prediction tasks, this suggests that while the literature often expects punishment to improve efficiency, the underlying mechanisms and context-specific factors (such as cognitive biases or heterogeneity in motives) may moderate this effect.

However, the paper does not provide quantitative estimates or meta-analytic synthesis of the effects of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is useful for mapping the landscape of research topics, identifying that punishment, reward, communication, and fairness are widely studied as cooperation-promoting mechanisms. However, it does not report or synthesize quantitative results on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes. For the downstream prediction task, this paper may help identify which dimensions are commonly studied and which mechanisms are considered important, but it does not provide parameter-level or effect-size evidence.

- source: Si, Z. H., He, Z. X., Shen, C., & Tanimoto, J. (2023). Speculative defectors as unexpected insulators of super cooperators in structured populations. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that the interplay between environmental feedback and game parameters (replenishment rate, dilemma strength, loner payoff, draw payoff) determines the long-run prevalence of cooperation, defection, and abstention in the population. Under resource scarcity, loners dominate; under resource abundance, all strategies coexist in a no-dominance equilibrium (RPS-like). Cyclic dominance emerges under intermediate conditions. The model shows that environmental feedback can shift the system between social dilemma and cyclic dominance regimes. However, the paper does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment/reward mechanisms.

It does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in illustrating how environmental feedback and the availability of an abstention (loner) strategy can alter the dynamics of cooperation and defection, sometimes leading to cyclic dominance. - source: Pedroso, M. (2022). Betting blind: coping with uncertainty through redundancy. *Synthese*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that redundancy in group tasks (such as public goods provision) is beneficial in unpredictable or 'opaque' environments, as it increases the probability that the group will succeed despite individual failures. However, the benefits of redundancy diminish as more redundant members are added, and can disappear if failures are correlated (coupling). High redundancy also increases the incentive to free-ride, making such arrangements evolutionarily unstable unless mechanisms like coercion (punishment) or ignorance of others' strategies are present. The paper discusses, but does not empirically test, the hypothesis that punishment can stabilize high-redundancy cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and theoretical background on the conditions under which redundancy (extra cooperators) is beneficial or stable in public-goods-like environments. It suggests that punishment or coercion may be necessary to maintain high levels of cooperation (and thus redundancy) in the face of free-rider incentives, but does not provide empirical or quantitative evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff. For prediction tasks, the paper supports the idea that punishment can be a moderator of cooperation in redundant groups, but does not inform the quantitative relationship between punishment and efficiency. Its value is mainly in highlighting the importance of free-rider suppression mechanisms (like punishment) for the stability of cooperation in redundant settings.

- source: Lean, C. H., & Jones, C. J. (2023). The evolution of multispecies populations: a multilevel selection perspective. *Biology & Philosophy*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper develops a theoretical model showing that in multispecies mutualisms, group-structured dispersal (MLS1) can stabilize cooperation (mutualism) against cheater invasion, even when mutualism is individually costly. The model is applied to obligate and facultative mutualisms, arguing that metacommunity structure and weight-of-numbers dispersal can allow mutualists to outcompete cheaters at the community level. The paper does not provide empirical or simulated data on efficiency or payoffs, but suggests that mutualistic communities are more productive and can proliferate under MLS1 dynamics.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is primarily theoretical and does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments with or without punishment. However, it is relevant in that it discusses how group structure, dispersal, and the presence of cheaters affect the stability of cooperation (mutualism) in multispecies settings. The mechanisms discussed (e.g., metacommunity dispersal, weight-of-numbers advantage) are analogous to factors that can affect efficiency in PGGs, but the paper does not quantify these effects or relate them to specific game design dimensions. It does not analyze punishment as a discrete, costly action, but rather as community-level selection against cheaters. Thus, its value for the prediction task is mainly conceptual and mechanistic, not quantitative.

- source: Skreli, E., Xhoxhi, O., Imami, D., & Rama, K. (2024). What explains collective action: The impact of social capital, incentive structures and economic benefits. *Journal of International Development*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that higher tolerance in reciprocity disposition, perceived positive leadership, and perceived economic benefits from cooperation are all associated with greater willingness to cooperate among Albanian farmers. The effect of reciprocity is stronger in the presence of leadership, though the interaction is not statistically significant in the main regression. Buyer power over price/payments is weakly positively associated with willingness to cooperate, while buyer power over quality is negatively associated. All outcomes are based on self-reported attitudes and perceptions, not on observed behavior or payoffs. Punishment is discussed as a theoretical solution to second-order collective action problems, but is not empirically measured or tested.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not use a game-theoretic or experimental design, nor does it report efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. The findings may be contextually informative for understanding factors that correlate with willingness to cooperate in real-world collective action, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency changes due to punishment or other game design dimensions. The discussion of punishment is theoretical and not empirically grounded in this study.

- source: Xu, L. Y., Wang, B. Y., & Guo, W. (2022). The Effect of Task Difficulty and Self-Contribution on Fairness Consideration: An Event-Related Potential Study. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that self-contribution strongly affects fairness-related decisions: participants are much less likely to accept unfair offers when they have contributed more to the joint task, compared to when their partner contributed more or both contributed equally. Task difficulty (hard vs. easy) does not significantly affect acceptance rates or reaction times, but does modulate neurophysiological responses (FRN amplitude is more negative for unfair offers in hard tasks). The main behavioral outcome is the acceptance rate of unfair offers, interpreted as a form of costly punishment, but the paper does not analyze group efficiency or total payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides indirect evidence about punishment-like behavior (rejection of unfair offers) in a two-player ultimatum game, not a public goods game. The findings suggest that willingness to punish unfairness (by rejecting offers) increases with self-contribution, but the effect on group efficiency or total payoff is not analyzed. The design does not include a baseline without punishment, nor does it report efficiency outcomes, so it cannot directly inform predictions about the effect of enabling punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. However, it does suggest that the salience of individual contribution can increase punitive responses to unfairness, which may be relevant for understanding when punishment is more likely to be used in related games.

- source: Li, O., Shi, Y., & Li, K. (2025). Red, rather than blue can promote fairness in decision-making. *Humanities & Social Sciences Communications*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: The study finds that the color red (vs. blue) increases (vs. decreases) the amount offered by proposers in the ultimatum game, and this effect is mediated by perceived competitiveness. In the impunity game, where responders cannot punish proposers by rejecting offers, color has no effect on offers. The results suggest that contextual cues like color can influence fair behavior in allocation games, but only when strategic motives (i.e., the threat of rejection/punishment) are present. The study does not report efficiency, group payoff, or total earnings, and does not use a public goods game or direct punishment mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or payoff outcomes in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers indirect evidence that contextual cues (like color) can affect proposer behavior in allocation games when strategic punishment is possible (as in the UG), but not when it is absent (as in the IG). The findings suggest that the presence or absence of punishment mechanisms (or their analogs) can moderate the effect of contextual factors on fair behavior, but the lack of efficiency or group payoff data limits its direct utility for the prediction task.

In all other cases, acceptance is neutral. The study does not measure or report any efficiency, payoff, or group welfare outcomes, nor does it manipulate or analyze game-theoretic parameters such as MPCR, punishment cost, or group size.

Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency or payoff effects of punishment in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hertz, U. (2024). A cognitive approach to learning, monitoring, and shifting social norms. *Current Opinion in Psychology*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that the cognitive demands of detecting and monitoring social norms vary by norm type: categorical norms are easiest to detect and punish, scale-sensitive norms require signal detection and are more ambiguous, and history-sensitive norms require tracking individual behavior over time. These differences affect the stability and flexibility of norms, the ease of norm change, and the likelihood of punishment. The paper references experimental paradigms such as the common-goods game and discusses how punishment likelihood increases with deviation from group norms, but does not report on efficiency or payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides conceptual and mechanistic insights into how punishment and norm enforcement may operate differently depending on the cognitive demands of the norm type. It suggests that punishment is more likely and more effective for categorical norms, and that scale-sensitive and history-sensitive norms are more context-dependent and malleable. However, it does not provide empirical data or quantitative findings on efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments. Thus, it is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions, but may inform hypotheses about when punishment is likely to be more or less effective based on the type of norm and cognitive demands.

- source: Bae, M., Shimada, T., & Baek, S. K. (2024). Exact cluster dynamics of indirect reciprocity in complete graphs. *Physical Review E*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count
  findings: The paper provides an exact theoretical analysis of how different indirect reciprocity norms (L4 and L6) shape the long-run social structure in large, fully connected populations. Under L6 (stern judging), the population almost always segregates into two mutually hostile clusters as the group size increases, due to the entropy of balanced configurations. Under L4, a subtle change in the assessment rule leads to a high probability of the 'paradise' state, where everyone assesses everyone else positively, especially as group size increases. The findings are about the stationary distribution of assessment configurations and the emergence of social structure, not about efficiency or payoffs.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it is highly informative about the structural consequences of different indirect reciprocity norms (i.e., whether societies segregate or reach universal cooperation), it does not model or report payoffs, efficiency, or welfare. The results may be relevant for understanding the conditions under which cooperation is structurally stable, but cannot be used to quantitatively predict efficiency outcomes from game design dimensions or control efficiency.

There is no significant association between cooperative attitude and vaccine hesitancy. The 'punishment' type is defined as not cooperating and letting the partner do the task alone after being let down, which is interpreted as a desire to punish free riders. The study does not use a public goods game or measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes.

The study uses a hypothetical scenario to classify cooperative attitudes, including a 'punishment' type, but does not implement or analyze any economic game, nor does it measure payoff, efficiency, or group outcomes. The findings are about associations between self-reported attitudes and real-world risk behaviors, not about the effect of punishment mechanisms in controlled game settings. Thus, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for the prediction task, though it may provide weak, indirect context about the social meaning of punishment attitudes.

- source: Benard, S., Doan, L., Nicholson, D. A., Meanwell, E., Wright, E. L., & Lista, P. (2024). An Eye For an Eye Versus Turning The Other Cheek? The Status Consequences of Revenge and Forgiveness in Intergroup Conflict. *Social Forces*.

  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The main findings are: (1) Forgiveness in intergroup conflict is perceived as more status-worthy than revenge, across national, political, and sports identities. (2) Justifying actions as group-motivated (versus individually motivated) has little effect on perceived status, except for a small positive effect for forgiveness in the sports scenario. (3) Open-ended responses show that forgiving actors are more often described as competent, warm, and high-status, while vengeful actors are more often described as dominant and conflict-seeking. (4) There is some evidence that group-motivated revenge is seen as making the group look bad. (5) The study does not measure or analyze efficiency, group payoff, or any material or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. While it conceptually links intergroup conflict to public goods problems and discusses status as a selective incentive, all outcomes are attitudinal (perceived status, trait attributions) rather than behavioral or payoff-based. There is no manipulation of actual contribution, punishment, or reward in a game-theoretic or economic sense, and no measurement of group payoff, efficiency, or welfare. Thus, the paper is not informative for predicting efficiency outcomes in experimental public goods games with or without punishment.

- source: Isern-Mas, C., & Gomila, A. (2022). A Second-Personal Approach to the Evolution of Morality. *Biological Theory*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper argues that moral motivation evolved in two forms: partial (toward close others) and impartial (toward the group or community at large). It proposes that social norms and sanctions (including third-party punishment and internalized sanctions such as guilt and shame) emerged at the community level and were later internalized, giving rise to the feeling of moral obligation. The authors critique and build on the accounts of Tomasello and Darwall, emphasizing the group-level origins of normativity and the psychological mechanisms that support norm compliance. The paper does not address efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it present experimental or empirical evidence.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its relevance is limited to theoretical insights about the evolutionary function of sanctions and norm internalization in supporting norm compliance and cooperation. It does not address game design dimensions, payoff structures, or efficiency outcomes, and thus should not be weighted heavily in quantitative prediction tasks focused on efficiency or payoff. However, it may provide useful conceptual background on why sanctions (including internalized ones) can support norm compliance.

- source: Bruttel, L., Güth, W., Nithammer, J., & Orland, A. (2022). Inefficient Cooperation Under Stochastic and Strategic Uncertainty. *Journal of Conflict Resolution*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=unclear
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: Allowing voluntary ex-post transfers in a two-player stochastic Ultimatum Game does not significantly increase efficiency (total profit) compared to a baseline without transfers. Proposers claim less and acceptance rates are somewhat higher when transfers are available, but responders do not transfer enough to equalize payoffs, and proposers do not trust responders to do so. The result is that the efficiency-enhancing potential of the transfer mechanism is not realized. The study provides direct evidence that, under high stochastic and strategic uncertainty, voluntary reward-like mechanisms may fail to improve efficiency, in contrast to some public goods game findings.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides a cautionary signal for predicting efficiency gains from enabling voluntary reward or transfer mechanisms in environments with high stochastic and strategic uncertainty, especially in two-player, sequential, asymmetric games. Even when such mechanisms reduce claims and increase acceptance rates, they may not translate into higher efficiency if trust and conditional cooperation are lacking. For prediction tasks, the presence of a voluntary reward/transfer mechanism should not be assumed to increase efficiency in such settings, especially when the control efficiency is already low due to uncertainty and lack of coordination.

- source: Lee, H. W., & Weng, Y. N. (2025). Granular Q-learning adaptation boosts collective welfare in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that more granular, neighbor-specific Q-learning (ID) enables agents to achieve higher collective welfare (average payoff) and maintain cooperation more robustly than aggregated Q-learning (II) in multi-agent Prisoner's Dilemma games. This advantage is most pronounced at intermediate dilemma strengths and in homogeneous networks, but persists in scale-free networks as well. The results are based on extensive simulations varying network structure, dilemma strength, and agent composition. No punishment or reward mechanisms are present; the only difference is the learning architecture. The findings suggest that the granularity of learning adaptation is a key moderator of efficiency in social dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in spatial and scale-free Prisoner's Dilemma environments, the granularity of learning adaptation (neighbor-specific vs. aggregated) is a major determinant of collective efficiency. While not a public goods game or a punishment study, the results are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games where the main design dimension is the learning rule or information granularity. The absence of punishment/reward means the findings are not directly transferable to punishment-enabled PGGs, but the payoff-based outcome and the focus on learning architecture offer useful indirect evidence for how cognitive sophistication or information structure can boost efficiency. The results are most applicable to settings where agent adaptation, rather than explicit sanctions, is the main intervention.

- source: Das Bairagya, J., Mondal, S. S., Chowdhury, D., & Chakraborty, S. (2023). Eco-evolutionary games for harvesting self-renewing common resource: effect of growing harvester population. *Journal of Physics-complexity*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that the realization or prevention of the tragedy of the commons (TOC) in a common-pool resource system depends critically on the relationship between the resource's intrinsic growth rate and the harvesting rates of the population, as well as on the incentive structure encoded in the payoff matrix. When the resource's growth rate is between the low and high harvesting rates, both collapse and partial/complete prevention of TOC are possible, depending on initial conditions and parameter values. Finite population size (carrying capacity) can introduce bistability and limit cycles, making the system's fate sensitive to initial conditions. The model does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, but suggests that such extensions could be studied in future work. The main outcome relevant to efficiency is the long-run resource state, which serves as a proxy for group welfare or efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides theoretical insights into how resource dynamics, harvesting strategies, and population size interact to determine group efficiency in common-pool resource settings. While it does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, it offers a detailed analysis of how efficiency (resource state) depends on game parameters such as harvesting rates, resource growth, and population carrying capacity. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency outcomes in the absence of punishment or reward, and for highlighting the importance of ecological feedbacks and population size. It does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment on efficiency, but it does suggest that efficiency is highly sensitive to the incentive structure and ecological parameters. The discussion of future extensions to include policing or punishment indicates that the model could be adapted for such analyses.

- source: Borzino, N., Fatas, E., & Peterle, E. (2023). In transparency we trust an experimental study of reputation, transparency, and signaling. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that providing ex-post transparency about receiver actions (Transparency treatment) significantly increases trust (the amount sent by senders) compared to both Baseline and Reputation (ex-ante information) treatments. However, neither information treatment increases trustworthiness (the amount returned by receivers) on average. The effect of the non-binding recommendation is positive but declines with higher suggested values and over time. The study does not include punishment or sanction mechanisms, and does not directly report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games, as it does not include a punishment or sanction mechanism, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as a main outcome. Its findings are only weakly relevant for the prediction task, as it focuses on how information and recommendations affect trust and trustworthiness in a networked trust game. The design and outcomes are adjacent to public goods games, but not directly transferable for the purpose of predicting efficiency changes due to punishment.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A

  findings: The studies show that people higher in dispositional compassion express less satisfaction with both advantageous and disadvantageous monetary allocations relative to others, indicating a preference for equality. These effects are robust to controls for prosociality, positive affect, and other personality traits. Experimental inductions of state compassion or pride do not reliably shift these social preferences. The tasks are abstract, one-shot, and hypothetical, with no real economic consequences or group interaction. There is no analysis of efficiency, group payoff, or the effects of punishment or reward mechanisms.

The design is adjacent to economic games but lacks key features: there is no group interaction, no repeated rounds, no punishment or reward, and no real payoffs. The findings may inform how dispositional compassion relates to preferences for equality in allocation, but do not inform the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in PGG-like environments. Thus, its value for the downstream prediction task is very limited.

- source: Yang, X. H., Huang, H. Y., Zhang, Y. C., Wang, J. S., Guan, J. H., & Zhou, S. G. (2023). Short Memory-Based Human Strategy Modeling in Social Dilemmas. *Mathematics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The study finds that individual accumulated payoff is the most important predictor of cooperation/defection decisions, more so than the outcome of the previous round. Behavioral clustering improves the predictability of individual moves, with F1-scores for predicting cooperation/defection reaching up to 95% in some clusters. The diversity of human strategies is substantial, but clustering allows for more accurate modeling. The experiment does not include explicit punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report group-level efficiency or payoff outcomes. The findings are most relevant for understanding individual decision-making and the role of payoff history, rather than for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides little direct support for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. The design is a repeated two-player Prisoner's Dilemma with optional partner selection (refusal to play), which is only weakly analogous to punishment. The main insight is that accumulated payoff is a strong driver of individual cooperation, but this is not directly transferable to the prediction of punishment effects on efficiency. The paper may be useful for understanding the importance of payoff history in individual decision-making, but not for the core prediction task.

- source: Rocha, A. C., & Monteiro, L. H. A. (2023). On the spread of charitable behavior in a social network: a model based on game theory. *Networks and Heterogeneous Media*.
