  findings: The model shows that the average percentage of donors in the population depends on the proportion of intrinsic (I) and extrinsic (E) individuals and the probability that I-individuals donate. When the probability of intrinsic donation is low, increasing the proportion of extrinsic (reputation-motivated) donors can increase the overall donor rate. The payoff parameter (psychological satisfaction from donating) has little effect on the donor rate. The model does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or surplus, and does not include explicit punishment or sanction mechanisms.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It does not model or manipulate punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main relevance is in showing how different motivations for prosocial behavior (intrinsic vs. extrinsic) can affect the prevalence of donation, but not the efficiency or payoff consequences. It provides little to no guidance for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Burridge, J., & Gnacik, M. (2022). Public efforts to reduce disease transmission implied from a spatial game. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=weak | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper finds that infection-reducing (cooperative) behavior, inferred from spatial disease data, increases in response to government-imposed lockdowns and is positively correlated with wealth. The tiered restriction system had limited impact except at the highest levels. Delays in public perception of risk can destabilize behavioral and disease dynamics, leading to oscillations. The model accurately reproduces observed infection rates and spatial patterns, but does not analyze efficiency or payoff outcomes in the sense of public goods games with monetary payoffs or explicit punishment.
  prediction_guidance: This paper is not directly informative for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games. While it uses a game-theoretic framework analogous to public goods dilemmas, it does not include or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main value is in illustrating how behavioral responses (cooperation) can be inferred from aggregate outcomes (disease spread) and how these responses are affected by policy and demographics. It provides little direct evidence for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment and efficiency.

- source: Robbins, B., Karell, D., Siegenthaler, S., & Kamm, A. (2023). Pathways to prosocial leadership: an online experiment on the effects of external subsidies and the relative price of giving. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: External subsidies (private piece-rate transfers) to leaders increase the proportion of community tokens given to recipients, thus increasing group welfare. The effect is robust to the timing of subsidy introduction or removal, with no evidence of crowding out or reduced giving after subsidy removal. The relative price of giving (multiplier) does not significantly affect giving. Norms about giving are not affected by either subsidies or the multiplier. The design does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and the game is a modified dictator game rather than a public goods game, but the main outcome is directly related to group welfare/efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides direct evidence that external subsidies to leaders (akin to positive incentives or rewards) can increase group welfare in resource allocation settings, even when leaders have full discretion. However, it does not address punishment or sanctioning, so it does not inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency. The design is adjacent to public goods games, as it involves redistribution rather than voluntary contribution to a public pool. The findings suggest that positive incentives can increase efficiency, but cannot be directly transferred to punishment effects. The absence of punishment, the dictator game structure, and the focus on leader-driven redistribution limit its direct applicability to PGG-like punishment predictions, but the evidence is informative for the effect of positive incentives on efficiency.

- source: Camera, G., Kim, J., & Arjona, D. R. (2025). Choice flexibility and long-run cooperation. *Economic Theory*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment shows that in indefinitely repeated helping games with groups of strangers, expanding the action set to allow partial cooperation (rather than only all-or-nothing) increases both cooperation and realized efficiency, but only when the payoff structure makes partial cooperation sufficiently rewarding. In the control (Baseline, binary choice), efficiency among strangers is much lower than in fixed pairs. Introducing flexible choices (Linear and Low treatments) raises efficiency among strangers by up to 50%, closing much of the gap with fixed pairs. The effect is not present in the High treatment (where partial cooperation is less rewarding) or in fixed pairs (where efficiency is already high). The mechanism appears to be that flexibility allows for trust-building and coordination on cooperation norms among strangers, shifting the distribution of player types toward more cooperative strategies. No punishment or sanctioning is present or studied.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in indefinitely repeated helping games without punishment, increasing the flexibility of contribution choices (moving from all-or-nothing to continuous/partial) can substantially increase efficiency among strangers, especially when partial cooperation is sufficiently rewarded. The effect is not observed in small, fixed pairs where efficiency is already high. The findings are directly relevant for predicting efficiency in games that manipulate the all-or-nothing dimension, the payoff structure for partial cooperation, and group size. However, the paper does not study punishment or sanctions, so it does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency. It is most useful for understanding baseline efficiency in control games (without punishment) as a function of action set flexibility and payoff structure.

- source: Garbus, J., & Pollack, J. (2024). Emergent Resource Exchange and Tolerated Theft Behavior Using Multiagent Reinforcement Learning. *Artificial Life*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, reward_exists, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The paper demonstrates that agents in a foraging environment with periodic forced congregation (via a campfire and night penalty) can learn to exchange resources, leading to higher cumulative group reward (efficiency) than agents in environments without such structure. The emergence of cooperation is sensitive to environmental parameters: with a strong night penalty, agents reliably learn to trade and achieve higher rewards; with a weak or absent penalty, agents do not reliably trade and achieve lower rewards. No explicit punishment or sanctioning is present; instead, agents develop indirect defenses against defection (e.g., exchange protocols that prevent being cheated). The study provides evidence that environmental design, rather than explicit punishment, can be sufficient for the emergence of efficient cooperation in multiagent systems.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is present or manipulated. However, it is informative for prediction tasks in that it shows efficient cooperation and high group payoff can emerge in the absence of punishment, given appropriate environmental structure (e.g., forced repeated interaction, resource interdependence, and congregation). For prediction, this suggests that the presence or absence of punishment is not the only moderator of efficiency: environmental design can be equally or more important. The paper does not inform the marginal effect of enabling punishment relative to a control, so it should not be used to estimate the effect of punishment per se.

- source: Johnson, T., & Obradovich, N. (2025). Testing for completions that simulate altruism in early language models. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, default_contrib, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study demonstrates that certain LLMs (notably text-davinci-003 and GPT-4) generate completions that simulate both payoff-maximizing behavior in non-social tasks and altruistic sharing in dictator game scenarios. The simulated sharing rates for text-davinci-003 are close to those observed in human dictator game experiments, while less advanced models do not consistently maximize payoffs or share resources. The findings are robust to prompt variations, model parameters, and resource framing. No punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game (PGG) structure is present; the focus is on one-shot dictator games and non-social maximization tasks.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It does, however, offer high-quality evidence on LLMs' simulated behavior in dictator games and non-social maximization tasks, including payoff-based outcomes. Since there is no punishment or sanctioning, and the game is not a PGG or close variant, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency in PGG-like environments. It may be useful for understanding LLMs' simulated social preferences or for comparison with human dictator game data, but not for the downstream prediction task focused on punishment effects.

- source: Schories, F. E. (2022). The Influence of Indirect Democracy and Leadership Choice on Cooperation. *Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=more_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: The experiment demonstrates that the way an institution is implemented (democratically by an elected leader vs. exogenously or by a random dictator) has a substantial effect on cooperation and group efficiency. When the group leader is elected and chooses to implement a more cooperative payoff structure, both cooperation rates and average earnings are significantly higher than in otherwise identical conditions where the change is imposed exogenously or by a randomly assigned leader. The democracy premium is large and statistically significant in the representative democracy (elected leader) treatment, but not in the random dictator treatment. The effect is especially pronounced among subjects who initially did not prefer the institutional change. The paper provides direct evidence that procedural legitimacy (democratic election) increases efficiency in social dilemma settings, even when controlling for selection and information effects.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that, in social dilemma environments structurally similar to the prisoners' dilemma (and by extension, public goods games), the introduction of a more cooperative institution (e.g., a coordination game) by a democratically elected leader leads to higher efficiency (group payoff) than if the same institution is imposed exogenously or by a randomly assigned leader. The findings suggest that, when predicting the effect of institutional changes on efficiency, the method of implementation (democratic legitimacy) is a critical moderator. However, the results do not inform about the effects of punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, as these are not present in the design. The evidence is most relevant for settings where institutional change is possible and the legitimacy of the process is a variable of interest.

- source: Zhu, E. Q., Chen, J., Liu, S. Q., Zhang, X. K., & Liu, C. J. (2025). The influence of heterogeneous investment on the evolution of trust in complex networks. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: The study shows that in a networked trust game, allowing investors to adjust their investment amounts based on local trust levels (heterogeneous investment) significantly increases total group assets and the prevalence of trust, compared to homogeneous investment. As the heterogeneity parameter alpha increases, the system transitions from collapse (no investors, zero assets) to stable coexistence of investors and trustworthy trustees, with high group efficiency. The effect is robust to initial conditions and persists even under strong social dilemmas. However, the model does not include punishment, reward, or sanctioning mechanisms, so it does not directly inform the effect of punishment on efficiency.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism. Its findings are relevant for understanding how heterogeneity in investment (contribution) can promote efficiency and trust in trust-game-like settings, but cannot be used to predict the effect of enabling punishment. For the downstream prediction task, this paper is not directly informative for the treatment effect of punishment, but may provide indirect context for the role of contribution heterogeneity.

- source: Gros, C. (2022). Collective strategy condensation towards class-separated societies. *European Physical Journal B*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that adding an envy-based psychological component to agents' utility functions in a competitive resource allocation game leads to a phase transition: for low envy, agents play diverse pure and mixed strategies, but for high envy, a large fraction of agents adopt the same mixed strategy, forming a lower class. This transition is accompanied by a substantial drop in mean income (efficiency), indicating that envy is detrimental to group welfare. The results are robust to variations in model details and to heterogeneity in envy levels. The paper also estimates, using ultimatum game data, that real-world levels of envy are likely high enough to induce this class-stratified, low-efficiency regime.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong theoretical evidence that adding a psychological penalty for relative disadvantage (envy) to a competitive resource allocation game reduces group efficiency/welfare, especially beyond a critical threshold. While the game is not a public goods game or a punishment game, the findings are relevant for predicting efficiency in environments where psychological or social comparison motives are present. However, the paper does not study punishment, sanctions, or public goods provision directly, so its predictive value is limited to adjacent cases where social comparison or envy-like terms are present in the utility function. The main actionable insight is that increasing the weight of social comparison (envy) reduces efficiency, even when the underlying game structure is otherwise unchanged.

- source: Drouvelis, M., Gerson, J., Powdthavee, N., & Riyanto, Y. E. (2023). Large losses from little lies: Strategic gender misrepresentation and cooperation. *Plos One*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that when participants are randomly allowed to misrepresent their gender to their partner in a one-shot Golden Balls game, the average defection (steal) rate increases by about 10-12 percentage points compared to control conditions (blind or true gender revealed). Those who actually choose to misrepresent are about 32 percentage points more likely to defect. The possibility of misrepresentation also increases defection among those who do not misrepresent, due to increased distrust. The result is a substantial reduction in overall cooperation and group welfare, as measured by the proportion of participants receiving the maximum possible payoff (£10 each for mutual cooperation) and the average payment per participant. The effect is robust across lab and online samples, and is not explained by differences in personality traits or risk/trust attitudes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides strong evidence that introducing opportunities for strategic misrepresentation of identity (here, gender) in a one-shot social dilemma with communication reduces cooperation and group welfare, even in the absence of explicit punishment or reward mechanisms. While not a public goods game, the Golden Balls game is a close variant of the prisoner's dilemma, and the findings are likely to generalize to other one-shot or anonymous social dilemmas with communication. The results suggest that any design feature that increases uncertainty about partner identity or intentions (such as allowing misrepresentation) can reduce efficiency. For the prediction task, this paper supports the expectation that enabling deception or increasing uncertainty will lower efficiency, even if punishment is not present. However, it does not provide direct evidence on the effect of punishment, as no punishment or sanctioning mechanism is included in the design.

- source: Bellucci, G. (2022). A Model of Trust. *Games*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr
  findings: The paper formalizes trust as a trade-off between vulnerability (potential loss) and positive expectations of a partner's behavior, using a computational model applied to the investment game. Simulations show that trustful investors (high trust attitude) invest more and are more likely to recover trust after breaches, but are also more vulnerable to exploitation. Loss aversion reduces investment and makes trust repair harder, while positive expectations and evidence of partner benevolence or competence increase investment. The model predicts that both trust attitude and learning about the partner's behavior jointly determine investment and, by extension, payoffs. The model is extended to consider both benevolence and competence of the trustee, showing that both traits affect investment and expected payoffs. However, the paper does not address punishment, sanctions, or public goods games, and does not report empirical efficiency outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their variants. It is a theoretical modeling paper focused on the investment game (trust game), which is adjacent but not equivalent to the public goods game. The model and its simulations may inform understanding of how psychological factors (trust, expectations, loss aversion) affect investment and payoffs in dyadic trust settings, but do not address punishment, sanctions, or group-level efficiency in multi-player public goods contexts. Therefore, this paper should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency changes due to punishment in public goods games.

- source: Schippers, A. L., & Soetevent, A. R. (2024). Sharing with minimal regulation? Evidence from neighborhood book exchange. *European Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, mpcr
  findings: Neighborhood book exchanges operate with minimal regulation and no formal punishment or sanctioning. Despite this, users maintain high levels of reciprocal exchange, returning nearly as many books as they take, and of similar value. Free riding exists but is limited. Owners' restocking compensates for the small deficit. Social norms and strong cooperative preferences among participants are likely responsible for this outcome. The system is stable over time, and a supply shock of high-quality books does not lead to persistent changes in usage or quality. The findings suggest that, in the absence of punishment, high efficiency (measured as reciprocal exchange) can be sustained in some real-world sharing environments, likely due to strong norms and self-selection.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides evidence that, in a real-world, unregulated sharing environment with no punishment or sanctioning, efficiency (measured as reciprocal exchange) can remain high if strong social norms and cooperative preferences are present. However, the setting is only adjacent to public goods games: there is no explicit group payoff, no monetary contributions, and no formal punishment. The findings suggest that the absence of punishment does not necessarily lead to collapse in efficiency, but this may depend on context-specific factors like self-selection and social norms. For prediction tasks, this paper suggests that in environments lacking punishment, efficiency may be higher than standard PGG models predict if strong informal norms exist, but the transferability to standard PGGs is limited.

- source: Lu, W., & Liang, S. (2025). The role of strategy-affected emotions in the evolution of cooperation. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=close
  outcomes: primary=mixed | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing
  findings: The paper finds that in a spatial three-strategy prisoner's dilemma model with emotion dynamics, increasing the intensity of friendliness (the degree to which strategies affect emotions) leads to higher levels of cooperation and higher average payoffs in the population. The effect is robust across different parameterizations of the game (including donor-recipient and boundary PD variants). Memory length moderates the effect: with low friendliness, longer memory increases cooperation; with high friendliness, longer memory decreases cooperation. The model demonstrates that stable, high-payoff cooperation can emerge through emotion-strategy feedbacks alone, without any punishment or reward mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or their close variants. It is relevant as a theoretical demonstration that high cooperation and group payoff can be achieved through emotion-based mechanisms, without punishment or reward. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects, this paper suggests that alternative mechanisms (like friendliness) can substitute for punishment in promoting efficiency, but it does not inform how punishment would change efficiency relative to a control. The game design dimensions relevant to punishment (punishment_cost, punishment_tech, etc.) are not present or manipulated. Thus, this paper should not be used to predict the effect of punishment on efficiency, but may be useful for understanding baseline efficiency in the absence of punishment or reward.

- source: Lyu, D., Liu, H. X., Wang, L., & Wang, X. F. (2024). Evolution of cooperation in a mixed cooperative-competitive structured population. *Physica A-statistical Mechanics and Its Applications*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, reward_exists, reward_cost
  findings: The study finds that introducing inter-group competition (external rewards for competing with other groups) increases the level of cooperation within groups, especially as the intensity (density) of inter-group connections increases. Strategic structuring of inter-group connections (e.g., connecting high-degree nodes in one group to low-degree nodes in another) can further enhance cooperation in the favored group. These effects are robust to different network generation models and strategy updating rules. However, the paper does not analyze efficiency, group payoff, or welfare as outcomes, and does not include any punishment or sanctioning mechanism.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not relevant for predicting the efficiency impact of punishment, as it studies a different mechanism (external rewards via inter-group competition) and reports only cooperation rates, not efficiency or payoff. The game structure is adjacent to PGGs (donation game within groups), but the absence of punishment and the focus on non-payoff outcomes limit its value for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Zou, M., Feng, J. Q., Qin, N., Diao, J. D., Yang, Y., Liao, J. J., Lin, J. B., & Mo, L. (2022). The Effect of the Quantity and Distribution of Teammates' Tendency Toward Self-Interest and Altruism on Individual Decision-Making. *Frontiers in Psychology*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The paper finds that participants' willingness to choose the collective solution (akin to cooperation in a public goods game) increases with the observed altruism of teammates and decreases with observed self-interest. There is a ceiling/floor effect: once teammates' altruism/self-interest passes a threshold, further increases have little effect. When a fixed amount of altruism is distributed across more teammates, trust and cooperation increase (decentralized effect); when a fixed amount of self-interest is distributed across more teammates, trust and cooperation decrease (convergent effect). The study does not include punishment or reward mechanisms, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or variants. However, it provides informative behavioral evidence about how the perceived distribution of cooperation and defection among group members affects individuals' willingness to cooperate. The absence of punishment or reward means it cannot inform the effect of those mechanisms. The design is adjacent to standard PGGs, as it is a step-level game with both individual and collective solutions, but the main outcomes are behavioral choices, not payoffs or efficiency. For prediction tasks focused on punishment effects or efficiency, this paper is only indirectly relevant.

- source: Mitani, Y. (2022). Is a PD game still a dilemma for Japanese rural villagers? A field and laboratory comparison of the impact of social group membership on cooperation. *Japanese Economic Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that social group membership (in-community vs. out-community) has a strong positive effect on cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma game, both in the field (with rural villagers) and in the lab (with students). Villagers achieve near-universal cooperation with in-community matching, and still high cooperation with out-community matching. Students cooperate much less, but the group identity effect is still present. The paper does not include any punishment or reward treatments, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff as an outcome. The findings are relevant for understanding the effect of social context and group identity on cooperation, but not for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods or similar games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment or sanctions on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It is not directly informative for predicting treatment efficiency from game design dimensions plus control efficiency, as it does not manipulate or analyze punishment, nor does it report efficiency or group payoff outcomes. Its main contribution is to show that social group identity can strongly increase cooperation rates in a one-shot prisoner's dilemma, especially among rural villagers. It may be useful for understanding baseline cooperation rates in the absence of punishment, but not for predicting the effect of adding punishment.

- source: Quan, J., Feng, Y. X., Nie, J. C., & Wang, X. J. (2025). Catalyst strategy for the evolution of cooperation in indirect reciprocity. *Applied Mathematics and Computation*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, mpcr, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that certain conditional cooperation strategies, especially those tolerant of cooperative behavior and harsh toward defection (the 'catalyst strategy'), can promote high levels of cooperation in indirect reciprocity models based on second-order action labels. The presence of noise (action or label errors) reduces cooperation rates but does not qualitatively change the system's state. Larger populations enhance the effectiveness of the catalyst strategy, while smaller populations and higher noise diminish it. However, the model does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms, and efficiency or group payoff is not a primary outcome.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not include punishment or sanctioning mechanisms. Its findings on cooperation rates in indirect reciprocity models with second-order action labels may be of limited indirect relevance for understanding how certain strategy structures (e.g., tolerance and harshness) affect cooperation, but it does not inform predictions about punishment-enabled efficiency. The absence of payoff/efficiency as a central outcome and the lack of a punishment mechanism mean this paper should not be weighted heavily for the downstream prediction task.

- source: Zhou, M., Yang, Y. L., & Xiang, S. W. (2025). Effect of community learning mechanism on cooperation in conflict societies. *Chaos Solitons & Fractals*.
  type: theory | empirical=N/A | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=adjacent | punishment=none | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that introducing a community learning mechanism—where individuals preferentially imitate the most successful community members—substantially increases the proportion of cooperators in a spatial Prisoner's Dilemma simulation. The effect is strongest when the minimum community size is small and when individuals are more likely to join large, distant communities (low distance cost). The emergence of large, cohesive communities is associated with high cooperation rates. However, the paper does not analyze or report group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare, and there are no punishment or reward mechanisms in the model.

  findings: The paper finds that the evolution and maintenance of cooperation in multi-community systems are primarily determined by the strength of the intra-community dilemma and the intensity of selection.   findings: The paper finds that introducing multiple interaction patterns (conditional and stochastic) based on opponent's ability can significantly increase the density of cooperators in a spatial prisoner's dilemma. The results are robust across weak and true prisoner's dilemma parameterizations. However, the paper does not study punishment, reward, or efficiency/welfare outcomes.

- source: Molho, C., Soraperra, I., Schulz, J. F., & Shalvi, S. (2025). Guilt drives prosociality across 20 countries. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment

  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Across 20 countries, participants were more likely to make prosocial choices in dictator games when they had full information about the consequences of their actions (guilt condition) compared to when they could remain ignorant. Making choices observable to others (shame condition) had negligible or inconsistent effects on prosociality. Individual-level guilt-proneness predicted greater responsiveness to information, but country-level differences were not robust moderators. The study does not include punishment, sanctions, or public goods game structures, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.

  findings: The experiment finds that increasing noise in the information network makes it less likely for groups to reach consensus and increases the time required to do so. The study does not analyze or report any efficiency, welfare, or group payoff outcomes.

  findings: The experiment finds that parochial altruism (allocation to the between-group account, which benefits the in-group and harms the out-group) is higher when both in-group and out-group distances are high. Low distance to the out-group reduces both altruism and parochial altruism. The main behavioral outcomes are the propensity and amount allocated to within-group and between-group accounts. There is no analysis of group efficiency, total payoff, or welfare. The game does not include punishment or reward mechanisms. The findings are robust to alternative distance measures and controls for group composition.

The game is an intergroup conflict variant (IPD-MD), not a standard PGG, and the main manipulations concern group distance, not institutional design features like punishment or reward. - source: Sequeira, M. E. (2023). Fairness, partner choice, and punishment: An ethnographic study of cooperative behavior among children in Helsinki, Finland. *Ethos*.

  findings: The paper finds that among Helsinki-based children, fairness is a central value, and children resolve conflicts or perceived unfairness primarily through partner choice strategies (avoiding or disengaging from unfair partners) rather than through punishment. Both children and adults show a strong aversion to punishment, which is rare in both peer and adult-child interactions. Autonomy and independence are emphasized in socialization, and norm enforcement is achieved through persuasion and shaming rather than coercion or punitive measures. The study does not use experimental games or report on efficiency or payoff outcomes, so it does not provide evidence on the effect of punishment on group efficiency.

However, it does provide strong qualitative evidence that in this context, punishment is rare and not socially valued as a means of sustaining cooperation. If one were to extrapolate, it suggests that in environments with strong norms of autonomy and aversion to punishment, introducing punishment mechanisms may not increase cooperation or efficiency, and may even be resisted or ignored. However, the lack of payoff or efficiency data means this paper cannot be used for quantitative prediction of efficiency outcomes.

- source: Radkani, S., Tenenbaum, J. B., & Saxe, R. (2025). What people learn from punishment: A cognitive model. *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: punishment_cost, show_punishment_id
  findings: The paper finds that observers' inferences about the wrongness of an act and the motives of an authority after observing punishment are interdependent and strongly shaped by prior beliefs about norms and authority. Punishment can communicate norm violation and enhance or damage the authority's reputation, depending on context. The authors' Bayesian inverse planning model quantitatively predicts these belief updates and outperforms alternative models. The study also shows that repeated observation of punishment can either reduce or sustain polarization in beliefs, depending on initial priors. However, all outcomes are belief-based; the study does not measure or report efficiency, group payoff, or actual behavioral cooperation.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about belief updating and interpretation of punishment, not about actual contributions, cooperation, or payoffs. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about treatment efficiency from game design dimensions or control efficiency. It may be useful for understanding how punishment is perceived or how norm communication and legitimacy are inferred, but not for payoff-based outcome prediction.

  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=field_experiment

  dimensions: show_punishment_id
  findings: Parents are significantly more likely to directly punish a norm violator (verbal confrontation) and to help a stranger in need when their child is present, compared to when they are alone. There is no significant increase in indirect punishment (withholding help) in the presence of a child. The findings support the idea that parents use opportunities to teach social norms to their children by modeling both norm enforcement and prosocial behavior. The study does not address efficiency, group payoff, or any payoff-based outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not inform the prediction of efficiency or payoff outcomes in public-goods-game-like environments, as it does not use a PGG or any group payoff structure, nor does it report on efficiency or related outcomes. Its relevance is limited to understanding behavioral mechanisms of norm enforcement and punishment in naturalistic settings, not to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in economic games.

- source: Alvarez-Benjumea, A., & Valentim, V. (2024). The Enforcement of Political Norms. *British Journal of Political Science*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that individuals in Spain perceive public displays of support for the radical-right party Vox as less socially and morally appropriate and more harmful than support for other parties. Respondents are more likely to view sanctioning (especially indirect sanctions like gossip and avoidance) as appropriate and report greater willingness to engage in such sanctions against radical-right supporters. The willingness to sanction is not strongly predicted by political ideology or interest, but is correlated with age, gender, education, local support for Vox, and emotional reactions (anger, disgust). The study does not measure or discuss any payoff-based or efficiency outcomes, nor does it use a public goods game or similar economic game structure.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. While it offers detailed insights into the social and psychological mechanisms of norm enforcement and sanctioning in a political context, it does not use a game-theoretic or economic game design, nor does it report any group payoff, efficiency, or welfare outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about the efficiency effects of punishment in PGG or similar environments.

- source: Stanley, M. L., Huang, S. Y., Marsh, E. J., & Kay, A. C. (2023). The Role of Structure-Seeking in Moral Punishment. *Social Justice Research*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=exact | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that (1) individuals with a higher need for structure are more likely to endorse punishment for moral rule violations, (2) experimentally inducing a threat to structure increases punitive judgments, and (3) recalling unpunished wrongdoing (vs. punished wrongdoing) reduces perceived structure in the social world. All outcomes are attitudinal or perceptual; there are no behavioral or payoff-based measures. The studies use hypothetical or autobiographical scenarios about moral rule violations, not economic games or public goods dilemmas.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use economic games, does not manipulate or report on any game design dimensions, and does not measure efficiency, group payoff, or related outcomes. Its findings are about psychological motivations for punishment and the perceived orderliness of the world, not about the consequences of punishment for cooperation, efficiency, or welfare. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about efficiency in PGG or similar environments.

- source: Castagnetti, A., Proto, E., & Sofianos, A. (2023). Anger impairs strategic behavior: A Beauty-Contest based analysis. *Journal of Economic Behavior & Organization*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=exact
  outcomes: primary=efficiency_or_payoff | overall_effect=less_positive
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, show_n_rounds, show_other_summaries
  findings: Inducing anger in participants leads to significantly lower payoffs and worse strategic performance in a 10-round, 3-player beauty contest game. Angry participants deviate further from the best response and are more likely to use non-strategic (level-0) reasoning. The effect is robust across group compositions and persists after controlling for prior play. Inducing sadness does not have a significant effect on payoffs or strategic reasoning. The results suggest that anger specifically, rather than negative emotions in general, impairs strategic thinking and reduces efficiency in strategic games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. However, it is informative for the prediction task in that it demonstrates how incidental emotions (specifically anger) can reduce efficiency in strategic games by impairing cognitive reasoning. The findings suggest that if punishment mechanisms in public goods games induce anger, this could potentially reduce efficiency, not by changing social preferences, but by impairing strategic reasoning. However, since no punishment, sanctioning, or public goods game is studied directly, the transfer value is limited and indirect.

- source: Gächter, S., Molleman, L., & Nosenzo, D. (2025). Why people follow rules. *Nature Human Behaviour*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The study finds that 55-70% of participants follow an arbitrary costly rule even when acting anonymously and alone, and when rule-breaking has no consequences for others. Social expectations and intrinsic respect for rules explain most rule-following. Adding externalities (harm to a charity) or punishment (with varying probabilities) increases rule-following rates, but a substantial baseline of rule-following exists without these. Observing peer rule-breaking reduces rule-following, but even with all peers breaking the rule, a significant fraction still comply. The main outcome is behavioral (rule-following), not efficiency or group payoff.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not directly inform predictions about the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar group cooperation settings. While it provides evidence that punishment increases rule-following in a single-player, non-strategic, asocial rule-following task, the outcome is not efficiency or group payoff, and the context is not a PGG or its variant. The findings may be relevant for understanding the motivational basis of compliance and the effect of sanctions on rule-following, but cannot be directly transferred to efficiency predictions in PGG-like environments.

- source: Hakimov, R., & Kajackaite, A. (2024). Breaking bad: Malfunctioning control institutions erode good behavior in a cheating game. *Games and Economic Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_tech, show_n_rounds
  findings: The experiment finds that the mere announcement of a control institution, even if it is not enforced, crowds out intrinsic motivation for honesty and leads to more cheating than a trust-based (no control) environment. Strong control (with actual monitoring and punishment) does not increase overall honesty compared to no control, due to offsetting effects on the extensive and intensive margins of lying. The most detrimental effect on honesty occurs when a strong institution is announced but not enforced. These effects are robust to comparisons with a 'weak' institution (very low probability of control), which does not produce the same negative effect as a malfunctioning institution. The findings are about individual-level honesty and do not address group efficiency or public goods provision.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting efficiency or group payoff in public goods games with or without punishment. However, it offers adjacent evidence that the announcement of unenforced punishment or control can crowd out intrinsic motivation and increase rule-breaking behavior, even more than having no punishment institution. For prediction tasks involving efficiency in PGG-like environments, this suggests that unenforced or malfunctioning punishment institutions may reduce cooperation or efficiency more than the absence of punishment, but this is an indirect inference. The results are not directly transferable to group payoff or efficiency outcomes.

- source: Berger, J. (2024). Social capital is associated with cooperation and indirect norm enforcement in the field: behavioural evidence from Switzerland. *European Sociological Review*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The study finds that neighborhoods with higher social capital exhibit higher rates of cooperation (as measured by the lost-letter technique) and more frequent indirect norm enforcement (as measured by bystanders picking up litter after a staged transgression). Direct enforcement (verbal confrontation) is rare and does not differ significantly between neighborhoods. Residents in high social capital neighborhoods have higher empirical and normative expectations of norm compliance and lower fear of retaliation. However, the study does not use a public goods game or any payoff-based group interaction, and does not report efficiency or group payoff outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence for predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. Its findings are about real-world cooperation and informal norm enforcement, not about payoff, efficiency, or sanctioning in economic games. The norm enforcement studied is indirect (e.g., picking up litter) and not costly punishment as in PGGs. Therefore, while the paper may be contextually relevant for understanding the social foundations of cooperation and informal enforcement, it does not inform quantitative prediction of efficiency changes due to punishment in PGG-like games.

- source: Clark, K. J. (2022). Cultural Traditions and Fitness Interdependence. *Evolutionary Behavioral Sciences*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=adjacent
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: none
  findings: The paper finds that four key cooperative behaviors (adat, biau, bedurok, gawai) in the Iban community are primarily transmitted vertically from ancestors to descendants, supporting the idea that traditions play a central role in shaping cooperation and fitness interdependence. The study uses semistructured interviews to show that most informants learned these behaviors from parents or grandparents and intend to teach them to their own children. The author argues that such traditional transmission may stabilize cooperation and social environments, but does not provide quantitative or experimental evidence on efficiency or payoff outcomes, nor does it analyze punishment in a game-theoretic or experimental context.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public-goods-game-like environments. It does not use a PGG or variant, does not manipulate or measure punishment or sanctions in a controlled setting, and does not report efficiency or payoff outcomes. Its relevance is limited to the broader context of how traditions and vertical transmission may support cooperation and norm compliance, and it mentions that traditions may be enforced by social consequences or punishment, but this is not empirically analyzed. Thus, it should not be used as direct evidence for predicting efficiency effects of punishment in PGG designs.

- source: Almeida, S. (2023). Punishment credibility and cooperation in public good games. *Journal of Behavioral and Experimental Economics*.
  type: empirical | empirical=experimental | experimental=lab_experiment
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=weak
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, chat, all_or_nothing, punishment_cost, punishment_tech
  findings: Children gave significantly more candies to the other child in the Ultimatum Game (where unfair offers can be punished by rejection) than in the Dictator Game (where the recipient has no power), indicating that the threat of punishment increases prosocial allocations. Higher Honesty-Humility (a personality trait) was associated with more generous offers in both games. The study does not report on efficiency, group payoff, or welfare, and the design is not a public goods game or a close variant. The findings are relevant for understanding how the possibility of punishment (retaliation) affects fairness-related behavior in children, but not for predicting efficiency in public goods games.
  prediction_guidance: This paper provides only indirect, adjacent evidence for the prediction task. It shows that the threat of punishment (in the form of possible rejection in the Ultimatum Game) increases prosocial allocations compared to a no-punishment baseline (Dictator Game) in children. However, the design is not a public goods game, does not involve group payoff or efficiency, and does not report payoff-based outcomes. The evidence is relevant for understanding how the possibility of punishment can shift individual allocation behavior, but cannot be directly used to predict efficiency effects of punishment in public goods games or similar environments.

- source: Schläpfer, A. (2025). The social benefits of anti-social punishment. *Evolution and Human Behavior*.
  type: empirical | empirical=observational | experimental=N/A
  relevance: pgg=none | punishment=adjacent | payoff=none
  outcomes: primary=non_payoff_behavior | overall_effect=N/A
  dimensions: player_count, num_rounds, show_n_rounds, show_punishment_id
  findings: The study documents that cultural background predicts the likelihood and speed of retaliation after a foul in professional soccer. Retaliation by the victim reduces the probability of repeated fouls by the perpetrator, but only if the perpetrator is from a culture that values revenge. Formal sanctions (yellow cards) reduce both the likelihood of retaliation and repeated offenses, and are about three times as effective as informal retaliation in deterring repeated fouls. There is evidence of crowding out: when a foul is formally sanctioned, victims are less likely to retaliate. The study does not report or analyze any efficiency, group payoff, or welfare outcomes.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide direct evidence relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency or group payoff in public-goods-game-like environments. Its findings are adjacent: it shows that informal and formal sanctions can reduce repeated transgressions in a real-world setting, and that the effectiveness of informal sanctions depends on cultural context. However, because the setting is not a public goods game or a close variant, and because no payoff or efficiency outcomes are reported, the paper offers only indirect, contextual support for the prediction task. It may inform mechanism discussions about the interaction of formal and informal punishment, and about heterogeneity in punishment effectiveness, but does not provide quantitative or directly transferable evidence for efficiency outcomes.

  findings: The report finds that while the overall gender distribution at CSIC is balanced, significant disparities remain in permanent and senior research positions, with women underrepresented especially at the Research Professor level (26.9%). The glass ceiling index is 1.42 overall, with higher values in some sub-areas. Positive trends include increases in the percentage of women directors, inventors in patent applications, and supervisors of theses. However, progress in closing the gender gap is slow, and disparities persist in access to higher research categories and leadership roles.
  prediction_guidance: This paper does not provide any evidence or findings relevant to predicting the effect of punishment on efficiency in public goods games or similar environments. It does not discuss game design, cooperation, sanctions, or payoff-based outcomes. Therefore, it should not be used to inform predictions about punishment effects in public-goods-game-like settings.
